Enhanced Fired-Clay Bricks Incorporating 
Cigarette Butts  
 
 
A thesis submitted in fulfillment of the requirements for the degree of  
Doctor of Philosophy 
 
 
Halenur Kurmus 
Bachelor of Engineering (Civil and Infrastructure) (Hons)/Bachelor of Business 
(Management), RMIT University, Melbourne, Australia 
 
 
 
School of Engineering  
College of Science, Technology, Engineering and Maths 
RMIT University 
 
June 2021 
 


=== PDF PAGE 2 ===
 i  
 
Declaration  
 
I certify that except where due acknowledgement has been made, the work is that of the author alone; 
the work has not been submitted previously, in whole or in part, to qualify for any other academic 
award; the content of the thesis is the result of work which has been carried out since the official 
commencement date of the approved research program; any editorial work, paid or unpaid, carried out 
by a third party is acknowledged; and, ethics procedures and guidelines have been followed.  I 
acknowledge the support I have received for my research through the provision of an Australian 
Government Research Training Program Scholarship. 
 
 
Halenur Kurmus 
17 June 2021 
 
 
 
  

=== PDF PAGE 3 ===
 ii  
 
Acknowledgments  
First and the most, I would like to sincerely express my deepest gratitude to my senior supervisor Assoc. 
Prof. Abbas Mohajerani for his helpful guidance, continuous motivation, ongoing advice and 
encouragement and positivity  throughout my PhD study. I am also thankful to my associate supervisor 
Dr. David Law for his valuable advice, encouragement, and support. 
I would like to thank my colleagues and friends, particularly  Md Tareq Rahman and Amir Siddiq, for 
their assistance and support througho ut my  candidature. I wish to express my most profound 
appreciation to technical officers Huw James, Stephen Grist, Dr. Bao Thach Nguyen, Eric Xiang Gao,  
Pavel Ryjkov, and Michael Swaney for their technical assistance and support in the laboratories.  
Finally, I offer my deepest gratitude to my dear mother and father, Zuhal Kurmus and Ceyhun Kurmus, 
and my amazing siblings Halime Kurmus and Furkan Kurmus, for their love, patience, and continuous 
support to fulfill my dream to attain a Doctor of Philosophy in Engineering. I am so grateful to have 
you all in my life.  
Halenur Kurmus

 1  
 
Executive Summary 
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the world’s most prevalent source of waste litter. In the year 2016, 5.7 trillion 
cigarettes were consumed worldwide, and about 97% of the cigarette filters were composed of cellulose 
acetate, a modified natural polymer. In Australia, it is estimated that over 20 billion filtered cigarettes 
are consumed each year, of which an estimated 7 billion are littered into the environment. The littering 
of CBs is a growing waste management issue and requires specific planning. When CBs are littered, 
harmful chemicals are leached into the environment, posing major concerns for the global environment 
and the quality of urban and aquatic life. In cigarette smoke, it is possible to classify more than 4,000 
chemical components that are generated during burning or that are distilled from tobacco, of which 69 
are carcinogenic. Cigarettes are made of cellulose acetate filters, which are designed to fully or partially 
retain particulate smoke components. Cellulose acetate filters have poor biodegradability and can take 
up to 18 months to decompose under normal environmental conditions, hence, posing a serious dilemma 
in terms of toxic waste and litter disposal. Based on several years of research, Mohajerani et al. (2016) 
proposed that the CB conundrum can potentially be eliminated by incorporating 1% CBs into 2.5% of 
the world’s total  fired clay brick  (FCB ) production. This research is a continuation and extensive 
expansion of the parent research on recycling CBs in FCBs. The initial stage of the research involved a 
comprehensive laboratory investigation to develop techniques to recycle CB waste in FCBs without 
compromising the physical and mechanical properties. FCB samples were prepared incorporating 0%, 
0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs and were tested for shrinkage, water absorption, initial rate of absorption, 
density, compressive strength, and efflorescence. The results from this investigation met the Australian 
Standards for masonry building blocks. The second part of the research investigated the thermal 
conductivity and energy savings of FCBs incorporating CBs, wher eby the results from the calculation 
and measurement method were compared. For the energy savings study, a novel method was developed 
to monitor the energy consumption of the furnace during the firing process of the FCB samples 
incorporating CBs. An increase in CB content positively resulted in a decrease in density and thermal 
conductivity and an increase in energy savings. X -ray micro -Computed Tomography (micro-CT) 
imaging was utilized to characterize the quantity, distribution, size, and shape of the micro- and macro-
scale pores generated from the combustion of the CBs during the firing process. The 3D models 
demonstrated a homogenous distribution of pores within the brick structure. The third part of the 
investigation involved a comprehensive leachate analysis of heavy metals for used, unused , and 
shredded used CBs, paraffin wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs, and unfired and fired clay bricks 
incorporating CBs. The leachate analysis was conducted according to the Australian Bottle Leaching 

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
 2  
 
Procedure (ABLP) for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2 using the Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass 
Spectrometry (ICP -MS). The leaching behavior of fired bricks incorporating CBs was considerably 
lower than that for the unfired bricks due to the immobilization of heavy metals during the firing 
process. Moreover, it was found the bitumen and wax encapsulation process successfully trapped the 
heavy metals present in the CBs. The leachate results for all samples were found to be below the 
regulatory limits set by the EPA Victoria solid industrial waste guidelines. To investigate the potential 
residual remaining in FCBs incorporating CBs a comprehensive polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon 
(PAH) extraction analysis was conducted in the fourth part of the investigation using the Soxhlet 
extractor and rotary evaporator. The samples were analysed using gas chromatography-mass 
spectrometry (GC–MS). The ΣPAH concentrations for all five FCB –CB mixes were well below the 
EPA Victoria solid waste hazard categorization threshold for industrial waste. Moreover, the samples 
were studied for their carbon content using the carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and sulfur (CHNS) analyzer 
and thermogravimetric analysis (TGA). The results confirm an almost 100% combustion process of 
CBs during the firing process.  The f ollowing part of the research involved the implementation of 
recycling CBs in bricks on an industrial scale. A bacteriological study was conducted , which involved 
the investigation of  the presence of ten common bacteria in two pilot studies , various sterilisation 
methods for CBs were examined, and three processing methods were suggested for the implementation 
of recycling CBs in FCBs on an industrial scale. Contact of naphthalene balls with CBs had a significant 
disinfecting effect on Bacillus spp. The final  part of the research involved investigating the issues 
relative to the strong odor of CBs and the potential release of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) 
during the storage and manufacturing process of CBs in FCBs. The aim was to determine techniques to 
detect and analyze the emission of VOCs generated from CBs, along with investigating methods to 
minimize or eradicate the odors generated from CBs. Charcoal sorbent tubes were used to collect the 
gas components, and gas chromatography -mass spectrometry (GC-M S) was used to identify and 
quantify the VOCs generated. Ultraviolet-C (UVC) light  was implemented to determine its effect on 
odor reduction and mitigation.  The results confirm the pollutant gas production in UV C light treated 
CB samples is significantly less than that of untreated CB samples. This research has discussed the 
world CB pollution issue, potential solutions, and the scope of expansion and further development.  
 

=== PDF PAGE 21 ===
 3  
 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
1.1 Background  
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the world’s most prevalent source of waste litter. The Tobacco Atlas [4] 
reported that during the year 2016, 5.7 trillion cigarettes were consumed worldwide and that about 97% 
of the cigarette filters were composed of cellulose acetate, a modified natural polymer [5]. In Australia, 
it is estimated that over 20 billion filtered cigarettes are consumed each year, of which an  estimated 7 
billion are littered into the environment [6]. That is 1 out of every 3 smoker litter their CBs into the 
environment.  
The Cigarette Butt Pollution Project in the USA es timated that one-third of the total production of 
cigarettes was littered to the environment [7] . While Clean Up Australia [8] reported CBs as the most 
common source of rubbish collected in Australia under the miscellaneous category, representing 91.5%. 
The littering of CBs is a gro wing waste management issue and requires specific planning. As climate 
change is becoming the world's biggest challenge, recycling wastes for sustainable development is 
becoming more crucial [9].  
There has been a great attempt by many Australian companies to develop CB disposal systems. The 
system involves placing receptacles that come in the form of wall -mounted, post -mounted, and 
freestanding in areas easily accessible by the public to dispose of their CB waste. Servicing regimes are 
also provided by companies. The CBs are then collected from the receptacles and disposed of. 
Landfilling and incineration are the two disposal methods for the collected CBs, however, both methods 
are costly and pose high exposure risks from hazardous fumes, which can be severely detrimental to the 
environment and human health [10].  
When CBs are littered, harmful chemicals are leached into the environment, posing major concerns for 
the global environment and the quality of urban and aquatic life. In cigarette smoke, it is possible to 
classify more than 4,000 chemical components that are generated during burning or that are distilled 
from tobacco, of which 69 are carcinogenic [11, 12]. The main toxic agents include polycyclic aromatic 
hydrocarbons, formaldehyde, argon, carbon monoxide, nitrogen oxides, benzene, hydrogen cyanide, 
ammonia, phenol, acetaldehyde, argon, acetone, and pyridines [11].  
Cigarettes are made of cellulose acetate filters, which are designed to fully or partially retain particulate 
smoke components, including tar and toxic chemicals [13]. Cellulose acetate filters have poor 
biodegradability and can take up to 18 months to decompose under normal environmental conditions, 

=== PDF PAGE 22 ===
 4  
 
hence, posing a serious dilemma in terms of toxic waste and litter disposal [14, 15] . CBs littered on 
streets commonly get washed away into stor m-water drains and end up on beaches, or in rivers and 
harbors, leaching toxic chemicals [16]. Marine life regularly mistake CBs for food, and they have been 
discovered in the stomachs of sea turtles, birds, fish, and other creatures in which they have caused 
severe digestive problems that have led to death [17, 18] . Being a major environmental issue, this has 
led researchers to explore feasible and practical recycling techniques. The incorporation of CBs in 
construction materials, particularly in fired clay bricks [1]  and asphalt concrete [19 -21], has gained 
pervasive interest. 
A number of studies have been conducted on recycling wastes in FCBs to investigate the relationship 
between the use of additives in fired clay bricks and the micro-pores and nano-pores formed as a result. 
Such additives include waste sawdust [15], desulfurization slag and basic oxygen furnace aggregates 
[16], marble powde r [17], biomass incineration [18], recycled paper processing residues [4] , bio -
briquette ash [19], sugarcane bagasse ash [20], and cigarette butts [21–27]. A recent study revealed the 
substructures produced by the additive olive pomace (10% weight) that resulted in a 14.4% decrease in 
thermal conductivity [28] . Comparing this to expanded vermiculite (10% content), a pore -forming 
material that expands when heated, declined from 0.96 to 0.65 W mK-1, which is a 32% drop in thermal 
conductivity [3]. While olive mill solid residue (OMSD) demonstrated the highest decrease in thermal 
conductivity of 70% with the use of 15% OMSD in fired clay bricks [29]. 
In 2016, Mohajerani et al. [1]  developed a novel method involving the recycling of CBs in fired clay 
bricks (FCBs) and proposed by incorporating 1% CBs into 2.5% of the worlds brick production, the 
global waste catastrophe can potentially be solved [1, 22-24] . With the addition of 2.5% CBs in fired 
clay bricks, density decreased by 8.3%, while bricks incorporating 1% CBs, the compressi ve strength 
was determined to be 19.53 MPa. All the mechanical and physical property values were within the 
acceptable range of the Australian Standards [1] . Through the addition of CBs, porous bricks were 
produced, and a drastic decrease in thermal conductivity and an increase in energy savings was observed 
with the increase in CB content [1].  
1.2 Research Objectives 
This study aims to investigate and develop environmentally and economically sustainable and practical 
techniques to recycle CB waste in FCBs. To major objectives are: 
1. To investigate and develop practical techniques to recycle CB waste in FCBs without compromising 
the physical and mechanical properties and determine the optimum percentage of CBs that can be 
recycled in FCBs. 

=== PDF PAGE 23 ===
 5  
 
2. To study energy savings during the firing process of clay bricks incorporating CBs and investigate 
the reduction of thermal conductivity of FCBs incorporating CBs. 
3. To conduct a macro- and micro -structural analysis of FCBs incorporating CBs to quantify the 
porosity and characterize the size, shape, and distribution of pores. 
4. To conduct a comprehensive leachate analysis of; (1) FCBs incorporating CBs and (2) bitumen and 
wax encapsulated CBs for heavy metals at three pH values. 
5. To conduct an extraction analysis of Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons (PAHs) from  FCBs 
incorporating CBs and investigate the carbon content in FCBs recycled with CBs. 
6. To develop practical procedures and guidelines for the implementation of the incorporation of CBs 
in FCBs on an industrial scale. 
7. To investigate and develop techniques to minimize or eradicate the odors generated during the 
production processes of FCBs incorporating CBs. 
1.3 Significance of the Research 
1. The research will develop and establish techniques to recycle CBs in FCBs. 
2. The research will potentially reduce the pollution caused by littered CBs. 
3. Lightweight, low-cost, energy-efficient, and environmentally friendly FCBs will be produced with 
the addition of CBs. 
4. The addition of CBs will partially replace the main raw material (clay) used in making FCBs.  
1.4 Thesis Structure 
This study developed an environmentally and economically sustainable and practical technique to 
recycle CB waste in FCBs. All stages of the proposed application on an industrial scale were addressed 
and investigated. This thesis is divided into seven chapters. 
Chapter 1 provides a comprehensive background of the research study, research objectives, significance 
of the research, and thesis structure.  
Chapter 2 provides a comprehensive review through its discussion of the various studies that have been 
carried out on the toxicity a nd valorization of cigarette butt waste and investigates the feasibility and 
sustainability of the recycling methods adopted. 

=== PDF PAGE 24 ===
 6  
 
Chapter 3 investigates the physical and mechanical properties of fired clay bricks incorporating 0%, 
0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass. The energy-saving percentage during the firing process of bricks 
and the reduction in thermal conductivity are presented as a function of the CBs present in the mixtures. 
Additionally, water absorption, compressive strength, efflorescence,  initial rate of absorption (IRA), 
density, and shrinkage were tested. 
Chapter 4 investigates the thermal properties, energy savings, porosity, and micro- and macro-structure 
of fired clay bricks incorporating various percentages of CBs. A novel method was developed to 
monitor the energy consumption of the furnace during the firing process, and Scanning Electron 
Microscopy (SEM) and X -ray micro -Computed Tomography (micro-CT) imaging were used to 
characterize the quantity, distribution, size, and shape of the micro- and macro-scale pores of the brick 
samples incorporating CBs. 
Chapter 5 presents and discusses the leaching behaviour of potentially hazardous metals from used, 
unused, shredded used CBs, bitumen and paraffin wax encapsulated CBs, and unfired and fired clay  
bricks incorporating CBs. The leachate analysis was conducted according to the Australian Bottle 
Leaching Procedure (ABLP) for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. 
Chapter 6 aims to conduct a comprehensive evaluation of PAHs in FCBs incorporated with CBs to 
examine the potential environmental impact of utilizing this toxic waste in the production of FCBs. In 
this study, a direct Soxhlet extraction method was employed, and the extract was analyzed for sixteen 
US EPA priority PAH compounds using gas chromatography -mass spectrometry (GC–MS). 
Furthermore, the FCB samples incorporating CBs were studied for their carbon content using the 
carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and sulfur (CHNS) analyzer and thermogravimetric analysis (TGA). 
Chapter 7 aims to conduct a bacteriological study involving the investigation of the presence of ten 
common bacteria in two pilot studies. In addition, to examining various sterilisation methods for CBs. 
Moreover, three processing methods are studied and suggested for the implementation of recycling CBs 
in FCBs on an industrial scale.  
Chapter 8 aims to determine techniques to detect and analyze the volatile organic compounds (VOCs) 
generated from CBs, along with investigating methods to minimize or eradicate the odors generated 
during the production processes of FCBs incorporating CBs. Several industrial methods and techniques 
to eliminate the emissions on an industrial scale were reviewed and analysed, including ultraviolet -C 
(UVC) light, ozone, and vaporized hydrogen peroxide.  
Chapter 9 summari ses the key findings , provides conclusions based on this study and offers future 
recommendations for possible studies in this area of research. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 25 ===
 7  
 
Chapter 2: The Toxicity and Valorization 
Options of Cigarette Butts 
Abstract  
Cigarette butts, one of the most abundant forms of waste in the world, contain more than 4,000 toxic 
chemicals and pose serious risks to the health of wildlife, humans, and marine and freshwater 
organisms. Although trivial in size, trillions of cigarettes are produced every year worldwide, resulting 
in the accumulation of tonnes of toxic waste litter. In 2016, a world production of over 5.7 trillion 
cigarettes was reported with the majority comprising cellulose acetate filters – a polymer with poor 
biodegradability. Depending on the environmental conditions, cellulose acetate filters can take up to 10 
years to decompose during which time they leach heavy metals and toxic chemicals into the 
environment. Although possible disposal methods for collected cigarette butt waste include incineration 
and landfilling, both techniques may result in the release of hazardous fumes and can be costly. 
However, recycling CBs in different materials could be a possible solution for this concurrent 
environmental pollution. A number of novel studies have been publicized on recycling cigarette butts 
with encouraging results, and several methods have been studied, including the recycling of cigarette 
butts in asphalt concrete and fired clay bricks, as a carbon source, sound absorbing material, corrosion 
inhibitor, biofilm carrier, and many more. Hence, this paper provides a comprehensive review through 
its discussion of the various studies that have been carried out on the toxicity and valorization of 
cigarette butt waste and investigates the feasibility and sustainability of the recycling methods adopted. 
However, for the widespread application of recycling cigarette butt waste, further research and 
developments are essential. 
The research presented in this chapter has been published in the following: 
Kurmus, H. and Mohajerani, A., 2020. The toxicity and valorization options of cigarette butts. Waste 
Management, 104, 104–118. 104-118. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.wasman.2020.01.011. 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
 8  
 
2.1 Introduction 
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the world’s most prevalent source of waste litter. The Tobacco Atlas [4] 
reported that during the year 2016, 5.7 trillion cigarettes were consumed worldwide and that about 97% 
of the cigarette filters were composed of cellulose acetate, a modified natural polymer [5]. In Australia, 
it is estimated that over 20 billion filtered cigarettes are consumed each year, of which an estimated 7 
billion are littered into the environment [6]. That is 1 out of every 3 smoker litter their CBs into the 
environment. China is the leading consumer of cigarettes, smoking 41.9% of all cigarettes globally [4]. 
It is estimated that during the period 2015 to 2025, tobacco smokers are set to increase by 7 million in 
Nigeria and 24 million in Indonesia [4]. 
To address the tobacco menace, certain developed countries have implemented strong tobacco control 
regulations and have seen significant reductions in smoking [25]. However, the decline in smokers 
appears insignificant when compared with the accruing cigarette consumption around the world.  
There is ongoing concern regarding the recycling of cigarette butt (CB) waste. The Cigarette Butt 
Pollution Project in the USA estimated that one -third of the total production of cigarettes was littered 
to the environment [7] . While Clean Up Australia [8]  reported CBs as the most co mmon source of 
rubbish collected in Australia under the miscellaneous category, representing 91.5%. The littering of 
CBs is a growing waste management issue and requires specific planning. As climate change is 
becoming the world's biggest challenge, recycl ing wastes for sustainable development is becoming 
more crucial [9].  
There has been a great attempt by many Australian companies to develop CB disposal systems. The 
system involves placing receptacles that come in the form of wall -mounted, post -mounted, and 
freestanding in areas easily accessible by the public to dispose of their CB waste. Servicing regimes are 
also provided by companies. The CBs are then collected from the receptacles and disposed of. 
Landfilling and incineration are the two disposal methods for the collected CBs, however, both methods 
are costly and pose high exposure risks from hazardous fumes, which can be severely detrimental to the 
environment and human health [10].  
When CBs are littered, harmful chemicals are leached into the environment, posing major concerns for 
the global environment and the quality of urban and aquatic life. In cigarette smoke, it is possible to 
classify more than 4,000 chemical components that are generated during burning or that are distilled 
from tobacco, of which 69 are carcinogenic [11, 12]. The main toxic agents include polycyclic aromatic 
hydrocarbons, formaldehyde, argon, carbon monoxide, nitrogen oxides, benzene, hydrogen cyanide, 
ammonia, phenol, acetaldehyde, argon, acetone, and pyridines [11].  

=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
 9  
 
Cigarettes are made of cellulose acetate filters, which are designed to fully or partially retain particulate 
smoke components, including tar and toxic chemicals [13]. Cellulose acetate filters have poor 
biodegradability and can take up to 18 months to decompose under normal environmental conditions, 
hence, posing a serious dilemma in terms of toxic waste and litter disposal [14, 15].  
CBs littered on streets commonly get washed away into storm -water drains and end up on beaches, or 
in rivers and harbors, leaching toxic chemicals [16]. Marine life regularly mistake CBs for food, and 
they have been discovered in the stomachs of sea turtles, birds, fish, and other creatures in which they 
have caused severe digestive problems that have led to death [17, 18] . Marinello et al. [26] , and 
Torkashvand and Farzadkia [27] also reviewed multiple solutions to CB management. Marinello et al. 
[26] have focused on the advantages and disadvantages of the reviewed CB recycling procedures and 
suggested proposals for future research. While Torkashvand and Farzadkia [27]  presented suggestions 
and models for the control of CB littering.  
The objective of this paper is to collect and review all major research studies on the toxicity and 
valorization of CB waste and investigate the feasibility and sustainability of the recycling methods 
adopted. Particular attention has been given to the toxicity and historical background of CBs, the 
leachability aspect of the newly derived materials, and the interpretation of engineering properties of 
two major potential applications (fired clay bricks and asphalt concrete) with graphical results and 
variations. Eleven CB recycling proposals and practices have been studied, including; (1) mixing in 
fired clay bricks, (2) mixing in asphalt concrete, (3) production of c ellulose pulp, (4) utilization as 
corrosion inhibitor, (5) preparation of porous carbon, (6) preparation of activated and charred carbon, 
(7) utilization as sound absorbing material, (8) productions of superhydrophobic sorbent , (9) use as 
biofilm carrier, (10) preparation of cellulose nano-crystals and (11) use as vector control.  
Valorization of  CBs in sustainable products may be an environmentally friendly way to manage a 
continuously growing pollution calamity and a sustainable development for the health of wildlife, 
humans, marine and freshwater organisms [9]. In some cases, it even implies an enchantment to the 
modified material. However, based on the review, further research and developments are recommended. 
In particular, to the methods adopted in handling the CBs and life cycle assessments for all CB recycling 
proposals.  
This systematic review was undertaken by searching papers in ScienceDirect, PubMed, Web of Science, 
and ProQuest with the aim of finding major investigations performed on recycling CBs.  The paper 
search was carried out in January 2019.  
The research was undertaken by identifying relevant keywords specific to cigarette butts (CBs), CB 
waste, and the application of recycling CBs to obtain novel materials applicable in civil engineering, 

=== PDF PAGE 28 ===
 10  
 
industrial, agricultural, and chemical or environmental cases. The primary keywords, ‘cigarette butt’ or 
‘cigarette’ or ‘cigarette filter’, were searched individually and had to appear in the title or abstract. They 
were then coupled with the secondary keywords ‘recycling’ or ‘toxicity’ or ‘littering’ or health 
implications’ or ‘history’ or ‘composition’ to gather quality information and data to prepare a 
comprehensive review paper. Titles, abstracts, and keywords which included any of the primary, or 
primary and secondary keywords, were studied, and 186 papers were found. 
The search process was followed by a selection and screening process to select papers most relevant to 
the scope of this study. The screening methodology is described as follows: 
i. The title and abstract were examined and checked whether the keywords ‘CB’ or ‘cigarette’ 
were consistent. If not, the paper was discarded.  
ii. Reliable content that covered interesting technologies on CB recycling was examined and 
checked whether the methodological process utilized was provided by the authors. Content on 
the toxicity of cigarettes or toxicity of littered CBs were reviewed and only selected if reliable 
experiments were conducted. Papers that provided information on the history and composition 
of cigarettes were thoroughly read and analyzed to make sure data was provided.    
iii. Publications that acknowledged the toxicity of CB waste or cigarettes were only selected. 
iv. The references in the selected papers were examined and read. 
Once all steps were considered and applied, a total of 101 papers were selected and reviewed.  
2.1.1 History of Cigarette Butts 
Tobacco was consumed in the form of cigars or through chewing tobacco in the late 19th and early 20th 
centuries. However, during the late 20 th century, cigarettes became the preferred tobacco smoking 
method due to the low-cost and efficiency in nicotine absorption [28]. Now, tobacco is one of the most 
widely used addictive substances in the world, growing in at least 124 countries and occupying more 
than 4.3 million hectares of land, which is equivalent to the entire land footprint of Switzerland [29]. 
By 1950, major concerns began escalating regarding the health effects of smoking. Researchers found 
a statistical connection between smoking and lung cancer, and a significant number of articles were 
published in medical and academic journals addressing the negative health effects. Following these 
findings, the tobacco industry responded by manufacturing low -tar and filtered cigarettes [30]. Tar is 
one of the toxic chemical particles remaining after burning tobacco and constitutes the leading health 
risks associated with smoking, including cancer, while nicotine is the chemical that causes addiction 
[12]. The filter provides a mouthpiece for s mokers and prevents the release of tobacco flakes into the 
smoker’s mouth. Subsequent to the modifications made to cigarettes, the nicotine yield per smoked 

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
 11  
 
cigarette decreased from 2.7 to 0.85 mg, and the tar yields decreased from 38 to 12 mg during the period 
1953 to 1996. The market share of filtered cigarettes jumped from 5% in 1953 to 20% in 1955, and, by 
1975, 90% of the manufactured cigarettes contained filters [31]. However, it should be noted that the 
reduced yields described here are for machine smoked cigarettes, and not measured in humans. 
According to the Washington DC -based Ocean Conservancy’s global beach clean -ups, since the 
development of cigarette filters, CBs have been reported as the most collected waste each year since 
the beginning of the initiative in 1986 [32] . A single machine can make up to 20,000 or more filter 
cigarettes per minute, which is equivalent to 333 cigarettes per second; consequently, 67 cartons of 
cigarettes are produced every 40 sec. In a year if 5.7 trillion cigarettes are manufactured, and about 97% 
of them contain filters, more than one million tonnes of plastic is being littered into the environment. 
At present, China is the largest producer and consumer of cigarettes, having consumed 2.35 trillion 
cigarettes in 2016 (Fig. 2.1). If current trends continue, cigarette production is expected to increase and, 
subsequently, CB litter. 
 
Fig. 2.1 Global cigarette consumption by WHO region, 1999 – 2016, in trillions (adapted from The Tobacco 
Atlas [4]) . 1Africa. 2Americas. 3Eastern Mediterranean. 4European. 5Southeast Asia. 6Western Pacific 
2.2 Composition of CBs  
The modern composition of a cigarette consists of a filter, cigarette paper, tobacco, tipping paper, and 
additives (Fig. 2). 


=== PDF PAGE 30 ===
 12  
 
2.2.1 Paper 
The tipping paper, which stabilizes the mouthpiece from saliva, is wrapped around the filter and 
contains small ventilation holes that allow fresh air to mix with the smoke, thereby diluting the toxic 
mix of chemicals inhaled [33] ; the cigarette paper is below the tipping paper and holds the tobacco 
blend; as shown Fig. 2.2. However, once the CB is discarded into the environment, cigarette and tipping 
paper will still be remaining. The paper may take up to 3 - 5 months to biodegrade under ambient 
conditions [34]. 
 
Fig. 2.2 Physical structure of a cigarette with each component identified and described. 
2.2.2 Tobacco and Additives  
The two major subgenera of the botanical genus Nicotiana are N. Rustica and N. Tabacum, which are 
the main commercial tobaccos [11]. The N. Tabacum is grown as the primary tobacco crop, which 
comes as flue-cured (Virginia tobacco), air-cured (burley tobacco), light air-cured, dark air-cured, and 
sun-cured tobacco (oriental tobacco) [35]. The composition of the tobacco blend varies according to the 
preferences of the country [11]. The manufacturing process of the tobacco consist of many stages, 
including growing the tobacco, harvesting the tobacco, curing the leaf, moistening, stripping, sorting, 
conditioning, aging, and, finally, blending the leaves [36]. The processing of the leaves produces several 
by-products, which are processed separately and then added back into the cigarette blend. Additionally, 
additives are incorporated into the tobacco mixture, including humectants and flavor additives. The 


=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
 13  
 
primary humectants in cigarette tobacco are glycerol and propylene glycol; their function is to preserve 
the plasticity and moisture in the cigarette and prevent or delay the drying of tobacco. While common 
flavor additives include licorice, cocoa solids, and various sugars, these additives act to mask the harsh 
flavors of smoke [11] . When a cigarette is discarded into the environment, unburned tobacco residue 
may remain in the cigarette butt. 
2.2.3 Filter - Cellulose Acetate 
Cellulose is a naturally occurring polymer of glucose (C 6H7O6) that is derived primarily from wood 
pulp and cotton linters. Each glucose unit consists of three hydroxyl groups (OH), which can hydrogen 
bond to adjacent chains; the result is a tough material [37]. Due to the extended chains of cellulose, it 
is almost impossible for solvents to penetrate in. The only way to process cellulose is to block the OH 
groups (hydrogen bonding) with acetate groups. Therefore, by esterifying natural cellulose with acetic 
acid, cellulose acetate is produced (Fig. 2.3) [38].  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 2.3 Molecular structure of cellulose acetate, which is used in the filter of a CB. 
Cellulose acetate is the primary component in the filter of a CB. Therefore the high degree of acetate 
substitution prevents the cellulose in the littered CB to access the microorganisms (bacterium, fungal, 
etc.) in the environment for biological decomposition [39, 40].  Ultraviolet (UV) radiation also promotes 
cellulose acetate degradation, however, the remaining tipping paper on the CB inhibits the sunlight UV 
radiation reaching the cellulose acetate fibers [40]. Ultimately the paper biodegrades, and the exposure 
of the UV radiation to the cellulose acetate surges. However, the composition of the cellulose acetate 
restricts the exposure of the sunlight to the surface area of the fibers. Additionally, the microorganisms 
within the environment do not accept the cellulose acetate as a food source [41]. Therefore, de-
acetylation proceeds through hydrolysis, however cellulose acetate hydrolysis is a slow course at 


=== PDF PAGE 32 ===
 14  
 
ambient conditions. Hence why, under anaerobic conditions, it may take up to 1 -2 months in soil, 6- 9 
months in freshwater, and 12 months or more under poor conditions for the CBs to degrade [14, 42]. 
2.3 Toxicity of Cigarette Butts 
When a cigarette is smoked, it is converted to particulate and volatile phases. The particulate phase 
consists of non-volatile and semi-volatile chemicals, and the vapor phase consists of volatile chemicals. 
The vapor phase is the portion of the smoke that passes through the filter, and 95% of its weight is made 
up of 400- 500 individual gaseous compounds including nitrogen, oxygen, carbon dioxide, carbon 
monoxide, water, argon, hydrogen, acetone, nitrogen oxides, and volatile sulfur compounds [30, 43] . 
The particulate phase is the portion of the smoke that is trapped in the filter and is made up of the 
remainder of the smoke weight with more than 3500 individual components; these include nicotine, 
nicotiana alkaloids, compounds specific to solanaceae, catechols,  aromatic hydrocarbons, and 
polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon (PAH) [11, 30].  
PAH was the first carcinogen identified in a cigarette [44], and, in 2000, the number of carcinogens 
identified in tobacco smoking increased to 69 [45]. According to the International Agency for Research 
on Cancer [45], among the carcinogens, 69 are animal carcinogens, 48 are possibly carcinogenic to 
humans, 8 are probably carcinogenic to humans, and 11 are proven to be carcinogenic to humans [11, 
30]. Hence, cigarette smoking is a serious threat to humans, for every cigarette burned, a smoker 
receives roughly 1.4 - 2.2 mg of dangerous compounds [46]. It is estimated a standard cigarette contains 
between 9 to 30 mg nicotine, of which 0.5 – 2 mg is inhaled by the smoker. While, 4 - 8 mg is the 
estimated toxic dose of nicotine intake for non-smokers [47]. The development of many organ systems 
can be detrimentally affected by nicotine due to the CBs toxicity, including the respiratory, 
cardiovascular, nervous, and reproductive systems [48, 49].  
Smokers and non-smokers may be uninformed regarding the toxicity level of CBs, and the potential 
hazard the waste may pose to the environment and wildlife [50]. When CBs are littered into the 
environment, significant amounts of toxic chemicals are leached and potentially accumulated in water 
channels, streets, nature strips, and footpaths, thereby posing a serious threat to local organisms and 
aquatic species [50]. Children are more likely to be exposed to CBs through oral contact as they often 
like to explore their environments, and 1 - 2 mg of nicotine is sufficient to cause vomiting and nausea 
due to the CBs toxicity [47]. CBs are reported as the most common toxic substance causing symptoms 
in children [51]. 
In 2011, Moerman and Potts [52] investigated the leaching behavior of heavy metals in CBs. The main 
purpose of the study was to determine the concentration of metals leached and the effect on the 
surrounding environment. To understand how the pH level affects leaching behavior, a pH range of 

=== PDF PAGE 33 ===
 15  
 
6.00 - 4.00 was selected, which is typically observed in natural rainwater. It was found the metals 
studied have different leaching behaviors over time. A direct relationship was found between the 
concentration of leached metals and the soaking period. An increase in metal concentration leached for 
Ba, Fe, Mn, and Sr was seen over time, while no significant change in metal concentration was seen for 
Ti, Pb, Zn, and Ni. In contrast, a decrease in metal concentration for Cu, Al, Cr, and Cd was observed 
over time. The variable relationships for the selected metals B a, Zn, and Cu can be seen in Fig. 2. 4. 
This indicates that a piece of cigarette litter is a point source of metal contamination for a least a month. 
Moreover, it suggests that the longer the waste remains in the environment, the greater the 
contamination of Ba, Fe, Mn, and Sr [52].  
 
Fig. 2.4 Relationships observed between metal concentration leached from smoked cigarettes and soaking time 
in aqueous solution (adapted from Moerman and Potts [52])  
To analyze the toxicity of CBs, the metal concentrations of whole unsmoked cigarettes were measured. 
The experimental studies exposed more than one-fifth of Mn, Zn, and Ba, one quarter of Cu and half of 
Sr and Pb may potentially be leached into the environment [53, 54] . When placed in contrast to the 
estimated amounts of CBs littered annually (4.95 trillion) [55] , the results indicate 9.26 ± 0.5 kg (Al), 
8.76 ± 2.52 kg (Ba) and 38.1 ± 8.2 kg (Fe) may enter the environment each year from CB litter [52].  
While Slaughter et al. [17] conducted a comprehensive study to identify the LC50 (lethal concentration 
50) of CB leachate to freshwater and marine ecosystems. The LC50 for leachate from smoked CBs was 
0.97 and 1.1 CB/l for both the freshwater fathead minnow and marine topsmelt. While the leachate from 
smoked cigarette filters exhibited reduced values of 4.3 and 1.8 CBs/l for both fish species. For 
unsmoked cigarette filters, LC50 values of 13.5 and 5.1 CBs/l were found for fish species [17]. 
Therefore, the results indicate the toxicity of CB leachate increase from unsmoked to smoked filter.  


=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
 16  
 
Chevalier et al. [56] investigated the possible release of nanoscale particles from CB waste by leaching 
the waste with rainwater. The results revealed the release of 4.12 +/ - 0.24% organic carbon in the 
spectrum of 10 nm up to 400 nm. Moreover, the analysis showed that As, Pb, Cd Cu, Ni, Cr, Co, Al, 
Mn, Zn, and Fe metallic species were principally attached to the nanoscale particles per gram of carbon 
released [56]. Hence, the results indicate an evolving global contamination of toxic chemicals, which 
requires urgent attention and investigation. 
To evaluate the genotoxic potential of CBs , Di Giacomo et al. [57] studied the mutagenicity of CBs in 
the bacterial reverse mutation test on Escherichia coli WP2uvrA and Salmonella typhimurium TA98 
and TA100 stra ins. The experiments were both conducted in the presence and absence of the S9 
exogenous metabolic activator. The findings confirmed the mutagenicity of CBs in the presence of the 
exogenous metabolic activator S9, thereby verifying that CBs contain pro-carcinogenic substances. As 
a possible preventative strategy, the natural sesquiterpenes β-caryophyllene oxide (CRYO) and β -
caryophyllene (CRY) compounds were studied to investigate their ability to inhibit the mutagenicity of 
CBs. This strategy was based on previous studies proposing the chemopreventative properties of CRYO 
and CRY [58 -62]. The experimental results confirmed the antimutagenicity properties of CRY and 
CRYO on CBs, and, hence, the possibility of using the compound as an environmental decontaminator. 
Furthermore, the paper suggests utilizing the compound as a maceration solution to apply during 
disposal for smokers and for use during degradation procedures for CB waste [57]. 
2.4 Research on Valorisation of Cigarette Butts  
2.4.1 Mixing in Fired Clay Bricks  
Due to the durable nature of the material, bricks have been used in construction for many years . Fired 
clay bricks are mainly composed of clay and sand, which are bound together under high temperatures 
in a kiln, making a remarkably strong construction material. Lately, incorporating waste materials in 
fired clay bricks has been investigated in the hope of offering a sustainable solution to a growing 
pollution catastrophe. Common waste materials include rubber [63] , polystyrene [64], fly ash [65], 
sludge [66], sawdust and limestone [67], and processed waste tea [68] . Moreover, in 2016, Ukwatta et 
al. [69] conducted a study based on incorporating biosolids in fired clay bricks. The results indicated 
that by adding up to 50% biosolids in fired clay bricks, acceptable physical and mechanical properties 
can be achieved, and up to 25% energy can be saved during the firing process [69]. Although recycling 
waste material in fired clay bricks may improve porosity, strength, and shrinkage, further 
comprehensive testing is required in the field of toxic leaching.  
In a recent research, the application of recycling CBs in fired clay bricks were comprehensively 
investigated [1, 22, 24, 70]. The experimental procedure involved preparing clay bricks with 2.5%, 5%, 

=== PDF PAGE 35 ===
 17  
 
7.5%, and 10% CB content by weight. CBs of varying sizes and brands were provided by Buttout 
Australia Pty Ltd (Fig. 2.5). The mixtures were prepared using a Hobart mechanical mixer with mixing 
times of 5, 10, and 15 min. The samples were then compacted manually in molds using predetermined 
masses, and the specimens were dried at 105 ºC for 24 h before being fired in a furnace at 1050 ºC. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 2.5 Prepared CBs of varying sizes and brands for recycling [1] 
The samples were tested based on their mechanical and physical properties and compared to control 
bricks with 0% CB content (Fig. 2.6). In addition, thermal conductivity, the effects of mixing time, and 
heating rate on brick properties, energy savings during firing, emission of gaseous compounds, and 
leachability were tested [1].  
 
 
 
 
Fig. 2.6 Cross-sectional view of bricks with 0% (a) and 2.5% (b) CBs [1] 
The property test results show a dramatic decrease in compressive strength from 25.65  MPa with 0% 
CB content to 3 MPa with 10% CB content (Fig. 2.7). The dry density decreased from 2118 kg m-3 for 
the control sample to 1482 kg m -3 with 10% CB content, producing lightweight bricks. The water 
absorption increased from 5% with 0% CB content to 18% with 7.5% CB content. Moreover, the results 
show a firing energy saving of 30.8% with the incorporation of 2.5% CB content, and a 58.4% energy 


=== PDF PAGE 36 ===
 18  
 
saving with a 5% CB content [1]. Additionally, an increase in CB content resulted in a red uction of 
thermal conductivity significantly. Therefore, incorporating CBs  in the manufacturing of bricks may 
solve a growing pollution problem, however, further research is essential, including a life cycle 
assessment.  
 
Fig. 2.7 Compressive strength versus CB% by mass [1] 
2.4.1.1 Leachate test on fired clay bricks incorporated with CBs 
The main environmental concern regarding recycling CBs in construction material is the possible 
leaching of contaminants, such as heavy metals. Therefore, leachate tests are necessary to assess the 
risk of contaminated leaching. Kadir and Mohajerani [70] investigated the possible levels of leachability 
of manufactured fired clay bricks incorporating 0%, 2.5%, 5%, and 10% CB cont ent. Leachate tests 
were carried out based on the Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP), Toxicity Characteristics 
Leaching Procedure (TCLP), and Static Leachate Test (SLT). The objective of the study was to 
determine whether the leaching of heavy metals including Arsenic (As), selenium (Se), Mercury (Hg), 
Barium (Ba), Cadmium (Cd), Chromium (Cr), Lead (Pb), Silver (Ag), Zinc (Zn), Copper (Cu), and 
Nickel (Ni) from the modified bricks exceeded the regulatory standards. The results exhibited a positive 
correlation between the leachate of heavy metals and leaching time; by day 134, a decrease in leachate 
was noted [70]. Low concentrations in leaching were found, which can be attributed to the high firing 
temperature during the firing process of the clay bricks incorporating CBs. The tests confirm that all 
the heavy metal leachate values were insignificant and below the standard acceptable limits. 
2.4.2 Mixing with Asphalt Concrete  
Asphalt concrete is a composite material consisting of coarse and fine aggregates, bituminous binder, 
and filler [71]. The material plays a fundamental role in flexible pavement structures, due to its ability 
to withstand high volumes of traffic load in various environmental conditions. Due to its composite 
nature, mixing asphalt concrete with various waste materials has gained widespread attention. The 


=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
 19  
 
application of waste materials in asphalt concrete mixtures may solve major environmental issues, 
reduce the demand for natural aggregates, and improve the performance of asphalt concrete [72]. 
Common modes of failure for asphalt concrete include rutting due to excessive deformation, cracking 
due to fatigue, raveling due to oxidation, and hardening of the binder.   
Therefore, methods to improve the properties of asphalt concrete are constantly being investigated. 
Experiments have confirmed that the incorporation of car tires [73], glass [74], pulverized computer 
circuit boards [75], and nuclear and hazardous wastes [76] have improved the chemical and mechanical 
properties of asphalt concrete. Paranavithana and Mohajerani [77] investigated the possible application 
of recycled concrete aggregates in asphalt concrete. The study found lower resilient modulus, creep, 
and volumetric property values (except for the percentage of air voids) when compared with the values 
for fresh aggregate specimens [77]. 
In a recent study, a group of researchers investigated the application of CBs in asphalt concrete [19]. 
The experiment involved preparing 10 kg m-3, 15 kg m-3, and 25 kg m-3 CBs encapsulated with different 
bitumen classes (C170, C320, and C600) (Fig. 2. 8) and incorporating them into an asphal t concrete 
mixture with Class 170 Bitumen (Fig. 2.9). CBs of varying sizes and brands were provided by Buttout 
Australia Pty Ltd. The initial step of the experimental procedure involved drying CBs in an oven to 
eliminate any trapped moisture. Once dried, the CBs were encapsulated with different classes of hot 
bitumen (C170, C320, and C600) and left to dry (Fig. 2.8). The aggregates were placed in the oven for 
24 h at 105º, once dried, the asphalt mix, the aggregates, and bitumen were placed in the oven and 
heated for 1 h at 150 ºC. The aggregates were then mixed with the bitumen for 3 min, then the asphalt 
mix and mold were conditioned for 1 h at 150 ºC in the oven and mixed together with the encapsulated 
CBs. Once conditioned, the samples were compacted using a gyratory compactor. Following a similar 
procedure to the first investigation, the second investigation involved assessing the effects of 
incorporating 10 kg m -3 of CBs  encapsulated with paraffin wax into asphalt concrete mixes 
manufactured with Classes 170 and 320 bitumen. 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 2.8 CBs encapsulated in bitumen [19] 


=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
 20  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 2.9 Asphalt concrete samples manufactured with CBs of 10 kg m-3, 15 kg m-3, 25 kg m-3, and no CBs 
(control) [19] 
Several tests were carried out for both investigations, and the experimental results demonstrated 
encouraging results. Asphalt mixes with 10 kg m-3 and 15 kg m-3 CB content encapsulated with bitumen 
demonstrated acceptable bulk density, percentage of air voids, and resilient properties. The stability and 
flow values satisfied the [71]  light, medium, and heavy traffic condition requirements of 5.5 kN for 
light traffic and 6.5 kN for medium and heavy traffic conditions (Fig. 10). Asphalt mixes with 10 kg m-
3 CB content encapsulated with paraffin wax exhibited a decrease in the percentage of air voids and 
voids in mineral aggregate for C170 samples and an increase for C320 samples [19]. Both sa mples 
satisfied the standard requirements of 3 -7% for air voids and a minimum of 15% for voids in mineral 
aggregate, while the stability values only satisfied light traffic conditions for road pavements. In 
addition, the results suggest a reduction in thermal conductivity, and, subsequently, on the Urban Heat 
Island effect due to the increase in porosity [19]. However, further investigations are necessary for the 
application of CBs  encapsulated with paraffin wax and bitumen in asphalt concrete for widespread 
industry adoption.  


=== PDF PAGE 39 ===
 21  
 
 
Fig. 2.10. The effect of different amounts of CBs on the Marshall Stability for CBs encapsulated with different 
classes of bitumen (C170, C320, C600) [19] 
2.4.3 Production of Cellulose Pulp 
Pulp is a lignocellulosic fibrous product manufactured by mechanically or chemically extracting 
cellulose fibers from renewable wood raw material. A group of researchers in Brazil developed a 
technology to produce cellulose pulp by recycling CBs [78]. The main idea of the technology is based 
on separating all the cellulose content in the CB by hydrolyzing the cellulose acetate in the presence of 
strong alkali (Fig. 2.11). The experiment involved four different processes, including dissolving all 
chemicals and ash absorbed in the f ilter, separating the cellulose acetate into cellulose, eliminating 
lignin from the residual tobacco and mitigating its cellulose, and, finally, disaggregating the remaining 
paper [78].  
 
Fig. 2.11 Cellulose pulp production flow chart from CBs 
At the end of the filtering process, cellulose pulp and a dark viscous liquor were produced. The 
experimental results confirmed that the cellulose pulp could, in fact, be used in the paper industry. 


=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
 22  
 
However, the dark liquor produced during the pulping process contained very high resistant organic 
content, including toxic compounds. Several treatment processes were conducted on the effluent, 
including precipitation by acidification, coagulation, and ozonation to reduce the chemical oxygen 
demand (COD) value, however, all methods were unsuccessful. Therefore, after precip itating the 
effluent with a pH of 3, Teixeira et al. [78]  proposed that the clarified effluent should be collected and 
used in a new pulping process. However, the proposed progression for the clarified effluent fails to offer 
a sustainable method. Constant production of effluent will be produced, and the effluent produced will 
be more than what is required for the alkaline pulping, therefore, a practical solution is essential.  
2.4.4 Utilization as Corrosion Inhibitor    
Many investigations have been conducted to determine the c orrosion inhibitive efficiency of nicotine 
[79], with one study presenting promising results involving applying CBs as corrosion inhibitors for 
N80 steel at 90º in a hydrochloric acid solution [80] . The investigation involved performing liquid 
chromatography (LC)/mass spectrometry (MS) and an infrared (IR) reaction analysis system to detect 
the chemical compositions of CB water extracts to ultimately determine the best aging time. The aging 
process involved adding five CBs in 100 mL distilled water  and continuous monitoring by an LC/MS 
and an IR reaction analysis system to determine the highest inhibition efficiency of the aging solution. 
The experimental results recorded 24 h as being the best aging time. To assess the corrosion inhibitive 
effect of the aged solution on N80 steel at 90 ºC in hydrogen chloride (HCI), electrochemical and weight 
loss techniques were used. During the investigation, various CB water extracts and HCI concentrations 
were used to determine their influence on corrosion inhibition. The results presented a maximum 
corrosion inhibition efficiency of 94.6% in 10% HCI solution with 5% inhibitor. However, it was 
determined that the increase in HCI solution increased the corrosion attack [80]. 
Following this study, Zhao et al. [81] compared the corrosion inhibitive effect of anaerobic and aerobic 
CB water extracts on N80 steel at 90º in HCI to determine the effect of oxidation. Mass 
spectrometry/liquid chromatography and the infrared reaction analysis system test method were adopted 
to contrast the chemical compositions of the anaerobic and aerobic extracts. Fewer types of compounds 
were found in the anaerobic extracts than in the aerobic extracts, thereby indicating that the aerobic 
condition may favor CBs water extraction over the anaerobic condition. Additionally, to study the 
corrosion inhibitive mechanism and assess the corrosion inhibitive effect of both anaerobic and aerobic 
CB water extracts, potentiodynamic polarization, X-ray diffraction, weight loss, electrochemical noise, 
and impedance were used. The results revealed that the aerobic extracts consisted of more compounds 
and had greater corrosion inhibitive effects than the anaerobic extracts [81].  

=== PDF PAGE 41 ===
 23  
 
To further enhance the inhibition efficiency, Zhang et al. [82] suggested incorporating cuprous chloride 
(CuCI) to inhibit the corrosion of N80 steel in HCI solution. The study involved comparing the 
corrosion inhibition of water extracts from CBs in the presence and absence of CuCI on N80 steel at 
90º in 15% HCI. In addition to the tests performed in the previous two papers, an X -ray photoelectron 
spectroscopy test was conducted to examine the composition of the protective coat formed by the CuCI. 
The results revealed an increase in inhibition efficiency of 95.3% with the addition of CuCI and 9% CB 
water extract [82] . In addition, the X -ray photoelectron spectroscopy results confirm that the copper 
added water extract from CBs improved the N80 steel surface with a protective coat. Although the 
results seem promising, the paper requires further research. The experimental procedure requires further 
clarification, and a comprehensive study is required on a leachate analysis as the paper fails to mention 
the prospect of toxic leaching during the lifetime of t he modified material.  A summary of all research 
conducted on the application of CBs as a corrosion inhibitor on N80 steel can be seen in Table 2.1.

2.4.5 Preparation of Porous Carbon 
Porous carbon is a versatile material and can be used for several applications due to the material’s high 
porosity, relative easy surface chemical tuning, large surface area, and excellent electron conductivity 
[83]. However, the preparation involves expensive and/or toxic chemicals and sophisticated techniques. 
Polarz et al. [84]  found a simple  one-step procedure for preparing porous carbons, which involves 
inexpensive precursors, cellulose acetate in the form of CBs. In conducting extensive experiments, it 
was found that complex biomolecules like cellulose acetate can produce porous carbons that exhibit 
well-defined positions on different length scales ranging from the nanometre to the micrometre [84] . 
Based on these findings, Lee et al. [85]  and Yu et al. [86] investigated recycling used CBs as a proper 
carbon source for use in super-capacitors. The process involved heat treating used CBs under nitrogen 
(N) containing atmosphere to prepare N-doped mesoporous and microporous hybrid carbon material 
(NCF) through a one -step method; pyrolysis (carbonization) (Fig. 2. 12). The combination of an 
enlarged surface area, the pseudocapacitive behavior, and well -developed mesopores and micropores 
resulted in achieving the desired supercapacitive performance [85].  
 
Fig. 2.12 Fabrication procedure used for preparing NCF by pyrolysis of a used cigarette filter  
Similarly, Ghosha et al. [87] , and Yazdi et al. [88]  prepared a conducting material from used CBs 
through heat treatment; the study required no pre -treatment for the CBs prior to the carbonization 
process. The process involved characterizing and investigating the electrochemical properties of the 
pyrolyzed material through  dynamic light scattering, Raman spectroscopic, X-ray diffraction, ultra -
violet visible spectroscopy, Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy, zeta potential measurements, and 
field emission scanning electron microscopy [88] . The results confirmed the prospect of using the 
pyrolyzed material in electronic applications as the material demonstrated good conductivity [87]. 
However, further investigations are required in this field of study for widespread industry adoption.  


=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
 25  
 
2.4.6 Preparation of Activated and Charred Carbon 
Hamzah and Umar [89]  investigated the potential of preparing active carbon from CB waste through 
microwave induced KOH activation. The microwave heating method was adopted in preparing active 
carbons due to the method’s rapid and uniform heating capacity. The process involved an activation 
time of 20 min at 630 Watts and a frequency of 2.45 GHz. The paper proposed that the resultant active 
carbon can be used as an alternative absorbent material for oil -water separation, supercapacitors, and 
methylene blue due to its structural diversity and large surface area [89].  
Masoudi Soltani et al. [90]  investigated producing charred carbon through the pyrolysis of CBs. The 
charred carbon was modified with NHO3, and a decrease in surface area and micropores was observed. 
The study analyzed the efficiency of using non-modified and nitric acid- modified charred carbon to 
remove lead from the aqueous solution [90]. 
2.4.7 Utilization as Sound Absorbing Material 
In a recent research, Escobar and Maderuelo -Sanz [91] proposed using CBs as an absorbing material 
due to the porous nature of the waste [83]. The investigation involved analyzing the acoustical 
performance of a new kind of material using used and unused CBs. In addition to studying the effects 
of compression, the influence of the length of butts, and a comparison with other materials. The results 
revealed a high absorption coefficient of  < 0.90 from mid-frequencies (> 2000 Hz), indicating that CBs 
can be used as a substitute for artificial based commercial products [91]. The samples used for this study 
comprised intact CBs, however, CBs contain toxic chemicals, and, therefore, require pre-treatment. 
Maderuelo-Sanz et al. [92]  investigated the acoustical performance of treated CBs. The CBs were 
initially dried at 80º and sealed in plastic bags; this process was conducted to disinfect the waste from 
any toxic chemicals and remove humidity. The physical properties, including fiber diameter, porosity, 
skeletal density, and flow resistivity, were measured. The acoustical performance of the new material 
was assessed and compared to other fibre materials, the results revealed that the performance of both 
materials were rather similar, however, the CB material demonstrated better performance [92]. 
2.4.8 Production of Superhydrophobic Sorbent 
Ou et al. [93]  found that by hydrolyzing cellulose acetate fibers from CBs in a sodium hydroxide 
(NaOH) solution, and, subsequently, in an ethanolic solution of hexadecyltrimet hoxysilane, 
superhydrophobic/ superoleophilic fibers (SFs) can be produced. The produced SFs can be used for oil 
spill clean-up due to its wetting behaviors towards kerosene and water. The study involved testing the 
clean-up efficiency of the SFs in a stag ed kerosene spill, and it was found that the SFs had a 96% 
efficiency in absorbing the kerosene. Additionally, the moisture content in the collected SFs was found 

=== PDF PAGE 44 ===
 26  
 
to be lower than the kerosene poured onto the water. Hence, the SFs may be used in the clean-up of oil 
spills, and, simultaneously, overcome the detrimental effects of CBs [93].  
Similarly, Ifelebuegu et al. [94] and Xiong et al. [95] investigated a two-step process to convert waste 
cigarette filters into a oleophilic and superhydrophobic sorbent for the application in spill clean-up and 
water/oil separation. The process involved cleaning the filters ultrasonically, followed by modifying the 
surface through chemical vapor deposition using methyltrichlorosilane. Ifelebuegu et al. [94] found the 
functionalized waste filters can exhibit superhydrophobic properties, absorbing 16 to 26 times their 
weights in numerous oils with up to 20 cycles of reuse capacity. While Xiong et al. [95]  discovered an 
absorption capacity of 80% and 82% for pump and silicone oil after 10 cycles.  
A similar investigation on recycling cigarette filters for oil -water separation was conducted by Liu et 
al. [96] and Liu et al. [97], which involved preparing cigarette filter coated meshes (CFCMs) through a 
facile electrospinning approach. The CFCMs achieved a performance of underwater superoleophobicity 
without the need for any chemical modification. An extraordinary separation efficiency larger than 
99.9% for water/oil mixtures was achieved after many cycles [96, 97].  
2.4.9 Use as Biofilm Carrier 
A study was carried out to evaluate waste cigarette filters in tobacco factories as a biofilm carrier in the 
integrated fixed film activated sludge (IFAS) process [98]. Biofilm reactors are used for the removal of 
nitrogen species and organic carbon in biological wastewater treatment. Hence, this study aimed to 
develop a new, efficient, and inexpensive carrier with the use of cigarette filters. The investigation 
confirmed the effectiveness and feasibility of using cigarette filter rods as a biofilm carrier in an IFAS 
system to simultaneously remove organic matters and nutrients from w astewater [98]. Following this 
study, Sabzali et al. [99] investigated the use of cigarette filter rods as a biofilm carrier in an anaerobic 
moving bed biofilm reactor (AMBBR). The study revealed that cigarette filter rods are feasible and 
effective as Kaldnes media for the removal of phosphate and organic matter from wastewater. 
Additionally, the new bi-carrier is cost effective and durable and can increase the effective mixed liquor 
suspended solids in an anaerobic reaction basin due to the modified materials roughness, high surface 
area, and high porosity properties [99]. 
2.4.10 Preparation of Cellulose Nano-crystals  
In a recent research, Abu-Danso et al. [100] investigated the use of CBs for the removal of diclofenac 
from water. To obtain phosphate ions (HPO) - cellulose nano-fibers (CNFs), acetyl groups were cleaved 
through alkali treatment and etched with HPO from phosphoric acid. While sulfuric acid was used under 
high temperatures to cleave phenol moieties from CNFs to obtain cellulose nano-crystals (CNCs). The 

=== PDF PAGE 45 ===
 27  
 
experiments revealed a maximu m removal capacity of 107.9 mg g -1 of Dfc through the synthesis of 
HPO-CNFs, suggesting that celluloses extracted from CBs can potentially be used as an absorbent for 
the removal of Dfc from water [100].  
2.4.11 Use as Vector Control 
A number of strategies have been developed to control vectors in dengue epidemic areas with 
insecticides [101]. However, the species resistance has resulted in little su ccess. CBs abound in 
chemical products, which are known to have pesticidal, fungicidal, insecticidal, and herbicidal 
properties [102]. Therefore, in search of an alternative control method, Dieng et al. [103]  and Mondal 
et al. [104]  found CB waste to be a potential source to manage mosquito-borne diseases. The study 
involved examining the developmental alterations of Aedes albopictus in the presence of CB waste 
[103]. The results exhibited an increase in the mortality of Aedes albopictus when subjected to CB 
waste solutions. Subsequently, Dieng et al. [105]  assessed the vulnerability of Aedes aegypti to CBs 
during their development and found an interruption of larval development when subjected to CB waste. 
They tested modifications in egg hatching, longevity, reproductive capacity, fecundity, and 
ovipositional behaviors. Based on this study, Dieng et al. [106]  further investigated the effects of CB 
exposure on the dengue vector Aedes aegypti to examine whether the waste may alter any biological 
parameters of parents and their progeny. The results indicate that CB waste can be developed as a novel 
vector control strategy as it has an indirect effect on adult life traits [106] . However, there are many 
unknown factors within this area of study, and, therefore, further research is essential. The valorization 
of CBs into these 11 novel materials has been investigated and summarized in Table 2.2.
