2.5 Discussion and conclusion  
The estimated volume of produced cigarettes worldwide, the persistency, as well as the toxic content 
after usage, support the necessity for efficient disposal and recycling strategies to solve a global CB 
waste catastrophe. Existing disposal strategies for collected CB wast e include incineration and 
landfilling. However, both techniques are considered unsustainable and inefficient due to environmental 
and economic concerns. Therefore, this study has reviewed possible technology on the valorization of 
CB waste and examined the viability of the research conducted. Eleven CB valorization proposals and 
practices have been studied, including; (1) mixing in fired clay bricks, (2) mixing in asphalt concrete, 

=== PDF PAGE 49 ===
 
31 
 
(3) production of cellulose pulp, (4) utilization as corrosion inhibitor, (5) preparation of porous carbon, 
(6) preparation of activated and charred carbon, (7) utilization as sound absorbing material, (8) 
productions of superhydrophobic sorbent, (9) use as biofilm carrier, (10) preparation of cellulose nano-
crystals and (11) use as vector control.  
Based on the extensive research conducted on the toxicity of CBs, it is clear the chemical and physical 
characteristics of CBs can potentially be utilized. However, this does not endorse the practicability and 
sustainability of the recycling method adopted. Hence why it is crucial to conduct a life cycle assessment 
of any new material recycled with CBs to prevent the beginning of a new waste catastrophe for the 
environment.  
Throughout the study, various CB processing methods were revie wed, and a vast majority of the 
recycling methods made use of the chemicals trapped in the CB or the cellulose acetate filter. In contrast, 
to a minority of studies utilising the CB as a whole, without employing a processing technique. In 
summary, the following conclusions have been derived from this investigation: 
• A practical recycling method involved incorporating CBs in the production of bricks. Whereby, 
the literature reviewed proposes that all CBs can sustainably be recycled in fired clay bricks by 
incorporating 1% CBs in 2.5% of the world’s brick production. Additionally, the experimental 
results were found to be within the standard limits for various properties, including strength, 
water absorption, efflorescence, and shrinkage, which are important parameters when 
manufacturing bricks. Moreover, the leaching of contaminants from CBs in bricks was found 
to be negligible. However, further research is recommended, predominantly a life cycle 
assessment.  
• CBs have the potential to be used as an aggregate in asphalt concrete. Asphalt concrete is a 
combination of aggregates and asphalt cement, whereby the aggregate constitutes 90 – 95% of 
the total mixture. Therefore the replacement of CBs in the production of asphalt concrete for 
natural aggregates can offer a sustainable alternative to reduce the demand for natural 
aggregates. Due to the toxic nature of CBs, the encapsulation process will act to prevent the 
leaching of those chemicals by inhibiting water from reaching the filters. Moreover, the 
physical and mechanical properties generally demonstrated acceptable bulk density, percentage 
of air voids, and resilient properties, while the stability values only satisfied the light traffic 
conditions for road pavements. However, research on CBs in asphalt concrete is limited, 
therefore further experimental work, including a leachate analysis and life cycle assessment, is 
essential.  

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===
 
32 
 
• Extracting the cellulose fibers from the cellulose acetate for use in the pulp and paper industry 
offers a sustainable solution to deforestation. However, the process consequently leads to the 
production of wastewater containing toxic chemicals from the cellulose acetate. Despite the 
inconclusive nature of the results, the extracted cellulose pulp from the CBs can decrease the 
harvesting of trees for paper manufacturing. Moreover, the porous nature of CBs allows it to 
be a perfect absorbing material with exceptional acoustical performance. Which can be used as 
a substitute for artificial based commercial products. However, the literature reviewed requires 
clarification on the pre-treatment process of the CBs due to the highly toxic nature of the waste.  
• The cellulose acetate in the CB is an important target for CB recycling . A vast majority of 
researches has utilized the porous nature of the waste for water and wastewater treatment 
applications. Including the fabrication of superhyrophobic fibers for clean-up of water/oil spills; 
biofilm carrier for the removal of phosphate and organic matter from wastewater; cellulose 
nanocrystals and nanofibres for the removal of diclofenac from water; active carbon for oil -
water separation, and charred carbon for the removal of lead from aqueous solution. Although 
there is various equipment available for wastewater treatments, the utilization of CBs offers an 
innovative and sustainable approach. It is important all components of the CB is considered 
and not discarded as waste or require further management.   
• Porous carbon derived from CBs a re of high surface area with excellent electron conductivity 
and can be used for supercapacitors and electronic applications. Porous carbon can be used in 
several applications, however, is considered expensive and consists of sophisticated techniques. 
Therefore recycling CBs to produce porous carbon is a practical technique, however, the 
research conducted requires further experimentation and clarification. The methods adopted 
fail to recycle the whole CB, but rather attempt to make use of only one component of the waste 
and discard the remaining. 
• The nicotine content trapped in the CB can be extracted to be used as an effective corrosion 
inhibitor for metals. However, applying the extract on metal raises environmental concerns for 
the possible leachate of t oxic chemicals. Similarly, utilizing CBs grounded on its pesticidal, 
fungicidal, insecticidal, and herbicidal properties for vector control may result in the release of 
pollutants during the recycling process.  
In conclusion, valorization of CBs may be an environmentally friendly way to manage a continuously 
growing pollution calamity. In some cases, it even implies an enhancement to the modified material. 
The aim of this review was to systematically evaluate the toxic nature of CBs, comprehensively review 
all major research studies on recycling CB waste, and investigate the feasibility and sustainability of 

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
 
33 
 
the recycling methods adopted. Based on the review, the following research and developments are 
recommended:  
• A life cycle assessment to evaluate the positive and negative environmental impacts of the 
proposed CB recycling procedures from the collection of the waste through to the production 
process and, finally, the packaging and storage.  
• The preparation and implementation of new policies for different recycling options through the 
affiliation of government agencies, the environmental protection authority (EPA), and waste 
recycling industries. In relation to the collection, safety, and storage of CBs; processing 
procedures of the various applications; toxicity and leachability; long term conditions and 
disposal. 
• The evaluation of employee exposure, transport, collection processes, and consumer exposures 
for all recycling schemes mentioned.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
 
34 
 
Chapter 3: Recycling of Cigarette Butts in 
Fired Clay Bricks: A New Laboratory 
Investigation 
Abstract  
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the most commonly littered waste material in the world. It is estimated that 
over 5.7 trillion cigarettes are consumed worldwide each year. Consequently, millions of tonnes of 
highly toxic waste are contaminating the environment. CBs are composed of cellulose acetate filters—
a polymer with poor biodegradability—and which, depending upon the environmental conditions, can 
take many years to decompose. In this study, fired clay bricks were manufactured with 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 
and 2% CBs by mass and tested against control bricks with 0% CBs. The results revealed a decrease in 
compressive strength from 48.6 MPa for 0% CB content bricks to 30.8 MPa for 1% CB content bricks, 
and a decrease in dry density with the increase in CB content, from 2114 kg m -3 for the control bricks 
to 1983 kg m-3 and 1969 kg m-3 for 1% and 2% CB content bricks. The highest value of water absorption 
appeared for 2% CB content bricks, which reached an absorption rate of 13.1% compared to 9% for the 
control bricks. The energy required during the firing process was calculated with a saving of up to 
10.20%, for bricks incorporating 1% CBs. The thermal conductivity of the samples showed a reduction 
of 17% from 1.078 to 0.898 W m −1·K−1 with the addition of 1% CBs. In addition, the manufactured 
bricks were tested for efflorescence, an initial rate of absorption (IRA), microstructural analysis, and 
shrinkage. A life-cycle assessment (LCA) is recommended to analyze the environmental impacts of 
bricks incorporating CBs. 
The research presented in this chapter has been published in the following: 
Kurmus, H. and Mohajerani, A., 2020. Recycling of cigarette butts in fired clay bricks: A new 
laboratory investigation. Materials, 13, 790. https://doi.org/10.3390/ma13030790. 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 53 ===
 
35 
 
3.1 Introduction 
Due to the increasing requirements of environmentally-friendly, lightweight, and low-cost construction 
materials, many studies have been devoted to finding ways to optimize the performance of the materials 
used in the building industry, such that the effect on the environment is minimized [1,2]. Ultimately, 
one-third of the world’s energy consumption is consumed in buildings [3], while 50% of this is lost due 
to the poor thermal conductivity of the walls [4]. Fired clay bricks are the most conventionally used 
masonry material around the globe [5] . Their interesting physical, thermal, and mechanical properties 
allow them to be the ultimate material for the construction of buildings, especially their durability , 
compactness, and strength [6]. 
Buildings consume great amounts of thermal energy. During the winter, the internal warmth generated 
by heating is lost due to the high thermal conductivity of traditional bricks, while, in the summer, the 
coolness can escape due to the same reasoning. Therefore, it is essential to develop a method to produce 
thermally-resistant walls, while remaining in the realms of practicality and maintaining the aesthetic 
appeal of the construction. Hence, enhancing the insulation effect iveness of construction material will 
significantly lower the impact on the environment.  
Many studies have been conducted to find ways to improve the thermal conductivity of fired clay bricks, 
with one being the recycling of waste materials composed of high organic content. It was found that the 
greater the energy consumption during the firing process, the greater the thermal conductivity of the 
bricks [7,8]. Therefore, the incorporation of additive materials with high organic content may create 
voids within the structure during the firing process and reduce energy consumption [9]. Wastes have 
higher heating values (HHV), which are added through self -combustion within the clay mixture, and, 
hence, less energy is required during the firing process [10]. In doing so, the porosity will increase due 
to the creation of pores, and the thermal conductivity and density of the fired clay bricks will decrease 
[11,12]. However, these modifications to the microstructure and composition of the brick will have a 
negative impact on the mechanical resistance of the material.  
The correlation between density and thermal conductivity is evident [9] . However, it is conceivable to 
discover situations where there is a variation between thermal conductivity and density [7,13] . The 
sources of these variations may be the nature and distribution of the pores or the presence of certain 
mineralogical components [13,14]. A study was completed on 29 samples of clay bricks in which the 
thermal conductivity was determined. Furthermore, the relationship with their physical, microstructural, 
and compositional features was evaluated. It was found that mineralogical components, like Ca-rich 
silicates, quartz, and amorphous phase, play a significant role in decreasing the insulation properties of 
fired clay bricks, while open pores have a positive effect on thermal conductivity [13]. 

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
 
36 
 
A number of studies have been conducted on recycling wastes in fired clay bricks to investigate the 
relationship between the use of additives in fired clay bricks and the micro-pores and nano-pores formed 
as a result. Such additives include waste sawdust [15], desulfurization slag and basic oxygen furnace 
aggregates [16], marble powder [17], biomass incineration [18], recycled paper processing residues [4], 
bio-briquette ash [19], sugarcane bagasse ash [20], and cigarette butts [21–27]. A recent study revealed 
the substructures produced by the additive olive pomace (10% weight) that resulted in a 14.4% decrease 
in thermal conductivity [28]. Comparing this to expanded ve rmiculite (10% content), a pore-forming 
material that expands when heated declined from 0.96 to 0.65 W mK-1, which is a 32% drop in thermal 
conductivity [3]. While olive mill solid residue (OMSD) demonstrated the highest decrease in thermal 
conductivity of 70% with the use of 15% OMSD in fired clay bricks [29]. 
3.1.1 Cigarette Butts 
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the most commonly littered waste material in the world. The Tobacco Atlas 
[30] reported that 5.7 trillion cigarettes were consumed worldwide in the year 2016 and that about 97% 
of the cigarette filters were made up of cellulose acetate filters, which is a modified natural polymer 
[31]. In Australia, every year, more than 20 billion cigarettes are consumed, and 7 billion are littered 
into the environment (Fig. 3.1) [32]. Clean Up Australia [33] reported that CBs were the most common 
source of rubbish collected under the miscellaneous category, which represented 91.5%. 
 
Fig. 3.1. Littered CBs. 
When CBs are littered, harmful chemicals are leached into the environment, which poseleading 
concerns for the global environment and the quality of urban and aquatic life. In cigarette smoke, it is 
possible to classify more than 4000 chemical components that are generated during burning or that are 
distilled from tobacco, of which 69 are carcinogenic [34,35]. Cigarettes generally incorporate cellulose 
acetate filters, which are designed to fully or partially retain particulate smoke components, in cluding 
tar and toxic chemicals [36]. Cellulose acetate filters have poor biodegradability and can take up to 18 


=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
 
37 
 
months to decompose under normal environmental conditions, and, hence, pose a severe dilemma in 
terms of toxic waste and litter disposal [37,38]. CBs littered on streets are commonly washed away into 
stormwater drains and end up on beaches, or in rivers and harbors where they leach toxic chemicals 
[39]. 
Landfilling and incineration are possible disposal methods for CBs. However, both methods are costly 
and universally unsustainable. The incineration of CBs can result in the emission of various hazardous 
substances, and landfilling of CBs will continue to be an environmental hazard regardless of the location 
of the landfill and can be severely detrimental to the environment and human health [40,41]. Therefore, 
it is becoming vital to find a practical and effective way to recycle CB waste. An alternative to the 
growing pollution catastrophe may be the method of recycling CBs into composite building m aterials 
such as fired clay bricks. 
Mohajerani et al. [27]  propose that all CBs can be sustainably recycled in fired clay bricks by 
incorporating 1% CBs in 2.5% of the world’s brick production. The study involves incorporating 
various percentages of CBs in clay bricks, including 2.5%, 5%, 7.5%, and 10% [23,24,27]. Experiments 
have confirmed a steady decrease in dry density and compressive strength with an increase in the CB 
content of the clay bricks. This decrease in density increases the porosity, which results in an 
exponential drop in thermal conductivity. Thus, this results in improved thermal performance and 
greater energy efficiency [26]. The lower energy consumption during the firing process is contributed 
through the high calorific value of the CBs.  CBs have higher heating values (HHV), which are added 
through self-combustion within the clay mixture, and, hence, less energy is required during the firing 
process [10]. Moreover, the high temperatures during the firing stage will result in the volatilization of 
dangerous components and eliminate any toxic compounds [27]. 
This paper is the continuation of an ongoing study on the incorporation of CBs in fired bricks. In this 
study, the properties of 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB bricks by mass (equivalent to a pproximately 10 
kg m-3, 20 kg m-3, 30 kg m-3, and 40 kg m-3 CBs) were investigated and compared with controlled fired 
clay bricks (0 kg/m 3 CBs). The energy saving percentage during the firing process of bricks and the 
reduction in thermal conductivity are presented as a function of the CBs present in the mixtures. 
Additionally, numerous tests were carried out, including water absorption, compressive strength, 
efflorescence, an initial rate of absorption (IRA), density, shrinkage, and microstructural analysis. 
3.2 Materials and Methods 
3.2.1 Materials 
The soil used in preparing the fired clay bricks was sandy silty clay (MC), which was provided by PHG 
Bricks Victoria (Victoria, Australia). Geotechnical laboratory tests were conducted on the soil, 

=== PDF PAGE 56 ===
 
38 
 
including the particle size distribution test (PSD), liquid limit, and plastic limit, according to the 
Australian Standards [42–44]. 
CBs of varying sizes and brands were provided by Buttout Austr alia Pty Ltd. (Melbourne, Australia), 
which were collected from dry receptacles. On arrival, the CBs were dried and sanitized at 105 °C for 
24 hr in the oven (Fig. 3.2), and then stored in airtight plastic bags. 
Chemical and mineral analyses were conducted  to determine the major chemical and mineral 
constituents of the experimental soil using X-ray fluorescence (XRF) (S4 Pioneer, Bruker, MA, USA) 
and an X -ray diffraction analyzer (XRD) (D8 Endeavor, Bruker, MA, USA). Thermo -gravimetric 
analysis (TGA) was carried out using the Perkin-Elmer TGA 8000 instrument (Waltham, MA, USA) to 
investigate the weight loss of the 0% and 1% CB content clay mixtures.  
 
Fig. 3.2. CBs used in this study. 
The geotechnical properties of the soil used in this study were determined according to the Australian 
Standards [43,44]. By testing the Atterberg limits, it was found that the liquid limit for the brick soil 
was 34%, while the plastic limit was 25%, respectively. These values are the average results from three 
replicate tests. 
The particle size distribution for the soil sample was determined through sieve analysis and is shown in 
Fig. 3.3. The brick soil comprised 77.5% fine particles (<75 µm). According to these results, the Unified 
soil classification of the brick soils is sandy silty clay (MC) [42]. 


=== PDF PAGE 57 ===
 
39 
 
 
Fig. 3.3. Particle-size distribution curve of brick soil used in this study. 
Chemical analyses were conducted to obtain the chemical composition of the brick soil using XRF. As 
shown in Table 3. 1, the main compound found in the soil was silicon dioxide, which is followed by 
aluminium oxide and ferric oxide. XRD was utilized to identify the main crystalline phases on a <75 -
µm sample of the brick soil (Fig. 3.4). The brick soil displayed Quartz (SiO2) as the primary crystalline 
phase while Muscovite (KAI 2(AISi3O10)(F,OH)2), Clinochlore (Mg 5AI(AlSi3O10)(OH)8), Mikasaite 
(Fe2(SO4)3), and Albite (Na(AlSi3O8) presented a lower intensity.

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===

Fig. 3.4. XRD patterns of brick soil. 
TGA was performed to monitor and compare the weight loss of the 0% and 1% CB content clay 
mixtures while the samples were exposed to a constant rate of heat. The samples were heated from 30 
°C to 850 °C at 20 °C min-1 in an air atmosphere with a purge rate of 15 mL min-1. Fig. 3.5 presents the 
thermogravimetric (TG) and differential thermal (DT) curves for 0% and 1% CB content clay mixtures, 
respectively. The 0% and 1% CB content clay mixtures demonstrated mass loss characteristics of 6.1% 
(0% CB content clay mixture) and 7.3% (1% CB content clay mixture). T he initial decrease occurred 
at 150 °C. This is due to the evaporation of water within the mixture. While a majority of the mass loss 
occurred at 150– 400 °C and 500– 600 °C, during these ranges, organic matter is burned and water 
molecules of the chemical compounds are released. The final mass loss occurred between 600–800 °C. 
This can be related to the decomposition of carbonates and sulphides. The 1.2% difference in mass loss 
between the two samples could be due to the high volatile organic content present in the 1% CB content 
clay mixture that burnt off during the high temperatures. 


=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
 
41 
 
 
Fig. 3.5. TG and DTA curves of 0% CB content clay mixture and 1% CB content clay mixture. 
3.2.2 Brick Sample Preparation 
The bricks were prepared incorporating varying percentages of cigarette butts and a control sample with 
0% CBs to investigate the mechanical and physical properties of the bricks. The mixes were categorized 
by the percentage of CBs by the mass of the brick. CB (0.0) was the control sample and consisted of no 
CBs. CB (0.5) consisted of 0.5% CBs by mass. CB (1.0) consisted of 1% CBs by mass, CB (1.5) 
consisted of 1.5% CBs by mass, and CB (2.0) consisted of 2% CBs by mass.  
The soil and CB mixtures were prepared using a Hobart mechanical mixer for a duration of 25 min with 
a 15.5% water content. Then it was placed in airtight plastic bags for 48 h to uniformly distribute the 
moisture throughout the mix. The mixtures were then compacted in molds of a 100-mm diameter to 
yield bricks with heights of approximately 50 mm with a compaction pressure of 240 kP a. The 
specimens were air dried for 24 h before being placed in an oven for 24 h at 105 °C. Once removed 
from the oven, the samples were then fired in an electric furnace at 1050 °C for 3 h. During each stage 
of the preparation process, the height, diameter, and  weight of the samples were measured using an 
electric scale and digital caliper.  The fired samples were tested for water  absorption, compressive 
strength, efflorescence, initial rate of absorption (IRA), density, shrinkage, thermal conductivity, 
microstructural analysis, and energy savings, according to the Australian Standards [45] (Fig. 3.6). All 
the results presented are the average of three replicates.

3.2.3 Energy Saving 
The calorific value of a material determines the amount of heat (energy) that is produced by the 
complete combustion of that material. Therefore, utilizing a material with a high calorific value as a 
partial replacement material in a mixture with a low cal orific value will result in a reduction in the 
overall required energy for bricks, since additional energy is produced by the constituents of the material 
when combusted, as compared to a standard mixture.  
In the calculation method [27] , the firing energy  for the fired clay bricks incorporating CBs was 
calculated, assuming that 2 MJ kg−1 energy was used for firing the bricks. This value was based on a 
survey conducted in 1993–1994, which shows that the firing energy consumption is in a range of 2 to 
3 MJ kg−1 in Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) countries [46]. A laboratory experiment 
was conducted to determine the calorific value of the CBs. Samples of used CBs were tested, and the 
calorific value for the CBs was found to be 16.53 MJ kg-1.  
The calculations were conducted for the CB bricks by considering the weight of CBs incorporated as 
well as the calorific value of the cellulose acetate. Therefore, the estimated firing energy saved through 
the addition of CBs into bricks and compared to the control brick was calculated as follows. 
Energy Used for Control Clay Brick (MJ) Q1 = q × m1 (3.1) 
Energy Used for Clay—B Brick (MJ) Q2 = q × m2 – CV × m3 (3.2) 
Energy Saved (MJ) Q1 – Q2 = (q × m1) – (q × m2 – CV × m3) (3.3) 
Energy Saved (%) 
                                                                                             
∆𝐸𝐸(%) = 𝑄𝑄1−𝑄𝑄2
𝑄𝑄1
× 100%   (3.4)

Where:

m1 = mass of clay in control clay brick (kg) 
m2 = mass of clay in clay-CB brick (kg) 
m3 = mass of CBs in clay-CB brick (kg) 
CV = calorific value of cellulose acetate = 16.53 MJ kg-1 
3.2.4 Thermal Conductivity 
Due to the increasing requirements for energy savings, the t hermal insulation properties of building 
materials, such as bricks, have become significant. The heat loss from a building depends on the thermal 
conductivity of the materials in the roof and wall. While the thermal conductivity of a brick is the 
quantity of heat transmitted through the brick. Components that influence the effective thermal 
conductivity of bricks are generally the geometrical design [47] , microstructure, and mineralogical 
composition [48].  
As part of these studies, Kadir and Mohajerani [22]  developed E q. (3. 5) to estimate the thermal 
conductivity of brick samples. The thermal conductivity of 256 results of various types of concrete, 
aggregate, and bricks was obtained to develop an equation using regression analysis as a function of 
density. Whereby, it was found that K (thermal conductivity) can be represented using its relationship 
with Dry Density. 
K = 0.0559e(0.0014)(Dd1) (3.5)

samples. According to Fourier’s Law, we find that the Heat Flow Rate (W) can be expressed as:

3.3 Results and Discussion 
3.3.1 Properties of the Fired Clay Bricks 
Property tests were conducted for five types of the sample: 0% CBs, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs. The 
results can be seen in Fig s. 3.7 – 3.12. The dry density of the prepared bricks decreased from 2114 kg 
m-3 for controlled bricks to 1983 kg m-3 for bricks with 1% CBs by mass. The addition of CBs resulted 
in the bricks becoming more porous. The development of pores within the structure of the brick can be 
associated with the added CBs that burnt off during the  firing process as a consequence of the volatile 
organic content in the CBs.

The compressive strength of the bricks tested decreased from 48.6 MPa for 0% CB content to 30.8 MPa 
for 1% CB content, and 27.2 M Pa for 2% CB content. The minimum recommended compressive 
strength is 3–5 MPa, and 5–10 MPa for non-load and load-bearing fired clay bricks, respectively [49 –
51]. The reduction in strength was associated with the l oss of organic content in the CBs, which were 
exposed to firing in the furnace. This resulted in the formation of bigger pores due to the CBs that burnt 
off, which lowered the strength of the CB-amended fired clay bricks. Similar results have been observed 
with the incorporation of hemp shiv fibers in fly ash-based alkaline mortars. The addition of hemp fibers 
led to a reduction in compressive strength due to the increase in porosity [52]. 
The higher percentage of CB content resulted in an increase in water  absorption and IRA. The water 
absorption was measured at 12.1% for 1% CBs by mass compared to 9% for 0% CBs by mass (Fi g. 
3.13). The greatest value of water absorption was measured at 13.1% for 2% CBs by mass. The IRA

was found to be 0.67 kg  m−2 min−1 for 1% CBs by mass and 0.44 kg m −2 min−1 for 0% CBs by mass, 
which falls within the IRA range of 0.2 and 5 kg m−2 min−1 [53]. A similar trend was observed with the 
incorporation of biosolids in fired clay bricks. The increase in the percentage of biosolids resulted in an 
increase in IRA due to the creation of pores during the firing process [54]. Water absorption and IRA 
values signify the long-term durability performance of the bricks. Therefore, excessively high values 
can lead to cracking as a consequence of volume changes. 
 
Fig. 3.13. 24-h cold water and 5-h boiling water test. 
Drying and firing shrinkage are essential indicators for evaluating the suitability of the added CBs from 
a durability point of view. The bricks are exposed to high temperatures  in the furnace. Therefore, any 
cracks that occur during drying can result in brick failure during firing. The results revealed a decreasing 
trend in height and diametric shrinkage with the increase in CB content. The diametric shrinkage values 
obtained were 4.64%, 3.45%, and 3.1% for 0%, 1%, and 2% CBs by mass bricks, respectively. The 
decreasing trend may be the consequence of the volatile content present in the CBs that burnt off, which 
results in a change in size. A similar trend in the variation of shr inkage in fired clay bricks with the 
increase in waste glass has been reported by Phonphuak et al. [55]. 
Analysis of efflorescence was carried out for the clay bricks and the clay -CB bricks. Efflorescence is 
the formation of salt deposits on or near the surface of the brick that causes a change in appearance [56]. 
For comparison purposes, 10 samples were prepared and sorted into two pairs so that both specimens 
of each pair had the same appearance. All external faces of each pair of specimens were examined (Fig. 
3.14). The results for the potential efflorescence of the control, 0.5%, and 1.5% CB bricks displayed 
“Nil” observable efflorescence, while there was “Slight” efflorescence for 1%, and “Moderate” 
efflorescence for the 2% CB bricks, according to the Australian Standards [57].

3.3.2 Energy Savings 
Using Eq. (3.4), the estimated amounts of energy saved during the firing process of 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 
and 2% CBs by mass bricks were calculated and are given in Table 3.2 and Fig. 3.15. The results show 
that, by firing bricks with 1% CBs by mass, there could be a possible 10.20% energy saving when 
compared to the control bricks. Furthermore, a 14.71% energy saving can be achieved with an increase 
in the CB content (2% CB bricks). This is due to the waste having a higher heating value, which is 
added through self-combustion within the clay mixture during the firing process [9].

Lower thermal conductivity is beneficial, as it will decrease the energy consumed for cooling and 
heating.

In Table 3. 4 it can be seen that the increase in CB content corresponds to the reduction in thermal 
conductivity, and, consequently, a reduction in the energy transfer between the fired clay brick walls. 
An energy savings of 16.7% was achieved for a brick wall with 1% CB content compared to a 0% CB 
content wall. This reduction in energy transfer, and, therefore, the energy savings will decrease the costs 
of heating and cooling. A life -cycle assessment (LCA) is recommended to analyze the environmental 
impacts of fired clay bricks by incorporating CBs [58]. 
3.4 Conclusions 
This study investigated the properties of 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB bricks by mass and compared 
them with those of standard fired clay bricks. Numerous tests were conducted, including water 
absorption, efflorescence, initial rate of absorption (IRA), density, shrinkage, microstructural analyses, 
thermal conductivity, and energy savings based on the Australian Standards. 
The compressive strength of the bricks tested decreased from 48.6 MPa for 0% CB content to 30.8 MPa 
for 1% CB mass. The lowest compressive strength value was obtained for the 2% CB content bricks 
(27.2 MPa). The minimum recommended compressive strength is 3– 5 MPa, and 5– 10 MPa for non-
load and load-bearing fired clay bricks, respectively. 
However, the dry density decreased with the increase in CB content from 2114 kg m-3 for control bricks 
to 1983 kg m-3 and 1969 kg m-3 for 1% and 2% CB content bricks, which produces light-weight bricks. 
The advantages of light-weight construction material are cheaper to produce and have a lower overall 
life cycle energy usage and design flexibility. The diametric shrinkage values obtained were 4.64% and 


=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
 
52 
 
3.45% for the 0% and 1% CBs by mass bricks, respectively, which rises fairly linearly as the CB content 
increased. 
The highest value of water absorption occurred for the 2% CB content bricks, which reached an 
absorption rate of 13.1% compared to 9% for the control bricks. The initial rate of absorption was found 
to be 0.67 kg m−2 min−1 for 1% CBs by mass bricks and 0.44 kg m −2 min−1 for the control bricks. The 
increase in CB content resulted in the increase in pores both in size and occurrence, which increased 
the water absorption rate of the bricks.  
The thermal conductivity was estimated by using a prediction model. The estimations showed that an 
increase in CB content could result in a significant decrease in the thermal conductivity, an d, 
consequently, result in lower energy costs for cooling and heating.  
The estimated amounts of energy saved during the firing process of 0.5%, 1.5%, 1% CB, and 2% CB 
content bricks were found using calculations. Approximate savings of 10.20% and 14.71% w ere seen 
for 1% and 2% CB content bricks, respectively. 
Consequently, bricks incorporating CBs have the potential to provide substantial energy savings and a 
unique, sustainable recycling solution to a growing global waste catastrophe. However, it is essential to 
adhere to international regulations and standards regarding the handling and recycling of toxic waste 
materials.  
The results found in this study complete and confirm the previous findings on recycling CBs in fired 
clay bricks at RMIT University and the published proposal that all CBs can be sustainably recycled in 
fired clay bricks by incorporating 1% CBs in 2.5% of the world’s brick production [27]. 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
 
53 
 
Chapter 4: Energy Savings, Thermal 
Conductivity, Micro and Macro Structural 
Analysis of Fired Clay Bricks 
Incorporating Cigarette Butts 
Abstract  
In this study, lightweight structural bricks incorporating various percentages of cigarette butts (CBs) 
are produced to investigate the thermal properties, energy savings, porosity and micro- and macro -
structure. A novel method was developed to monitor the energy consumption of the furnace during the 
firing process, and Scanning Electron Microscopy (SEM) and X -ray micro -Computed Tomography 
(micro-CT) imaging were used to characterize the quantity, distribution, size, and shape of the micro - 
and macro-scale pores of the brick samples incorporating CBs. The energy savings of the microporous 
bricks with 1% CBs by weight (wt.) varied between 8% (theoretical value) and 10.2% (measured value). 
Clay bricks showed an increase in porosity and a decrease in density with the increase in CB content, 
which reduced thermal conductivity. Thermal conductivity was measured to reduce from 0.463 to 0.347 
W m-1 K-1 with the addition of 2% CBs by wt. compared to the control bricks. The 3D models of the 
bricks incorporating various percentages of CBs demonstrated a homogenous distribution of pores 
within the brick structures. On a micrometer scale, the size of the micro- and macro- pores varied within 
a range of 0.035 to 1.365 mm. 
The research presented in this chapter has been published in the following: 
Kurmus, H. and Mohajerani, A. 2021. Energy Savings, Thermal Conductivity, Micro and Macro 
Structural Analysis of Fired Clay Bricks Incorporating Cigarette Butts . Construction and Building 
Materials, 283. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.conbuildmat.2021.122755. 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
 
54 
 
4.1 Introduction  
The demand for bricks is growing, particularly in economically developing countries, where 
infrastructure development projects are expanding. Establishing energy -efficient buildings is the 
method of improving the efficiency of buildings through the sustainable use of resources. The 
construction industry alone consumes between 30 to 40% of the total energy available in the world, 
while 41% of the total energy consumed in the construction industry is employed for heating and 
cooling [107, 108]. Although the production of bricks is expanding, the technology used in firing bricks 
remains the same, whereby energy is consumed inefficiently. In general, 1 MJ kg-1 of energy is required 
to transform a clay mixture into a brick [109]. However, the specific firing energy consumption ranges 
from 2 to 3 MJ kg -1, which is roughly 25 to 35% of the total costs associated with producing bricks 
[110]. Therefore, producing energy -efficient residential blocks can potentially decrease the usage of 
energy, lower costs, and offer sustainable alternatives to boost energy conservation  [111, 112]. 
To reduce the energy demand in the building sector, and, consequently, the costs associated with energy 
usage, it is essential to manufacture lightweight, low -cost, and environmentally friendly building 
materials. Producing high insulation capacity bricks can potentially diminish the building’s energy 
consumption. According to the European standard EN832, walls should be pr oduced from materials 
with a heat transfer coefficient of approximately 0.4 to 0.7 W m -2 K-1 [113]. The standard brick with a 
density of 2400 kg m-3 is equivalent to a thermal conductivity value of 1.4 W m-1 K-1 [114]. The thermal 
conductivity of a brick is mai nly based on the density; therefore, increasing the thermal transmittance 
implies producing high porosity bricks, which can be achieved with the addition of waste material. 
Organic wastes contribute to auto-thermal combustion due to their high calorific va lue. Therefore, the 
energy consumed during the firing of bricks incorporating wastes will essentially be lower. As such, 
there is a growing interest among researchers to investigate potential waste products to incorporate in 
the production of fired clay bricks to improve their thermal properties and attain energy savings [115-
119]. Some of the organic wastes that have been studied include glass powder and palm oil fly ash 
[120], iron tailings [121] , biosolids [122, 123], waste marble sludge [124] , and cigarette butts (CBs) 
[1]. 
Each year, globally, over 1.2 million tons of toxic CB waste is being littered into the environment, 
which, potentially, can take up to 10 years to decom pose under normal environmental conditions [4].  
Littered CBs consist of arsenic, tar, CO, nicotine, and many other hazardous chemicals [125] . The 
release of toxic chemicals and heavy metals to the environment results in the contamination of plants, 
animals, water bodies, and soil [51, 52]. Being a major environmental issue, this has led researchers to 
explore feasible and practical recycling techniques. The incorporation of CBs in construction materials, 

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===
 
55 
 
particularly in fired clay bricks [1] and asphalt concrete [19-21], has gained pervasive interest. 
4.1.1 Recycling Cigarette Butts in Fired Clay Bricks 
In 2016, after extensive research, Mohajerani et al. [1]  proposed that by incorporating 1% CBs into 
2.5% of the bricks produced worldwide, the CB dilemma could conceivably be eradicated [1, 22- 24]. 
With the addition of 2.5% CBs in fired clay bricks, density decreased by 8.3%, while bricks 
incorporating 1% CBs, the compressive strength was determined to be 19.53 MPa. All the mechanical 
and physical property values were within the acceptable range of the Australian Standards [1]. 
Through the addition of CBs, porous bricks were produced, as shown in Fig. 4.1, and a drastic decrease 
in thermal conductivity was observed with the increase in CB content [1]. Fired clay bricks 
incorporating 1% CBs resulted in an energy saving of 9%. For the energy savings, the specific firing 
energy was assumed to be 2 MJ kg -1, and the calorific value of the cellulose acetate was presumed to 
be 19 MJ kg-1 for the calculation method. Therefore, it should be mentioned, that changes in the assumed 
values (specifically the firing energy and calorific value) will lead to variations in the energy savings 
for bricks incorporating CBs.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 4.1. ESEM images of FCBs with 0%, 2.5%, 5%, and 10% CBs (adapted from Mohajerani et al. [1]).   
The implemen tation of recycling CBs in bricks on an industrial scale was explored extensively by 
Mohajerani et al. [2] . The paper suggested the use of naphthalene containing mothballs during the 
0% 2.5% 
5% 10% 

=== PDF PAGE 74 ===
 
56 
 
collection and storing phase of CBs to reduce the bacteria content. While, to eliminate the odor from 
Volatile Organic Compound (VOC) emissions in CBs, the use of UV light was recommended. The 
paper further discussed CB collection systems, energy savings, properties of bricks incorporating CBs, 
and an implementation procedure (Fig. 4.2). All the results confirmed that the addition of 1% CBs in 
2.5% of the brick production is adequate to free the world from CBs [2].   
 
Fig. 4.2. Green and fired clay bricks (adapted from Mohajerani et al. [2]). 
In 2020, Kurmus and Mohajerani [126]  conducted a comprehensive leachate analysis of heavy metals 
for used, unused, and fired CBs, and unfired and fired clay bricks incorporating various percentages of 
CBs. The leaching results were compared to the EPA Victoria solid industrial waste guidelines and 
were found to be below the regulatory limits, and, therefore, were categorized as non-hazardous waste 
[126].  
This paper is a continuation study on recycling CBs in fired clay bricks. The objecti ve of this study is 
to investigate and analyse the effects of incorporating CBs on energy savings, porosity, thermal 
conductivity, and the micro- and macro-structure of fired clay bricks. Scanning Electron Microscopy 
(SEM) and X-ray micro -Computed Tomography (micro-CT) was used to quantify the porosity and 
characterize the size, shape, and distribution of the pores within the brick structure. The energy 
consumption of the furnace during the firing process of the clay brick specimens was monitored via the 
development of a novel method. The utilization of CBs in the production of clay bricks can be valuable 
in enhancing the thermal insulation properties of construction buildings, reduce the energy consumption 
of conventional furnaces while lowering costs and p romoting eco-friendly and sustainable building 
materials. In this study, clay bricks incorporating various percentages of CBs (0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 
and 2% by wt.) are investigated. 


=== PDF PAGE 75 ===
 
57 
 
4.2 Materials and Methods  
4.2.1 Preparation of brick samples 
For brick manufacturing, CBs and clay (soil) were used as the raw materials. The raw clay material was 
obtained by PGH Bricks and Pavers (VIC, Australia), and the CBs were provided by Butt Out Australia 
Pty Ltd. But Out is a CB collection company in Melbourne that colle cts CBs from dry receptacles that 
are installed in outdoor areas. The CBs and soil were oven dried for 24 h at 105 ºC before being 
transferred into plastic bags. Various tests on the soil and brick specimens were performed to determine 
their fundamental pr operties. The clay powder was characterized by mineralogical and chemical 
analysis using the X-ray diffraction pattern (XRD) (D8 Endeavor, Bruker, MA, USA) and X-ray 
fluorescence (XRF) (S4Pioneer, Bruker, MA, USA). Particle size distribution was determined  using 
sieve analysis according to the Australian Standard [127] . The washing method procedure was 
followed, which involved mixing the clay material with an antifocculation solution (sodium 
hexametaphosphate) in a large tray. The mixture was then washed on a 75 μm sieve, the sample retained 
on the 75 μm sieve was dried, and the mass was determined. The dry sample was then passed through 
sieves from 2.36 mm to 150 μm, and the masses retained on each sieve were recorded.  
Selected combinations of soil containing 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by wt. were prepared. The 
raw mixtures were mixed and homogenized using a Hobart mechanical mixer for 25 min with a 15.5% 
water content. Cylindrical samples were prepared with the use of a compactor with a compaction 
pressure of 240 kpa and dried at 105 ºC for 24 h. After drying, the green brick samples were fired at 
1050 ºC for 3 h in the Thermoconcept electrical chamber furnace (Model KC 220/13, Hylec Controls 
Pty Ltd, NSW, Australia), which has a volume capacity of 0.216 m3. The ramp rate of the furnace was 
set to 0.7 °C/min, and when the temperature reached 1050 °C, it was held for 3h and then cooled down 
at a rate of 0.7 °C/min to a safe temperature. 
4.2.2 Energy Saving  
4.2.2.1 Calculation Method 
In order to determine the calorific value of CBs, a laboratory investigation was undertaken by a group 
of researchers for various CB sample types. Based on the study, an average value of 16.53 MJ kg-1 was 
determined to be the calorific value of CBs [2]. Partially replacing clay with a high calorific value 
material (CBs) during the brick preparation will allow the CBs to act as energy carriers in the brick 
firing phase due to the high percentage of organic and carbonaceous substances contained in the waste. 
Utilizing a calorific value of 16.53 MJ kg-1 and assuming that 2 MJ kg-1 energy is used for the firing of 
bricks, the energy-saving through the addition of CBs compared to the conventional brick making was 

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
 
58 
 
determined [110]. In the calculation method [1] , the calculations were conducted by considering the 
mass of CBs (m3) and clay (m2) in the clay-CB brick, mass of the controlled sample (m1), energy used 
for brick firing (q), and the calorific value of the cellulose acetate (CV).  
Energy Saved, ∆𝐸𝐸(%) = (q × 𝑚𝑚2) – (q × 𝑚𝑚2 – CV × 𝑚𝑚3)q × 𝑚𝑚1 × 100% 
4.2.2.2 Measurement Method 
Energy-saving tests were performed on 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by wt. samples to compare 
the energy usage by the furnace during firing. For the purpose of this investigation, the  environmental 
and internal conditions were intended to be kept the same for all mixtures, including the initial 
temperature within the furnace, the external temperature of the furnace, the time when the furnace was 
operated, moisture content of the mixture, operation of the ventilation system, and program setup of the 
furnace. Therefore, to manage all the conditions during the operation of each mixture, each firing 
session was reduced to the minimum temperature required for brick making to decrease the time needed 
for the firing process, while roughly two-thirds of the furnace capacity was filled with samples. It is 
known that silica changes its crystal form at 573 °C and is a prevalent component of the quartz 
component of the clay used for making bricks, consequently the minimum temperature range for brick 
firing should be between 600 and 700 °C [110]. Therefore, a ramp rate of 2 °C min -1 and a maximum 
temperature of 700 °C was selected for the furnace set up for each mixture. This particular furnace setup 
was only applied to the energy savings measurement method.  
The energy usage of each mixture during the firing process was evaluated by means of a Fluke 1736 
Three-Phase Power Energy Logger, which contains a power box and a magnetic sensor cla mp. The 
experimental set up to measure the energy consumption of the furnace can be seen in Fig. 4.3. The main 
plug is connected to the power box, which contains a magnetic sensor clamp fixed around a red cable 
to measure the current flowing to the furnace. The current is then transmitted to the energy logger, and 
the voltage is transmitted through a black cable that is connected to both the main cable and the logger. 
The captured voltage and current are then automatically converted to energy demand, and the energy 
consumption of the furnace is given for the period desired. All data are the mean of three replicate tests. 
(4.1) 

=== PDF PAGE 77 ===
 
59 
 
 
Fig. 4.3. Experimental setup used to measure energy consumption during the firing of bricks. 
4.2.3 Micro- and Macro-Structural Analysis 
The distribution and texture of pores in the FCBs incorporating CBs were analysed using SEM (Quanta 
200 SEM) and micro -CT (Bruker SkyScan 1275) imaging. Micro-CT provides data related to the 
connectivity and orientation of the pores within the structure. In addition, the micro-CT permits the 
visualization of the internal structure of the samples and will explain how the addition of CBs influences 
the physical-mechanical characteristics of fired clay bricks. Brick samples with a diameter of 2.5 cm 
and 5 cm i n height were placed in the micro-CT. The samples were scanned at 100 kV and 100 µA 
exposure, producing an axial sequence of X -ray attenuation imagery. A 1 mm copper filter was 
employed between the brick and tube to reduce the hardening of the artifacts and amplify the contrast 
between the sample phases. The subsequent 3D imagery consisted of a voxel size of 35 µm resolution, 
and data on internal porosity, pore distribution, and pore thickness were collected. 
4.2.4 Thermal Conductivity  
The addition of waste material in the production of fired clay bricks can drastically improve the thermal 
conductivity and resistance properties of the construction material, so, effectively, the building will not 
lose too much heat in the winter or gain too much heat in the summer [128]. When wastes are introduced 
into the clay brick mixture, during the firing phase, the organic components burn off, and micro- and 


=== PDF PAGE 78 ===
 
60 
 
macro-scale pores are created within the brick structure. Essentially the bricks become less dense, and 
the thermal conductivity value decreases [129].  
The determination of thermal conductivity of FCBs incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs 
by wt. using the Transient Line Source technique was carried out. The apparatus adopted in this study 
is the Transient Line Source (TLS) meter (TLS-100, Poly Technologies, Australia), which complies 
with standard ASTM D5334-14 [130]. The TLS-100 measures thermal conductivities between 0.1 to 5 
W m-1 K-1, with an accuracy of 5% and reproducibility of 2%. The line source method is based on a 
constant current source (q) being delivered to heat the sample, whereby the temperature rise is recorded 
over a defined period of time. The slope (a) from a plot of the logarithm of time versus temperature rise 
is used in the calculation of the thermal conductivity (k) and is described by Eq. (4.2): 
k = 
𝑞𝑞
4𝜋𝜋𝜋𝜋                      (4.2) 
A sensor needle (4 mm in diameter and 50 mm in length) comprising a thin heating wire and temperature 
sensor sealed in a steel tube (to protect the sensor) was completely inserted in the brick sample (Fig. 
4.4). A hole 4 mm in diameter was drilled into the brick sample using the drill bit provided to host the 
probe, and thermal contact grease was applied to the probe to minimise the sample/sensor contact 
resistance. The measurements were performed with an average temperature of 20 ± 2 °C and a 
measurement time of 5 min each. A time interval of 15 min for each test was adopted to allow for the 
thermal equilibration between the sample and the probe. Each sample was measured three times, and 
the mean of the three measurements was retained.

4.3 Results and Discussion  
4.3.1 Characterization of raw materials  
The chemical composition measured by XRF of the clay soil is provided in Fig. 4.5. According to the 
data, the soil comprises a significant amount of silica (SiO 2) (62.59%), followed by alumina (Al 2O3) 
(19.95%) and, to a reduced degree, iron (Fe 2O3) (6.15%) and potassium oxide (K 2O) (4.87%). The 
recommended SiO2 and Al2O3  content the clay should be composed of when used for brick making 
should be within the range of 20% to 50% and 10% to 20%, respectively [131] . The XRD patterns of 
the clay soil present the mineral phases (Fig. 4.6). The soil was mainly composed of Quartz (SiO2), but 
also comprised Muscovite (KAI 2(AISi3O10)(FOH)2), Albite (Na(AlSi 3O8), Clinochlore 
(Mg5AI(AlSi3O10)(OH)8), and Mikasaite (Fe2(SO4)3) minerals.

4.3.2 Energy Savings  
4.3.2.1 Calculation method 
Using Eq. (4.1), the estimated amounts of energy saved during the firing process of 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 
and 2% CBs by wt. bricks were determined and are presented in Fig. 4.9. The results indicate that by 
firing bricks with 1% and 2% CBs by wt., potential energy savings of 10.2% and 14.71% can be 
achieved in comparison to bricks containing no CBs. CBs have a higher heating value compared to clay. 
Therefore, the partial replacement of clay with CBs results in additional heat being released from the 
combustion of the waste during the firing process [132]. The higher the content of CBs, the greater the 
energy saved during firing. Similarly, Boldyrev [133]  observed 20% to 40% energy savings by 
recycling coal-mining waste in fired clay bricks, with the highest energy saving being 60%. The range 
of coal-mining content incorporated into the clay brick varied between 5% and 15% by mass.  
 
Fig. 4.9. Calculated energy-saving versus CB%. 
4.3.2.2 Measurement Method 
The calorific contribution provided by wastes during the firing process of fired clay bricks is regarded 
as a vital phenomenon in terms of energy conservation. CBs are composed of cellulose acetate 
(polymer) filters, and the melting point of cellulose acet ate ranges between 164 and 170 °C [134] . 
Therefore, once the furnace reaches a temperature between 164 and 170 °C, the CBs combust, and 
additional energy is available for the furnace [134]. As shown in Fig. 4.10, a relationship has been 
derived between the CB content of the brick mixture and the energy saved during the firing process. 
The total energy-used values were captured by the energy logger for each session and can be seen in 
Table 4.1.

The findings demonstrate that by firing bricks with 1% CBs by wt., an 8% energy saving can be 
achieved in comparison to the conventional brick (0% CBs) and a maximum 13.3% energy saving with 
the addition of 2% CBs by wt. This shows that  the percentage of energy savings is significantly 
contingent on the percentage of CBs present within the clay mixture. Similarly, Dondi et al. [13 5] 
reported a 15% energy saving during the firing process of fired clay bricks incorporating 4 to 5% 
sawdust by mass. The calorific value of the sawdust waste ranged between 7 and 19 MJ kg -1, which 
effectively contributed to the firing process. 
 
Fig. 4.10. Measured energy-saving versus CB%. 
Currently, the total production of bricks in Australia per year is 1.6 billion [136], and the average brick 
weighs 3.2 kg. Consequently, the total mass of bricks produced in Aust ralia per year can be estimated 
(5,120 million kg). Assuming the energy consumption for firing bricks is 2 MJ kg -1 [110], and the 
average electricity usage rate in Victoria is 23.272 c kWh -1 [137], the average cost of firing bricks in 
Australia is roughly $661.96 million per year. Based on the derived assumptions and c alculations, the 
estimated cost savings in the energy consumption of firing bricks incorporating CBs are computed in 
Table 4.1. As shown in Table 4.1, with an energy saving of 8%, an estimated $53.15 million can be 
saved during the firing process of bricks incorporating 1% CBs by wt. compared to controlled bricks.

The deviation between the theoretical value and actual measured value of the energy savings may be 
the cause of several factors, including the assumed specific firing energy value in the calculation 
method, the maximum temperature reached in the furnace during the firing process; moisture contents 
of the mixtures; program setup of the furnace, and the calorific value of the CBs. How ever, the major 
source of discrepancy is essentially the assumed specific firing energy value of 2 MJ kg -1. In reality, 
based on a survey performed in ASEAN countries during the years 1993 to 1994, the specific firing 
energy consumption spans from 2 to 3 MJ kg-1 [110]. In this investigation, a lower firing energy value 
was selected to achieve a higher energy saving. However, to investigate whether a change in the specific 
firing energy will result in a digression in energy savings, Fig. 4.11 was produced. 
 
Fig. 4.11. Calculated energy savings for brick specimens incorporating CBs for different specific firing energy 
values.

energy saved for all four brick-CB combinations. For firing energy values of 2, 2.5, and 3 MJ kg -1, an

energy saving of 10.2%, 8.58%, and 7.5% was achieved for bricks incorporating 1% CBs. A specific 
firing energy value between 2.5 and 3 MJ kg-1 is consistent with the actual measured value of 8% energy 
savings. Therefore, based on the calculation and measurement methods, we can conclude that for bricks 
incorporating 1% CBs by wt. a minimum energy saving of 8% is achievable.  
It is critical to mention the energy savings investigation is based in a laboratory environment, and when 
compared to an industrial scale, the results may vary due to many factors. The conventional kiln 
operation in a large scale brick production involves a tunnel kiln, where the loading, firing, an d 
unloading process is a continuous process and can operate over a range of months [2] . In comparison 
to a laboratory environment, bricks are fired in batches of 20 in an electric furnace for a duration of 2 
days. The heat loss and efficiency of both processes may vary, and therefore the energy savings results 
may differ. However, if we were to compare the firing of controlled bricks to bricks incorporating CBs 
on an industrial scale, we can assume both settings and conditions are similar, including the heat loss, 
energy consumption, efficiency, firing time, and brick quantity. The only variance in both operations is 
the amount of heat added to the firing process through the combustion of the CBs, which will potentially 
decrease the energy consumption during the firing process. Therefore, although we cannot assume a 
certain 8% of energy savings is achievable, we can confirm an energy savings of up to 8% can be 
achieved. However, further research on an industrial scale is recommended.  
4.3.3 Micro and Macro-Structural Analysis 
As discussed above, thermal conductivity is significantly affected by density and porosity. However, it 
is not only the porosity and density but the size, shape, and distribution of the pores that play an 
important role in thermal conductivity. This is supported by the heat transfer mechanism, which can be 
depressed or enhanced by the distinct mean path of air molecules entrapped in the pores, and, 
subsequently, by the diverse pore size [138].  Therefore, to investigate this phenomenon, micro-CT 
imaging was used to characterize the distribution, size, and shape of the micro - and macro-scale pores 
within the brick structure formed from the addition of CBs. 
A total of 1581 2D images of cylindrical brick samples of 2.5 cm in diameter and 5 cm in height with 
a 35 µm thickness was obtained. The 2D scanning images depict the size, shape, and abundance of the 
micropores and macropores at particular sections of the sample. Fig. 4. 12 represents 2D slices of 
random sections selected from the various brick samples in corporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% 
CBs by wt. with the thresholding operation. The thresholding operation is a manual interface, which 
has an inherent error margin based on the human perception of the edge of the void region [139] . The 
black regions represent pores, and the white and light grey regions represent coarse aggregates. As 

=== PDF PAGE 85 ===
 
67 
 
shown in Fig. 5.12 the pores vary in size, shape, and quantity with the various samples. An increase in 
pore frequency is observed with the increase in CB content, which is due to the creation of voids from 
the burnt CBs during the firing stage.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig 4.12. 2D CT scanning images at random positions of (a) 0%, (b) 0.5%, (c) 1%, (d) 1.5%, (e) 2% CBs by wt. 
bricks. 
To explore the 3D structural features of the pores, including the distribution, interconnectivity, and size, 
2D images were used in the 3D numerical simulation. (1) Pore distribution: layered micro- and macro-
pores are homogeneously distributed in a vertic al direction. (2) Pore interconnectivity: in most cases, 
the micropores are not connected with each other in an isolated state. However, those micropores 
distributed in strips connect with each other and the macropores to some extent. (3) Pore size: macro - 
and micropores have various sizes. Macropores are generally greater than 0.08 mm in diameter, and 
micropores are less than 0.08 mm in diameter. Micropores occur in isolated points and in strips [140].  
The determination of the structural separation resulted in a relative volume distribution over discrete 
size intervals. The relative volume is plotted against the midpoint of the class. To obtain better 
knowledge regarding the size and quantity of pores for each brick sample, Fig. 4 .13 was developed.  
The average pore size interval for bricks incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by wt., range 
from 0.035 – 0.455 mm, 0.035 – 1.015 mm, 0.035 – 1.085 mm, 0.035 – 1.365 mm, and 0.035 – 1.365

69 
 
compared to the bricks containing CBs. This is likely due to the air bubbles that were formed during 
the compaction process as a result of the air entrapped in the soil mixture (Fig. 4 .14). As sintering 
progresses, pores start to round and become smaller when compared to the solid -state and are 
completely isolated from the surface at the end of the sintering phase [141]. Sintering accelerates over 
950 °C because of the vitreous phase formations. This phase penetrates into the pores and closes them, 
and then separates them from the neighboring pores; this is why the pores are evenly distributed within 
the control brick [142] (Figs. 4.14 – 4.18).   
In contrast, in the bricks incorporating CBs, the CBs burn during the sintering process, and the pores 
are formed in the structure by CO 2 emissions [142]. Because the liquid phase will decrease with the 
reduction in clay quantity, the formed pores cannot be filled completely [141] . This results in the 
interconnection of micro- pores and the pores formed due to the burnt CBs, which produces macro-
pores. As shown in Figs. 4.15 – 4.18, the increase in CB content results in an increase in pores within 
the brick structure. The image on the left of each figure represents the whole brick sample with the pore 
structure and the image on the right exclusively represents the pore network.

4.3.4 Thermal Conductivity  
The recent trend in the brick manufacturing industry has revealed an increase in the use of combustible 
organic pore formers to improve the thermal insulation in fired clay bricks [143]. The bulk density is a 
key component that governs the overall thermal conductivity of solids. Images from micro- CT were 
used, and solids and voids were separated to obtain the porosity following the method described in the 
experimental method section. In the micro-CT analysis, the total porosity of the brick specimens was 
calculated as the volume of all pores as a percentage of the total volume. The attained porosity values 
were then analyzed to examine the relationship with the thermal conductivity values obtained from the 
TLS-100. The correlation between the thermal conductivity, density, and porosity of the brick samples 
incorporating various percentages of CBs is presented in Fig. 4.20. 
A lower thermal conductivity value is desirable as it represents a lower heat loss and better insulating 
properties. The obtained thermal conductivity and density values for control bricks (0% CB content) 
was 0.463 W m-1 K-1 and 2114 kg m-3 and reduced to 0.393 W m-1 K-1 and 1983 kg m-3 with the addition 
of 1% CBs by wt. (Fig. 4.20(a)). The thermal conductivity was further reduced to a value of 0.347 W 
m-1 K-1 with the addition of 2% CBs by wt. Aouba et al. [144]  similarly reported a decrease in the 
thermal conductivity from 0.51 W m-1 K-1 for the control specimens to 0.39 W m-1 K-1 with the addition 
of 8% olive stone flour to fired clay bricks. Likewise, Munir et al. [124]  observed a drop in thermal 
conductivity from 0.56 to 0.47 W m-1 K-1 by incorporating 15% waste marble sludge in clay bricks. The 
results obtained in this study are in agreement with Al -Hadhrami and Ahmad [145], thereby affirming 
that lower density results in better thermal insulation bricks. 
(a) (b) 

=== PDF PAGE 91 ===
 
73 
 
The general increase in CB content resulted in a decrease in thermal conductivity and an increase in 
porosity, as shown in Fig. 4.20(b). Porosity values of 7.09% and 20.57% were obtained for bricks 
incorporating 1% and 2% CBs by wt. with corresponding density values of 1983 and 1969 kg m-3. When 
both parameters are compared, we see a variance in the increase in porosity. Although the density values 
are quite reliable, the porosity values obtained from the micro -CT analysis may be influenced by 
subjectivity. Despite the technique (micro-CT analysis) possessing many advantages, it also presents 
limitations and difficulties. During CT analysis, the partial volume effect may result in changes in 
surfaces, higher degree of interconnection between pores, reduction of volume, and increase in pore 
size [146]. Moreover, image noise largely influences the calculation of certain parameters of pore size 
and, therefore, may lead to a substantial decrease of the noise pixel size inside the pore space [147] . In 
consideration of the mentioned factors, which may result in calculation errors, the density values present 
a more reliable data set in comparison to the micro-CT pore analysis results.

4.4 Conclusion  
In this study, bricks were manufactured incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by wt. and 
were tested for energy savings, thermal conductivity, porosity, micro - and macro-structural analysis. 
The current study has confirmed that it is possible to obtain light bricks from CBs as low-cost materials. 
The utilization of CB was te as a raw material in manufacturing fired clay bricks can be an important 
way of recycling for the final disposal of this abundant waste, leading to conservation of the 
environment. The key findings of the present work are as follows: 
• Based on the novel method developed to monitor the energy consumption of the furnace during 
the firing of brick specimens , for bricks incorporating 1% CBs by wt., a minimum energy 
saving of 8% is achievable. This means that an estimated $53.15 million in terms of electricity 
usage could be saved in Australia every year, thereby leading to economic construction.  
• The porosity, and micro- and macro-scale pore size, and distribution of bricks incorporating 
CBs by wt. were assessed by reconstructing a 3D section of samples from the micro-CT images. 
Based on the analysis, the following was observed: 
- The porosity ranged from 3.96% to 20.57% for bricks incorporating 0% to 2% CBs by wt. 
The loss of organic content in the CBs during the firing process resulted in the formation 
of macropores within the brick structures.  
- On a micrometer scale, the size of the micro- and macro- pores varied, and their diameters

ranged between 0.035 and 1.365 mm. The largest pore size of 1.365 mm was observed for 
bricks incorporating 1.5% and 2% CBs by wt. 
- The 3D models of the bricks incorporating CBs demonstrated a homogenous distribution 
of pores within the brick structures. In most cases, the micro - and macro-pores were not 
connected to each other. This indicates that the CBs within the clay mixtures were evenly 
distributed and homogenized during mixing. 
• A reduction of up to 15.12% in thermal conductivity was achieved with the addition of 1% CBs 
by wt. in clay bricks compared to traditional bricks. That is a decrease from 0.463 W m -1 K-1  
to 0.393 W m-1 K-1 in thermal conductivity due to the formation of pores from the burnt CBs, 
thereby improving t he thermal performance. Consequently, this will decrease the energy 
required for cooling and heating in households.  
• Density decreased with the addition of CBs, due to the burning of CBs in the brick body, 
producing lightweight bricks, which, in turn, may potentially reduce transportation and labor 
costs.  
Finally, recycling CBs in fired clay bricks offers a sustainable solution to a growing pollution problem, 
and, by recycling CBs in bricks, it is possible to completely offset the total littered CBs in Aus tralia, 
and, possibly, from around the world. Moreover, it is cheaper to produce in terms of energy 
requirements, the more CBs are added in the manufacturing of fired clay bricks, the further the costs 
associated with energy decrease.

Chapter 5: Leachate Analysis of Heavy 
Metals  
Part 5.1: Leachate Analysis of Heavy Metals in Cigarette Butts 
and Bricks Incorporated with Cigarette Butts 
Abstract  
Billions of cigarette butts (CBs) are discarded as litter in the environment every year worldwide. As 
CBs have poor biodegradability, it can take several years for them to break down while leaching toxic 
chemicals and heavy metals. Mohajerani et al. (2016), based on long-term research, developed a method 
for the recycling of CBs in fired clay bricks with promising results. This paper presents and discusses 
the leaching behavior of potentially hazardous metals from used, unused, and shredded used CBs, and 
unfired and fired clay bricks incorporating CBs. The leachate analysis was conducted according to the 
Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. The aim was to quantify 
the amount of heavy metals leached, determine the relationship between the metal concentration 
leachate, pH of the solution and condition of the sample, and examine the effect of firing on the leaching 
capability of bricks. The leachate results were then compared to the concentration limits for heavy 
metals set by the USEPA national primary drinking water and the EPA solid industrial waste hazard 
categorization thresholds to assess the suitability of fired clay bricks incorporating CBs. Metals Cu, Zn, 
Mn, Al, Fe, Ti, and Ba demonstrated the highest leachate concentrations for pH 2.9 and pH 5.0 for used 
CBs. This suggests that used CBs are more prone to leaching heavy metals in areas with highly acidic 
rain compared to the natural range of precipitation. The leaching behavior of fired bricks incorporating 
CBs was considerably lower than that for the unfired bricks due to the immobilization of heavy metals 
during the firing process. However, the leaching of Cr and Ni was almost completely impeded after the 
firing of the bricks, and more than 50% of all the tested heavy metals were hindered. 
The research presented in this chapter has been published in the following: 
Kurmus, H. and Mohajerani, A., 2020. Leachate Analysis of Heavy Metals in Cigarette Butts and Bricks 
Incorporated with Cigarette Butts. Materials, 13(12), 2843. 10.3390/ma13122843. 

=== PDF PAGE 95 ===
 
77 
 
5.1.1 Introduction 
The littering of cigarette butts (CBs) has become a critical environmental issue around the world. In 
Australia, 20 billion filtered cigarettes are consumed annually, and it is predicted that over 7 billion are 
thrown into the environment [6] . In 2016, 5.7 trillion cigarettes were consumed worldwide, and it is 
estimated that by 2025, cigarette production will increase by 50% [4].  
Over 97% of cigarettes have cellulose acetate filters [4], which are designed to partially p reserve 
particulate smoke components. The major toxic agents include nicotine, polycyclic aromatic 
hydrocarbons (PAHs), compounds specific to Solanaceae, nicotiana alkaloids, and catechols [148, 149]. 
Cellulose acetate filters have poor biodegradability, and, under normal environmental conditions, they 
can take up to 18 months to decompose; thus, becoming a source of toxic waste, polluting waterways 
and groundwater, and harming aquatic life and animals [50]. Several companies in Australia have 
developed CB disposal systems consisting of receptacles, the service includes the removal and final 
disposal of the CB waste. The common methods implemented for the disposal of collected CBs include 
landfilling and incineration. However,  neither technique is economically viable or environmentally 
friendly [10]. Therefore, it is possible for companies to directly deliver CB waste to brick manufacturing 
facilities to be used for recycling.  
The prevalent heavy metals found in CBs that prompt a likely risk to human health, aquatic organisms, 
and the environment, are Na, As, Mg, Sc, Pb, La, Bi, Cr, Mn, Cs, Co, Ni, Cu, K, Ag, Zn, Cd, Fe, Hg, 
Al, Sb, Au, Se, and Te [150, 151]. To investigate the transfer of toxic metals in the tobacco to the smoke 
component, several studies have been conducted [152-154] . Benson et al. [155]  found that the 
concentrations of Cu, Mn, Pb, and Zn are relatively higher in the filler tobacco compared to the 
concentration in the smoked cigarette filter, except for Cd. While Dobaradaran et al. [156]  found that 
Hg and Pb may be potential sources of prolonged contamination for aquatic organisms in coastal areas 
based on their response to heavy metals [156]. From a general point of view, Ar and Cr are causally 
associated with cancer in humans, while Ni and Cd are probably carcinogenic to humans [153]. Heavy 
smokers accumulate more than twice the body burden of Cd compared to a non-smoker. The inhaled 
heavy metals may prompt disorders of mineral metabolism, such as osteoporosis [150].  
A promising approach to a growing pollution calamity may be through the recycling of CBs in fired 
clay bricks. Mohajerani et al. [1] carried out a comprehensive study on recycling 0%, 2.5%, 5% 7.5%, 
and 10% CBs in fired clay bricks with promising results.  The tests carried out include density, 
compressive strength, water absorption, initial rate of absorption, shrinkage, modulus of rupture, 
thermal conductivity, and energy savings. A decrease in compressive strength was observed from 25.65 
MPa to 12.57 MPa for bricks incorporating 0% and 2.5% CBs. While water absorption decreased from 
18% to 5% for bricks incorporating 7.5% and 0% CBs. The study found an increase in CB content 

=== PDF PAGE 96 ===
 
78 
 
resulted in less dense clay bricks with greater porosity and consequently improved thermal performance 
and energy savings. A comprehensive study was completed for 1% CBs in fired clay bricks for the 
confirmation of the results. The compressive strength for controlled brick decreased from 25.65 M Pa 
to 19.52 MPa for bricks incorporating 1% CBs, and water absorption increased from 5% to 7.4% with 
the addition of 1% CBs. The paper proposed that by incorporating 1% CBs in 2.5% of the world’s brick 
production, all CBs can sustainably be recycled [22, 23, 157]. 
Several other investigations have been conducted to recycle wastes in the production of fired clay 
bricks. Such wastes include waste marble powder [158], recycled paper processing residue [143] , 
sugarcane bagasse ash [159], olive pomace [160], bio-briquette ash [161], expanded vermiculite [162], 
and olive mill solid residue [163]. However, placing toxic wastes in construction materials poses serious 
exposure risks to the environment and human health. Consequently, it is essential to conduct 
comprehensive environmental valuations of innovative building materials incorporating wastes prior to 
their use as commercial products.  
The main environmental issues raised from the use and disposal of construction materials incorporating 
various hazardous wastes are the possible leachate of toxic chemicals and heavy metals to the 
surrounding environment. Therefore, it is essential to conduct a comprehensive leachate analysis to 
classify and quantify those harmful constituents. Currently, there are over 50 leaching tests available 
for various sample conditions, pH values, and solid to liquid ratios to mimic a range of possible 
environmental conditions. A leachate test determines the liquid that may liquify or dissolve toxic 
chemicals or heavy metals and potentially enter th e environment [164]. The methods vary in terms of 
the mass and particle size of the sample, the type and volume of the leachate fluid, the leachate delivery 
method, and the leac hate contact time. The main leachate methods have been summarized in Table 
5.1.1, including their applications and limitations. 
Table 5.1.1 Applications and limitations of various leachate testing methods.
