By exploring the various leachate testing methods and comparing their applications and limitations, it 
is apparent that each method is limited to a specific material or environment. For particularly 
construction materials that are exposed to harsh weather conditions, the pH of the solution is very 
critical. Therefore the Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) [165]  or Method 1313 [168]  
testing methods are recommended as both are pH dependence tests and are suitable for fired clay bricks. 
The leachates from CB bricks represent heavy metals, which can possibly leach into the surrounding 
environment, and pose significant concerns for the quality of aquatic and urban life. The Leaching 
Environmental Assessment Framework (LEAF) is the most comprehensive method due to its extensive 
scope and comprises of Methods 1313, 1314, 1315, and 1316 [168-171]. However, the four methods in 
LEAF are recommended to be used in conjunction when conducting a leachate analysis and can be time 
consuming and unnecessary.  
Therefore, in reviewing the various leaching test methods, the ABLP [165]  was determined to be the 
most suitable leaching procedure for this particular study based on the sample size, pH values, disposal 
to land scenarios, and condition of the sample in comparison to the USEPA methods. Previous studies 
concerning the leachate of bricks incorporating CBs have found the concentrations of the metals to be 
in trace amounts and less than the regulatory thresholds [1] . However, the framework and 
methodologies followed did not account for the leaching behavior of bricks incorporating CBs over the 
scope of potential environmental situations expected in various disposal scenarios. In addition, the 
procedure followed was based on the Australian Standards edition published in 1997. 
This paper is the continuation of an ongoing study on the incorporation of CBs in fired clay bricks. The 
objective of this study was, therefore, to conduct two comprehensive leachate analysis investigations. 
In the first study, the leachate characteristics of used, unused, and shredded used CBs for various heavy 
metals were assessed. The aim was to quantify the amount of heavy metals leached from littered CBs 
and determine the correlation between the metal concentration leached and the pH of the solution. The 
second part of the study involved evaluating the leachate characteristics of unfired and fired clay bricks 

=== PDF PAGE 99 ===
 
81 
 
incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB content by mass. The leachate concentrations of various 
heavy metals were examined for unfired and fired bricks and compared to study the effect of firing on 
the immobilization of heavy metals of fired clay bricks. The leachate tests were performed in 
accordance to the ABLP method [165] for three pH values; 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2, to investigate how the pH 
affects the leaching behavior of the metals studied over a wide range of possible conditions. To evaluate 
the suitability of the fired clay bricks incorporating CBs, the leachate results were compared to the 
concentration limits for heavy metals set by the solid industrial waste hazard categorization published 
by the EPA Vic Industrial Waste Resource Guidelines [173]  and the United States national p rimary 
drinking water thresholds [174].  
5.1.2 Materials and Methods 
5.1.2.1 Materials 
Sandy silty clay (MC) and CBs were selected as the raw materials. The CBs of varying brands and sizes 
were supplied by Buttout Australia Pty Ltd. (Melbourne, Austral ia), while the soil was provided by 
PHG Bricks (Victoria, Australia). On arrival, the CBs were oven-dried at 105 °C (24 h) and disinfected. 
The oven-dried CBs were then placed in airtight plastic bags ready for use for the preparation of bricks 
samples. Particle size distribution (PSD) test was conducted on the brick soil using sieve analysis [127]. 
The brick soil was characterized by determining the mineralogical composition through an X -ray 
diffraction analyzer (XRD) (D8 Endeavor, Bruker, Billerica, MA, USA) and the chemical composition 
through X-ray fluorescence (XRF) (S4 Pioneer, Bruker, Billerica, MA, USA). 
5.1.2.2 Preparation of Brick Samples 
Five different mixtures were prepared and used in this experiment: clay bricks with 0% CBs by mass 
and bricks made of clay and 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass. The mechanical and physical 
properties of the prepared brick samples were assessed and prepared to be used for the leachate analysis 
test. Two batches of specimens were prepared and mixed in a Hobart mechanical mixer for 25 min with 
a water content of 15.5% and then compacted in cylindrical molds of 50 mm x 100 mm. The clay brick 
specimens were oven-dried at 105 ºC. The oven dried bricks were named as ‘unfired bricks.’ The second 
batch of unfired specimens was then fired at a temperature of 1050 ºC for 3 h. The fired brick was 
named as ‘fired bricks.’ The fired brick samples were tested for the initial rate of absorption (IRA), 
compressive strength, shrinkage, water absorption, efflorescence, microstructural analysis, and density 
according to the Australian Standards [175]  (Fig. 5.1.1). All the results presented are the average of 
three replicates.  

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
 
82 
 
 
Fig. 5.1.1. Some of the prepared brick samples with different percentages of cigarette butts (CBs). 
5.1.2.3 Sample Preparation and Leachate Analysis 
To evaluate the relationship between the metal concentration leached and the pH of the leaching 
solution, and to simulate various environmental conditions, three pH values were selected, 2.9, 5.0, and 
9.2 (Fig. 5. 1.2). The metals selected for both investigations were based on their toxicity levels to the 
local and aquatic organisms, their presence in CBs, and their ability to easily be analyzed by the 
Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass Spectrometry (ICP-MS). The leaching of heavy metals from the 
prepared brick samples was determined based on the ABLP [165]. Heavy metals, including Cadmium 
(Cd), Iron (F e), Aluminum (Al), Lead (Pb), Arsenic (As), Mercury (Hg), Barium (Ba), Nickel (Ni), 
Copper (Cu), Titanium (Ti), Chromium (Cr), and Zinc (Zn), were analyzed for used, unused, and 
shredded used CBs. While Chromium (Cr), Copper (Cu), Silver (Ag), Barium (Ba),  Mercury (Hg), 
Selenium (Se), Nickel (Ni), Cadmium (Cd), Arsenic (As), Lead (Pb), and Zinc (Zn) were examined for 
unfired and fired clay bricks incorporating CBs. In the ABLP method as presented in Fig. 5. 1.3, the 
brick samples were crushed with a hammer and sieved using a 2.4 mm sieve.

Fig. 5.1.2. Leaching procedure for leachates derived from (a) used, unused, and shredded used CBs and (b) fired 
clay bricks and (c) unfired clay bricks incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass. 
The fluid extract of pH 2.9 was prepared by adding glacial acetic acid (5.7 mL) to 900 mL of water, 
which was later diluted to 1 L with water. pH 5.0 was prepared by adding glacial acetic acid (5.7 mL) 
to approximately 900 mL of water to which sodium  hydroxide solution (64.3 mL of 1 mol  L-1) was 
added; the solution was then diluted to 1 L with water and mixed well. pH 9.2 was achieved by 
dissolving sodium tetraborate decahydrate (borax) (38.2 g) in approximately 900 mL of water and then 
diluting this to 1 L with water. The pH of this fluid was to be between 9.1 and 9.3, and where the pH 
was not between these levels, the fluid was discarded. 
Samples were prepared based on a 1:20 ratio in screw cap bottles. The mass of the crushed fired clay 
brick samples used in this study was 10 g, while the volume of the extraction fluid was 200 mL. The 
bottles were then placed in an agitator and rotated at 30 ± 2 rpm for 18 ± 2 h. The fluid extract was 
attained by filtering the samples through a 0.45-microfilter to separate it from the solid phase. The ICP-
MS was utilized to analyze the extracted samples for heavy metals. The results exhibited in this study 
are the average of three replicates.

5.1.3 Results and Discussion 
5.1.3.1 Characterization of Raw Materials 
The brick soil used in this study was sandy silty clay (MC) according to the Unified soil classifications 
[127]. The results of the PSD are shown in Fig . 5.1.4, which was carried out using sieve size analysis 
[127]. For the brick soil, the plastic limit was found to be 25% and 34% for the liquid limit. The XRF 
results of the chemical composition of the brick soil are given in Fig. 5. 1.5, while the XRD results of 
the mineralogical composition of the brick soil can be seen in Fig. 5.1.6. It was observed that the main 
crystalline phase on a <75 µm soil sample of the brick soil was Quartz (SiO2).

5.1.3.2 Properties of Fired Clay Bricks 
The properties of 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB content b y mass bricks are shown in Table 5. 1.2 
[116, 177]. The compressive strength of a brick is a crucial quality for construction materials. In this 
study, the results indicate that the strength of the clay brick was significantly dependent on the quantity 
of CBs added. A decrease in compressive strength was found with the decrease in density and the 
increase in CB content. The results reveal that the compressive strength was in the range of 27.2 – 48.64 
MPa, with a decrease in CB content from 2% to 0% by mass. Although there is a substantial decrease 
in strength with the increase in CB content, the values are considerably abo ve the standard minimum 
compressive strength of 17.2 MPa for Grade MW (moderate weathering) bricks [178] . The density of 
the fired bricks was in the range of 1969 - 2114 kg m-3, with a decrease in CB content from 2% to 0%

The loss of water in the brick between the clay particles, which results in the proximate packing of those 
particles, is called firing shrinkage. The results show that the firing diametric and height shrinkage 
values decrease with the increase in CB content. It was found that the diametric shrinkage values were 
between 3.1% and 4.64%, with the decrease in CB content in the bricks from 2% to 0% by mass, which 
is below the maximum standard shrinkage value of 8% [179].  
Water absorption determines the durability of a brick. When a high content of water can infiltrate a 
brick, the durability decreases, which can give rise to frost damage, salt crystallization, and change in 
volume, which can eventually result in structural damage to the building, therefore, a brick must be 
adequately dense to avoid the encroachment of water [22, 180]. According to Table 5. 1.2, water 
absorption and IRA increase with the increase in CB content. This is due to the decrease in density and 
the creation of pores from the CBs that were burnt off during t he firing process. Bricks with a CB 
content of 0% to 2% by mass presented water absorption values between 9% to 13.1%. The water 
absorption for standard fired clay bricks should range between 5% to 20% [65]. The IRA values ranged 
between 0.44 to 0.84 kg m-2 min-1 for bricks incorporating between 0% to 2% CBs by mass. The values 
presented for IRA fall within the acceptable range of 0.2 to 5 kg m-2 min-1 [65]. Nevertheless, the water 
absorption and IRA results comply with the typical requirement range. 
5.1.3.3 Leachate Analysis of Cigarette Butt (CB) Samples 
Cigarettes contain a distinctive quantity of trace elements known to have toxicological effects on living 
organisms, and the smoking process drastically increases the concentrations of those elements in the 
cellulose acetate filter [153]. To identify and quantify the metals leached from discarded CBs and 

=== PDF PAGE 106 ===
 
88 
 
determine the relationship between the metal concentration leached, the pH of the solution and the 
condition of the sample, a comprehensive leachate analysis was conducted based on the ABLP [165].  
To simulate natural conditions, the leaching procedures used in this study were designed as closely as 
possible to an open environment where CBs can be found. To investigate how the pH affects the 
leaching behavior of metals from CBs, three pH values were selected: 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. The selected 
metals for this study include Ni, Zn, As, Hg, Al, Cu, Ba, Cr, Fe, Ti, Pb, and Cd and are based on their 
high degree of toxicity and presence in CBs [150-154]. 
As illustrated in Fig. 5.1.7, the findings suggest that differences in pH over a wide range of precipitation 
have an effect on the behavior of the metal concentrations leached from unused CBs. A direct 
relationship between the concentrations of Cu, Zn, Mn, Al, and Ba leached, and the corresponding pH 
value was observed. Cu, Zn, Mn, Al, and Ba demonstrated the highest leachate concentration for pH 
2.9, followed by pH 5.0. While pH 9.2 exhibited the lowest metal leachate concentration. The 
concentration level for Ba was 7.610, 4.572, and 3.1 mg L -1 for pH 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. This implies that 
unused CBs are more likely to leach heavy metals in areas with highly acidic rain compared to the 
natural range of precipitation. This is due to the enhancement of metal mobilization in acid rain. The 
hydrogen (H+) ion in the acidic rain disturbs the cations (positively charged ion) from their connection 
sites, this results in the decrement of cation exchange capacity and increase in the concentration of 
cations in CBs, therefore allowing the cations to be leached from the waste [181]. Likewise, the toxic 
ions (toxic metals) usually bound to the negatively charged surface and are displaced by H + ion, too 
[182]. 
 
Fig. 5.1.7. Leachate concentrations of unused CBs.

The concentration of metals leached for used CBs for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2 can be seen in Fig. 
5.1.8. The results indicate that changes to the pH of precipitation will likely enhance or reduce the 
magnitude of metal contamination from used CBs. The metal concentration leachate for Zn, Mn, and 
Al for pH 5.0 was found to be much more significant compared to pH 2.9 and 9.2. In comparison, Ba, 
Cu, Ti and Fe depicted greater metal contamination for pH 2.9. Similarly, to unused CBs, used CBs are 
more prone to leaching in highly acidic areas due to the enhancement of metal mobilization in acidic 
water.   
 
Fig. 5.1.8. Leachate concentrations of used CBs. 
A direct relationship between the metal concentration leached and pH value was found for shredded 
used CBs. The concentration of the metals leached for pH values, 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2, can be seen in Fig. 
4.9. pH 5.0 resulted in the highest magnitude of metal contamination for Ba, Zn, Mn, and Al, followed 
by pH 2.9, while pH 9.2 demonstrated the least possible metal contamination. The relationship between 
metal leachate and pH value supports the results attained for both unused and used CBs.

Fig. 5.1.9. Leachate concentrations of shredded used CBs. 
To determine whether the smoking process influences the quantity of heavy metals found within CBs, 
the metal leachate concentrations for pH 5.0 for unused, used,  and shredded used CB samples was 
analyzed. A linear relationship between the concentration of metals leached and the condition of the 
sample was found. Cr, Cd, Zn, Mn, Al and Fe contamination was greater for shredded used CBs 
compared to used and unused C Bs (Fig . 5. 10). For shredded used CBs, 6.982 mg L -1 leachate was 
recorded for Al, compared to 6.887 mg L -1 leachate for used CBs and 4.161 mg  L-1 for unused CBs. 
Generally, Al is one of the most abundant elements found in CBs due to its presence in tobacco. Al 
toxicity can lead to tissue-damaging effects, alteration in calcium metabolism in several organ systems, 
and Alzheimer’s disease [183]. The results suggest that used and shredded used CBs are more prone to 
leaching heavy metals Cr, Cd, Zn, Mn, Al and Fe compared to unused CBs. One explanation for this 
observation is that an enhanced level of Cr, Cd, Zn, Mn, Al and Fe is absorbed and retained by the filter 
during combustion. The remnant tobacco, which comprised of burnt and unburnt tobacco, effectively 
exacerbated the toxicity [17] . Other metals, including Pb and As are of insignificant concern for all  
sample conditions as their leachate concentrations are below 0.08 mg L-1.

In contrast, the leaching of Cu, Ba, and Ni at pH 5.0 demonstrated a distinctive trend. Unused CBs 
exhibited the highest leachate concentration for all three metals, followed by shredded CBs, while used 
CBs displayed the least metal contamination. One explanation for this observation is that majority of 
the weight of Cu, Ba, and Ni pass through the vapor phase, contrary to being trapped in the filter. 
5.1.3.4 Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) Leachate Analysis of Fired Clay Bricks 
The toxicity of CBs is a concern when incorporated in construction materials like fired clay bricks. 
Therefore, it is essential to conduct extensive environmental valuations of innovative building materials 
modified with waste materials prior to being used as a commercial product. The transfer of heavy metals 
from bricks incorporating CBs to the environment is determined through a leachate analysis using the 
ABLP [165] and compared with the threshold regulatory limits for  heavy metals set by the USEPA 
[174] and Industrial Waste Resource Guidelines [173]. Table 5.1.3 and Figs. 5.1.11 – 5.1.15 summarize 
the ICP-MS results for heavy metals using the ABLP method for pH 2.9, pH 5.0, and pH 9.2. All data 
are means of three values relative to standard deviation. The standard deviation is relatively small for 
most of the results, confirming the efficiency of the method.

=== PDF PAGE 113 ===
 
95 
 
 
Fig. 5.1.15. Leachate concentrations for crushed bricks with 2% CB content. 
The findings of this study indicate that differences in pH have an effect on the quantity of metal 
concentration leached for fired clay bricks incorporating CBs. The results imply that changes to the pH 
will likely increase or decrease the magnitude of metal contamination from fired clay bricks. In this 
study, an increasing trend in the metal concentration leachate was observed for fired clay bricks with 
the increase in CB content from 0% to 2% by mass. This is due to the increase in CB content, as 
discussed above, CBs are more prone to leaching heavy metals in areas with a pH of 2.9, compared to 
a pH of 9.2. The effect of the pH on the convergence of heavy metals plays a crucial role in the migration 
and transformation of metals. For instance, Yang et al. [184] found that the speciation of Cd in sediment 
increases with the increase in pH from 4.5 – 9.5. While Zhang et al. [185]  found the release tendency 
of Ni and Cu to be lower in a pH range of 5 - 10, compared to pH 0 – 4, probably because of the low 
concentration of dissolved organic carbon (DOC) in deionized water. 
A direct relationship between the pH and the metal concentration leachate was found. A decrease in pH 
from 9.2 - 2.9 resulted in an increase in the metal concentration leached for  Zn, Cu, Ba, and Ni for 
bricks incorporating 1% and 2% CBs by mass (Figs. 5.1.16 & 5.1.17). This implies that pH values, such 
as those tested in areas with highly acidic rain (pH 2.9) outside the natural scope of precipitation, will 
result in the metal contamination of Zn, Cu, Ba, and Ni. This is because acidic water composes of a 
high number of H + ions. Therefore the surface of the particles is positively charged, which results in 
weak metal sorption and the release of toxic ions such as Zn, Cu, Ba, and Ni  [185]. Other metals, 
including As, Cr, Se, Ag, Cd, Hg, and Pb are of insignificant concern as the leachate concentrations are 
below 0.03 mg L-1.

The results confirm that fired clay bricks incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass can 
be categorized as non-hazardous waste under various pH values as they are clearly below the regulatory 
limits for heavy metals. To better interpret the insignificance of the results, the leachate concentrations 
were compared to the United States national primary drinking water thresholds set by the USEPA [174]. 
Cu and Ba leachate concentrations for 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB bric ks were the only metals 
moderately above the threshold limits, while the remaining leachate concentrations were considerably 
below the limits.

5.1.3.5 Unfired Bricks and Fired Bricks Comparative Leachate Analysis 
Concerns about heavy metal contamination to  the environment that might be initiated through the 
incorporation of CBs, which cannot be combusted during the firing process, represent a major issue. 
Therefore, to investigate the variations in concentrations of 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CB bricks 
before and after firing, pH value 9.2 was selected. The results are presented in Figs. 5.1.18 – 5.1.21 and 
Table 5.1.4. All data are means of three values relative to standard deviation. The findings show that 
the leaching behavior of the fired clay bricks is notably lower after the firing process. This is the result 
of the suppression of metals that are locked in the bricks when fired to high temperatures. This behavior 
can be associated with the silicate-based physio chemical containment process of fired clay bricks [135]. 
The main compounds found in the brick soil were silica (SiO2) and iron oxide (Fe2O3). Silica enhances 
the interconnection and compactness of the material, wh ich can result in metal immobilization [186], 
while iron oxide can assist in the formation and stability of the material, because of the crystallization-
dehydration of less crystallized free iron oxides and the removal of organic materials [187]. Hence 
decreasing the leaching potential of heavy metals from the fired clay bricks incorporating CBs. 
 
Fig. 5.1.18. Heavy metal concentration of 0.5% CB content bricks before and after firing.

The fluctuations in the leachate concentrations for unfired and fired bricks incorporating 0.5%, 1%, 
1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass are summarized in Table 5.1.4. The results indicate that the addition of CBs 
in fired clay bricks considerably reduces the leaching ability of heavy metals. For example, the leaching 
of Cr and Ni was almost completely impeded after firing for bricks incorporating 0.5% and 1.5% CBs 
by mass. In addition, over 50% of all tested heavy metals were immobilized after firing for 0.5%, 1%, 
1.5%, and 2% CB bricks. However, Ba demonstrated the least change in metal contamination after the 
firing process. More than 90% of Cr was immobilized in CB fired clay bricks compared to CB unfired 
bricks. Incorporating CBs in fired clay bricks resulted in a decrease in the metal contamination of the 
toxic waste, which can be an environmental advantage compared to the littering of CBs. The high firing 
temperature immobilized the heavy metals within the brick due to the crystalline structure of the 
material.

pH values subject to changing environmental conditions, it does not consider leaching subject to various 
liquid to solid ratios and compacted granular material. However, it can be stated that fired clay bricks 
amended with CBs are safe under various conditions and environments as the results are significantly 
below the threshold limits.

5.1.4 Conclusions 
The incorporation of CBs into fired clay bricks could provide a viable resolution to an escalating waste 
pollution tragedy, with acceptable mechanical and physical properties and a new cost -effective 
construction product. Therefore, the results presented here may clarify issues raised regarding the 
possible leachate of heavy metals from the use and disposal of bricks incorporating CBs. The following 
conclusion can be drawn from this study: 
• Various leachate testing methods were compared, including the ABLP and USEPA methods. 
In reviewing the various leaching test procedures, the ABLP was determined to be the most 
suitable leaching procedure for this study based on the sample size, pH values, disposal-to-land 
scenarios, and condition of the sample.  
• Metals Cu, Zn, Mn, Al, Fe, Ti, and Ba demonstrated the highest leachate concentration for pH 
2.9 and 5.0 for used CBs. This implies that used CBs are more likely to leach heavy metals in 
areas with highly acidic rain compared to the natural range of precipitation. This is due to the 
enhancement of metal mobilization in acidic water, whereby the toxic ions (toxic metals) bound 
to the negatively charged surface and are displaced by H+ (hydrogen) ion. 
• It was found that used and shredded used CBs are more prone to leaching heavy metals Cr, Cd, 
Zn, Mn, Al, and Fe compared to unused CBs. This is because an enhanced level of Cr, Cd, Zn, 
Mn, Al, and Fe is absorbed and retained by the filter during combustion. 
• The leachate results for fired clay bricks incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by 
mass were found to be below the regulatory limits set by the EPA Victoria solid industrial waste 
guidelines. Therefore, bricks modified with CBs can be categorized as non-hazardous waste.  
• The leaching of Cr and Ni was almost completely hindered after the firing process of clay bricks 
incorporating 0.5% and 1.5% CBs by mass. In addition, more than 50% of all the tested heavy 
metals were immobilized after firing for 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by mass bricks. Hence 
the addition of CBs in fired clay bricks may be a feasible option to hinder the heavy metal s 
present in CBs. 
• The compressive strength of the CB brick samples varied considerably with the percentage of 
organic content within the mixture. However, the values were considerably above the minimum 
requirement of 17.2 MPa for compressive strength. The addition of CBs produced less dense 
and porous bricks, with better thermal insulation properties.  
• The water absorption and IRA results were within the acceptable range, indicating CB brick 
samples are competent to endure harsh weather conditions.  
It is recommended to investigate the leaching performance of toxic metals at the various liquid to solid 
ratios and for compacted granular material, as described in Method 1314 [169] and Method 1315 [170], 

=== PDF PAGE 120 ===
 
102 
 
 
to consider leaching of bricks subjected to various disposal or use scenarios. Moreover, acceptable 
limits for heavy metals for various applications, particularly for construction material manufactured 
with waste material, should be developed. However, it can be stated fired clay bricks incorporating CBs 
are safe under various conditions and environments as the leachate concentrations are adequately below 
the regulatory limits. Moreover, the addition of CB waste in brick manufacturing offers a sustainable 
recycling solution to a growing pollution issue. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 121 ===
 
103 
 
 
Part 5.2: Physical Properties and Leachate Analysis of Bitumen 
and Paraffin Wax Encapsulated Cigarette Butts and its use in 
Road Construction 
Abstract 
Littered cigarette butts (CBs) are a form of poorly biodegradable waste. Attempts have been made to 
prevent the pollution caused by the waste through the recycling of CBs in construction materials. A 
novel method involving the encapsulation of CBs with paraffin wax and bitumen was developed, which 
investigated its use in hot -dense asphalt concrete with promising results. The objective of this study 
was to carry out a comprehensive leachate analysis to examine the effectiveness and efficiency of the 
encapsulation method for the prevention of leachates from CBs and to investigate the physical 
properties of wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs. The leachate analysis was conducted using the 
Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. The leaching behavior 
of encapsulated CBs was insignificant compared to fresh CBs due to the immobilization of metals after 
being encapsulated in bitumen and w ax. The findings suggest that the variance in pH influences the 
behavior of the metal concentration leached. Generally, pH 2.9 resulted in the highest metal 
contamination for all sample types, followed by pH 5.0 and pH 9.2.  The water absorption decreased 
significantly from 383% for fresh CBs to 4% and 5% for bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs. Micro-
X-ray CT imaging was used to study and analyze the porosity and pore structure of CBs, and a 
substantial decrease in porosity from 66% to 0.5% was determined when CBs were encapsulated with 
bitumen.  
The research presented in this chapter has been accepted in the following: 
Mohajerani, A., Kurmus, H.,  Rahman, M.T., Smith, J.V., Woo, S.S., Jones, D., Dastjerdi, M.G., 
Nguyen, B.T. and Calderon, C., 2021. Bitumen and Paraffin Wax Encapsulated Cigarette Butt; Physical 
Properties and Leachate Analysis. Journal of  Pavement Research and Technology.  
https://doi.org/10.1007/s42947-021-00063-9 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
 
104 
 
 
5.2.1 Introduction  
5.2.1.1 Cigarette butt pollution 
Cigarette butts (CBs) are one of the most frequently littered wastes in the world [188] . Worldwide, 
smokers roughly consume 6.5 trillion cigarettes each year, and an estimated one-third are disposed of 
correctly while the remai ning 4 trillion CBs are discarded into the environment [188]. In Australia, 7 
billion CBs are carelessly thrown into the environment each year [189]. It costs the Victorian economy 
$25.7 million for tobacco-related litter control and roughly $7.5 million in San Francisco every year 
[188, 189]. Furthermore, the environmental impacts of the continuous littering of CBs are of greate r 
concern for the health of humans, wildlife, and marine and freshwater organisms [125]. 
The abundance and slow decomposition rate of CBs exacerbate the detrimental effects on the 
environment. Approximately 95% of CBs are comprised of plastic filaments that are fabricated from 
synthetic fibers (cellulose acetate) derived from wood pulp [190] . The conventional composition of a 
cigarette can be seen in Fig. 5.2.1. The decomposition of cellulose acetate varies between a few months 
to several years, depending on various environmental factors [40, 191-194], and only dissipates 37.8% 
of their initial mass after two years of decomposition [195].  
 
Fig. 5.2.1. Physical structure of a cigarette with each component described and identified.  
Cigarette filters are designed to absorb and trap smoke components, including tar and toxic chemicals 
[196]. Cigarette smoke contains up to 4,000 chemical components, approximately 3,000 in the gas 
phase and 1,000 in the tar phase. The compounds include N-nitrosamines, aromatic amines, hydrogen 
cyanide, ammonia, formaldehyde, benzene, acetaldehyde, phenols, pyridines,  and toxic heavy metals, 
including nickel and cadmium that can combine to form over 60 chemicals that are known to be 
carcinogenic [11, 196]. Smoked CBs contain a substantial quantity of nicotine and other compounds 
produced during the combustion of tobacco and, essentially, pose a higher toxic effect in contrast to 


=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
 
105 
 
 
unsmoked cigarettes [125]. Therefore, when CBs are littered, toxic chemicals and heavy metals are 
leached into the environment, resulting in a significant impact on living organisms and the health of the 
ecosystem [50, 196-198]. 
The possible disposal methods for CBs include incineration and landfilling. However, neither method 
is sustainable or economically feasible. CBs remain an environmental hazard when sent to landfills, 
and the continuous disposal of hazardous wastes to landfills is becoming costly and difficult [125, 199, 
200], while the process of incineration can result in the release of harmful emissions [10]. 
Therefore, it is becoming crucial to find an effective and practical method to recycle CB waste. One 
environmentally friendly way to solve this growing global pollution problem may be through the 
sustainable method of recycling CBs in construction materials. Indeed, several studies have been carried 
out on the incorporation of CBs in fired clay bricks [1, 23, 24, 116, 177, 201, 202] . Various mixes 
containing between 0% to 10% CBs by weight (wt) were prepared and tested for density, shrinkage, 
initial rate of absorption (IRA), water absorption, compressive strength, thermal conductivity, a nd 
energy savings. Bricks incorporating 1% CBs by wt demonstrated a 5.2% decrease in dry density and 
a 24% decrease in compressive strength compared to the control bricks. While water absorption and 
IRA increased from 9% to 12% and 0.44 kg m-2 min-1 to 0.67 kg m-2 min-1 for bricks incorporating 0% 
and 1% CBs by wt. By using the calculation method, an energy saving of 10.2% was estimated for fired 
clay bricks incorporating 1% CBs by wt. Furthermore, it was estimated that if 1% CBs were 
incorporated into 2.5% of the bricks produced worldwide, the CB problem could possibly be eradicated 
from the world [1, 203].   
5.2.1.2 Encapsulation of CBs 
Mohajerani et al. [19] developed a novel method involving the encapsulation of CBs with bitumen and 
paraffin wax and incorporating them into asphalt concrete (AC). Dense graded asphalt samples 
containing different classes of bitumen (C170, C320, C600) encapsulated CBs (10 kg  m-3, 15 kg m-3, 
25 kg m-3) were manufactured according to the Austroads [204].  The stability values for asphalt mixes 
with 10 kg m -3 and 15 kg m -3 CB content encapsulated with C170, C320, and C600 bitumen satisfied 
the conditions for road pavements for light, medium, and heavy traffic, respectively. However, the AC 
mixes incorporating 25 kg/m3 CBs encapsulated with C170, C320, and C600 bitumen did not fulfill the 
minimum conditions for medium and heavy traffic. The flow values were all within the aimed range of 
2 to 4 mm other than the AC mix comprising 10 kg  m-3 CB content encapsulated with C320 bitumen. 
A decline in the inflow was observed for the AC mixes from 2.5 mm for the controlled sample to 1.8 
mm for the C320 bitumen encapsulated CB sample. Moreover, the increase in CB content ensued in an 
increase in the inflow. For C170 bitumen, the resilient modulus decreased from 4703 MPa for the AC 
mix containing no CBs to 3387 MPa for the AC mix containing a CB content of 15 kg m-3. It is apparent 

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
 
106 
 
 
that the increase in CB content in the AC mix results in a decrease in the resilient modulus. However, 
it can be noted that the resilient modulus for the 15 kg m -3 and 25 kg m -3 CB content AC mixes was 
within the 2500 to 4000 MPa resilient modulus target range.  
The bulk density and maximum density results for the AC bitumen encapsulated CB samples ranged 
from 2262 to 2421 kg m -3 and 2352 to 2546 kg m -3. Two trends were observed, the increase in CB 
content resulted in a decrease in the maximum density and bulk density, and the maximum density was 
found to be greater than the bulk density. Lower density is desirable as it will decrease the thermal 
conductivity. 
As a continuation of the parent research, Rahman et al. (2020) investigated the application of bitumen 
encapsulated CBs in stone mastic asphalt (SMA). The research has revealed mechanical properties like 
Marshall stability, flow, and resilient modulus of modified SMA [205]. SMA samples were compacted 
(by maintaining density) using 1%, 2%, and 3% (by weight of asphalt mix) bitumen encapsulated CBs, 
and results from laboratory investigation indicated up to 1% use of bitumen encapsulated CBs met 
industry requirements [205]. Later as an expansion of the original research, rutting, fatigue, and 
moisture damage characteristics of dense graded asphalt prepared with bitumen encapsulated CBs were 
investigated, and the use of up to 1% bitumen encapsulated CBs met industry requirements and 
supported the application of bitumen encapsulated CBs in asphalt [206]. 
The process of encapsulation involves the coating of hazardous materials in matrices to prevent the 
escape of toxic chemicals [207] . The use of bitumen to encapsulate radioactive waste started i n the 
1960s and was used due to its insolubility and impermeability to water [207, 208] . Water -soluble 
radioactive wastes, such as spent organic ion exchangers and bottom residues from evaporative 
treatment, are exceptionally suitable for bituminization [208] . Prefabricated bitumen membranes and 
asphalt concrete are used to seal the soil surface where water pollution is likely to occur to prevent the 
subsoil from being polluted by industrial waste [209].  
Another effective mixture for encapsulation is paraffin wax. Paraffin wax is obtained from  petroleum 
and was first commercially produced in 1867, less than ten years after the initial petroleum well was 
drilled [210]. Throughout the 20 th century, it was one of the most popular and earliest water repellent 
finishes [211]. A highly efficient way to create water repellent fabric  was to coat it with paraffin wax. 
This coated the fibers with a thin layer of wax, especially those exposed to water, thereby giving the 
fabric exceptional water repellent properties. Therefore, the use of paraffin wax as an encapsulation 
material can potentially avert the escape of the toxic chemicals and heavy metals found in CBs.  
5.2.1.3 Objective 
The objective of this study was to carry out a comprehensive leachate analysis to examine the 
effectiveness and efficiency of the encapsulation method for the prevention of leachates of heavy metals 

=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
 
107 
 
 
from CBs and investigate the physical properties of wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs. The leachate 
analysis was performed using the Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) [212]  for pH values 
2.9, 5.0, and 9.2. Method 1313 [168] is a similar leachate testing method to the ABLP, both being pH-
dependent. However, Method 1313 is part of the four procedures in the Leaching Environmental 
Assessment Framework (LEAF), and it is recommended all four methods be used in conjunction when 
examining a sample. In the ABLP, the metals studied include Be, Mg, Al, Se, As, Pb, V, Mn, Fe, Co, 
Ni, Cu, Cr, Ag, Zn, Mo, Cd, Ti, Th, and U. To evaluate the suitability of bitumen and wax encapsulated 
CBs and their possible use in road construction, the leachate results were compared to the concentration 
limits for heavy metals set by the Australian drinking water guidelines [213] and solid industrial waste 
hazard categorization thresholds [173]. To determine the effectiveness and efficiency of the developed 
method, the encapsulated CBs underwent microstructural analysis and were tested for weight, density, 
bulk density, water absorption, and strength.  
5.2.2 Materials and methods 
5.2.2.1 Bitumen 
Bitumen, also known as asphalt binder, is a highly viscous petroleum -based hydrocarbon that can be 
found in natural deposits or refined from crude oil.  The primary use of bitumen is in road construction, 
where it is used as a binder between aggregates, however, it is also commonly used to create bituminous 
waterproofing products and to encapsulate radioactive waste [15]. Approximately 102 million tons of 
bitumen are used per year, and of that, about 85% is used as the binder in asphalt concrete for roads. 
There are different grades of bitumen available that all have di fferent properties. The different grades 
in Australia are detailed in Standards Australia [214], including the method of testing and classification. 
In this research, Bitumen PMB A10E was used to encapsulate CBs. The properties of the bitumen used 
according to the supplier specifications are given in Table 5.2.1 [3]. 
Table 5.2.1. Properties of bitumen PMB A10E (adopted from Puma Bitumen [3]). 
Properties Typical value 
Softening Point  95°C 
Torsional Recovery at 25 °C 73% 
Viscosity at 165 °C 0.9 Pa·s 
Stiffness at 2 5°C 25 kPa 
Segregation 0.5% 
Consistency at 60 °C  16,000 Pa·s 

=== PDF PAGE 126 ===
 
108 
 
 
5.2.2.2 Paraffin wax 
Paraffin wax is a crystalline mixture of solid straight -chain hydrocarbons, characterized by its solid 
state at room temperature. It is a white and colorless soft, solid wax. Paraffin wax is obtained from 
petroleum, and the common uses of paraffin wax include candles, polishes, electrical insulators, wax 
paper, and cosmetics [210]. The main properties of the type of paraffin wax used in this paper are 
summarized in Table 5.2.2.

5.2.2.3 CB Encapsulation process  
5.2.2.3.1 Pre-processing of CBs 
Used CBs of varying sizes and brands were supplied by Buttout Australia Pty Ltd and dried in the oven 
for 24 h at 110 °C to remove the moisture content. It is critical that the CBs are completely dried out to 
ensure that the bitumen and wax completely fill the voids within the CBs during the encapsulation 
process. After drying, the excess paper and tobacco were removed from the CBs. 
5.2.2.3.2 Bitumen encapsulation of CBs 
The procedure followed to encapsulate CBs with bitumen is shown in Fig. 5.2.2(a). To increase the 
workability of the bitumen as a liquid form to perform an effective encapsulation, the bitumen was 
placed in the oven in a container for 2 h at 160 ºC. In a thin aluminum tray, the liquid bitumen was 
poured, and CBs were placed in the tray and then placed in the oven for 1 hr at 160 ºC. Throughout the 
oven drying process, the bitumen-CB mixture was stirred with a steel rod every 10 min. During this 
process, the air voids within the CB filter were replaced with bitumen. Once removed from the oven, 

=== PDF PAGE 127 ===
 
109 
 
 
the encapsulated CBs were collected and placed on a 6.7 mm sieve to break up the excess bitumen from 
individual bitumen encapsulated CBs. The collected encapsulated CBs were stored at room temperature 
in a tray. 
5.2.2.3.3 Wax encapsulation of CBs 
The procedure followed to encapsulate CBs with wax is demonstrated in Fig. 5.2.2(b). A saucepan was 
used to heat the wax to 140 ℃ until it was completely melted. The CBs were placed in the mixture and 
gently pressed to the bottom of the pot to ensure the wax completely infiltrated into the voids of the 
CBs. The CBs were then left to soak in the hot wax for approximately 10 min. Once the CBs were 
soaked in wax for 10 min, the CBs were carefully removed from the hot wax and placed in a sieve to 
remove the excess wax. The CBs were then placed on a baking tray and left to dry and harden. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 128 ===
 
110 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 5.2.2. Flow chart of the process followed to encapsulate CBs with (a) bitumen and (b) paraffin wax.

5.2.2.4 Leachate analysis 
To determine whether the encapsulation process of CBs prevents or reduces the leaching of metals 
found within CBs, a comprehensive leachate analysis was conducted. The ABLP [212]  was followed 
in this study on oven-dried CBs, fresh CBs, bitumen encapsulated CBs (broken), bitumen encapsulated 
CBs (unbroken), wax encapsulated CBs (broken), and wax encapsulated CBs (unbroken). To simulate various 
disposal scenarios, pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2 were selected. Metals including Beryllium (Be), Magnesium (Mg), 
Selenium (Se), Aluminum (Al), Thallium (Ti), Vanadium (V), Molybdenum (Mo), Chromium (Cr), Lea d (Pb), 
Manganese (Mn), Copper (Cu), Iron (Fe), Silver (Ag), Cobalt (Co), Arsenic (As), Nickel (Ni), Zinc (Zn), 
Cadmium (Cd), Thorium (Th), and Uranium (U) were examined for all six sample types. The experimental 
procedure followed in this study to prepare the leachates is shown in Fig. 5.2.3. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 5.2.3. Leachate analysis process followed in this study. 
Initially, three different pH leaching fluids were prepared. For leaching fluid pH 2.9, 5.7 ml of glacial 
acetic acid was added to 900 ml of deionized water. The solution was then diluted with deionized water 
to a volume of 1L. The pH of the solution was then determined using a pH meter, and if the value was 
not in the range of pH 2.8 - 3.0, the solution was discarded, and the process started again.  Leaching 
fluid pH 5.0 was prepared by adding 5.7 ml of glacial acetic acid to 900 ml of deionized water, then 
64.3 ml of 1 mol  L-1 sodium hydroxide solution was added to the solution. The fluid was then diluted 
pH extraction fluids 2.9, 5.0 
and 9.2 are prepared 
Bottles rotated in agitator 
Solid to extraction fluid 
ratios 1:20 are prepared 
Liquid phase filtered through 
a 0.6 µm glass fibre filter 
Filtered leachates 
Leachates analysed using 
ICP-MS 

=== PDF PAGE 130 ===
 
112 
 
 
with deionized water to a volume of 1L and mixed well. The pH of the solution was then determined 
using a pH meter; if the pH value was 5.0, the solution was finished, while if it was in the range of 4.9 
to 5.1, the pH was adjusted by the addition of 1 mol  L-1 acetic acid or 1 mol L -1 sodium hydroxide 
solution. The final solution was then stored in an airtight container. Leaching fluid pH 9.2 was prepared 
by dissolving 38.2 g of sodium tetraborate decahydrate into 900 ml of water. The solution was then 
diluted with water to 1 L. The pH of the solution was then determined using a pH meter; if the pH was 
not 9.2, the solution was discarded, and the process started again. 
Samples were prepared for various CB types with a solid to fluid ratio of 1:20 inside screw cap extractor 
bottles. The extraction bottles were rotated at 30 rpm for 24 h by means of a VELP Scientific agitation 
apparatus in an end over end manner. The liquid and solid phases were separated by filtering through a 
0.6 µm glass fiber filter, and the leachates were transferred to centrifugal tubes for analysis. The 
leachates were then analyzed using the ICP-MS for metals.  
5.2.3 Results and discussion 
5.2.3.1 Mass and density of encapsulated CBs   
To determine the mass of unused and used CBs, batches of 50, 100, and 200 unused CBs and a batc h 
of 50 used CBs were weighed. The average mass was determined from each batch, and, therefore, the 
approximate mass of one unused and one used CB was calculated, as shown in Table 5.2. 3. The mass 
of one unused CB was found to be approximately 0.420 g, whi le the mass of one used CB is 
approximately 0.830 g. There is a reasonable variance between the two samples, which is due to the 
excess tobacco and paper present in used CBs.  
The Australian standards [215] and ASTM5 [216]  were followed to determine the bulk density for 
unused, used, bitumen encapsulated, and wax encapsulated CB samples. The bulk density for bitumen 
encapsulated CBs was found to be 708.33 kg m-3 in contrast to 781.46 kg m-3 for wax encapsulated CBs. 
In the case of unused CBs, bulk density was 150.16 kg m -3 and 154.286 kg m-3 for used CBs.  The 
obtained values for bulk density are essential when encapsulated CBs are intended for use as aggregate 
in construction material.  
Table 5.2.3. Calculation of material content of CBs encapsulated with bitumen and wax.

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===
 
113 
 
 
5.2.3.2 Encapsulation material content 
The average bitumen content of a CB encapsulated with bitumen was found to be 69.15%. In contrast, 
the wax content of a CB encapsulated with wax was found to be 77.1% (Table 5.2.3). There is a 
difference of 7.95% between the penetration percentage of the wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs. This 
indicates that the bitumen does not penetrate through the paper and/or the filter as effectively as the 
wax. This could be due to the much higher viscosity of bitumen than wax that makes it harder to 
penetrate, as one study found that the viscosity of bitumen had a large effect on its absorption into 
crumb rubber [217].  
5.2.3.3 Water absorption 
One of the vital properties of encapsulated CBs is water absorption, as the results will determine 
whether toxic chemicals and metals will escape if the material is penetrated by water. Dried, wax -
encapsulated, and bitumen-encapsulated CB samples were completely submerged in water for 24 h and 
48 hr to determine the degree of water absorption. To ensure the sample surfaces were completely dried 
after the soaking process, the samples were dried using a paper towel and then weighed. The water 
absorption values obtained could also be used to indicate the resistance to freeze-thaw action. A highly 
absorptive aggregate is unacceptable in hot mix asphalt, and if aggregate has a water absorption value 
of 5% or higher, it could be defined as a highly absorptive aggregate [218]. Table 5.2. 4 presents the 
water absorption results for dried, bitumen encapsulated, and wax encapsulated CBs for 24  h and 48 h 
testing. 
Table 5.2.4. Water absorption of dried, bitumen encapsulated and wax encapsulated CBs for 24-hr and 48-hr 
testing. 
Properties  Dried CB Bitumen encapsulated CB Wax encapsulated CB 
  Test 1 
24 h

Water absorption (%) 382.98 220.69 3.59 3.68 5.02 4.24 
Water absorption drop (%) - - 99 98 99 99 
The results show that the encapsulation process of CBs significantly reduces the water absorption of 
dried CBs. For 24 h testing, the water absorption decreased from 382.98% for dried CBs to 3.59% and 
5.02% for bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs, respectively. While for 48 h testing, the water absorption 
decreased from 220.69% for dried CBs to 3.68% and 4.24% for bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs, 
respectively. The results show that the encapsulated CBs behave as hydrophobic materials, meaning 

=== PDF PAGE 132 ===
 
114 
 
 
water cannot be absorbed by the encapsulated CB filter. Hence, it can be deduced that the 3.59% and 
5.02% (24 h) water absorption found for bitumen and wax encapsulate d CBs is truly the surface water 
content. To place this in perspective, the water absorption drop was determined for both wax and 
bitumen encapsulated CBs relative to the water absorption for dried CBs (Table 5.2.4), and a 99% water 
absorption drop was discovered for CBs when encapsulated with bitumen and wax (24 h). The other 
1% can be interpreted as the moisture content developed during testing.  
5.2.3.4 Microstructural analysis 
The penetration of the bitumen and wax during the encapsulation process is an important factor in 
achieving the minimum escape of toxic chemicals from within the encapsulated CBs. In this 
investigation, encapsulated CB samples were cut in half, and images were taken at three different 
magnifications to allow observation of the internal structure of dried, bitumen, and wax encapsulated 
CBs, as shown in Fig. 5.2.4. 
 
Fig. 5.2.4. CBs in an optical microscope (a) dried, (b) wax encapsulated, and (c) bitumen encapsulated. 
(a) 
(b) 
(c) 

=== PDF PAGE 133 ===
 
115 
 
 
The images taken by the microscope of the dried CBs show the fibrous nature of the cellulose acetate 
filter. There is also discoloration of the middle part of the filter due to being smoked. The internal 
surface of the bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs is characterized by an uneven and undulating surface 
with many small peaks and valleys. There are no breaks or discontinuities in the internal distribution of 
the bitumen except for around the edges of the CBs where the paper surrounds the filter. The 500 
microns and 1 mm photos show that the bitumen and wax are very evenly spread throughout the entire 
filter and that the bitumen and wax have successfully penetrated the CB filter.  
In addition to the microscopic images, Bruker SkyScan 1275 micro-X-ray Computed Tomography (CT) 
images were taken of a dried CB and bitumen encapsulated CB sample (Fig. 5.2.5). Fig. 5.2.6 represents 
random 2D section slices of a dried CB and bitumen encapsulated CB in which the black pixels 
represent pores. As shown in Fig. 5.2. 6, the pores within the dried CB drastically decrease when 
encapsulated with bitumen. The encapsulation process effectively and completely penetrates the 
bitumen inside the filter and creates a dense and water -repellent material. Micro -X-ray CT provides 
data related to the quantity and orientation of pores within the structure. Porosity analysis is performed 
on white and black pixels within the object. The program can classify pores as being a number of black 
pixels encased by white pixels and differentiate between closed pores (bubbles) and open pores 
(connecting channels) [139] . Images from the mi cro-CT were used, and the solids and voids were 
separated to obtain the porosity; the porosity of selected samples was estimated in which a 66% porosity 
was found for the dried CB, and a 0.5% porosity was found for the CB encapsulated with bitumen. 
Hence, it can be seen that the encapsulation process significantly reduces the porosity.

5.2.3.5 Leachate analysis of CB samples 
A leachate analysis was performed following the ABLP method to identify and quantify the metals 
leached from wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs, and analyse the relationship between the pH of the 
solution and the metal concentration leached, as well as to investigate whether the encapsulation process 
of CBs prevents or reduces the leachate of metals found within CBs. The selected metals for this study 
include Be, Mg, Al, Se, As, Pb, V, Mn, Fe, Co, Ni, Cu, Cr, Ag, Zn, Mo, Cd, Ti, Th, and U for wax and 
bitumen encapsulated CBs, and are insignificant.  
Metals Al, Cr, Fe, Ni, and Cu, and metals Mg and K are presented separately due to the high variance 
in metal concentration in leachate. While the leachate concentrations for Al, Cr, Fe, Ni, and Cu are in 
the range of 0.001 to 0.954 mg L -1, the leachate concentrations for Mg and K are between 3.442 and 
105.316 mg L-1. As demonstrated in Figs. 5.2. 7 and 5.2.8, the findings imply that the variance in pH 
influences the behavior of the metal concentration leached from the bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs 
for both the broken and unbroken samples. Generally, pH 2.9 resulted in the highest metal 
contamination for all sample types, followed by pH 5.0 and pH 9.2. This implies that wax and bitumen 
encapsulated CBs are more susceptible to leaching metals in the presence of highly acidic water. This 
shows that different environmental conditions play a major role in the leaching behavior of CBs and 
validates that all leachate tests should be performed for various environmental scenarios.  
Comparing the leachate concentrations of metals from wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs, wax 
encapsulated CBs depict higher leachate concentrations. For Al (pH 2.9), the leachate of bitumen 
encapsulated CBs for broken and unbroken is 0.263 mg L-1 and 0.230 mg L-1, respectively, whereas, 
for wax encapsulated CBs, it is 0.954 and 0.943 mg L -1 for broken and unbroken, respectively. This is 
due to bitumen having a higher cohesiveness compared to paraffin wax, which assists in trapping metals 
more effectively.

No relationship between the leachate concentrations of wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs for broken

higher leachate concentration than the undamaged samples (unbroken). This shows that the damage 
reduces the effectiveness of immobilizing the leachate of metals. However, it is to be noted that there 
is not a significant variance to the intact samples, with the largest drop in performance being only 
2.63%. This shows that if the encapsulated CBs were to be damaged, they would still be able to contain 
the metals within the CB.

Tables 5.2.5, 5.2.6, and 5.2. 7 depict the leachate results for metals in bitumen and wax encapsulated 
CBs for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 9.2, respectively. The leachate concentrations of wax and bitumen 
encapsulated CBs were compared to the Industrial Waste Resource Guidelines [173] and were found to 
be below the regulatory threshold limits for heavy metals for distinct pH values. To further highlight 
the triviality of the leachate concentrations, the results were compared to the Australian drinking water 
heavy metal guideline thresholds [213].  
To investigate the variations in concentrations of dried CBs before encapsulation and after bitumen and 
paraffin wax encapsulation, the leaching drop was determined. The findings in Tables 5.2.5, 5.2.6, and 
5.2.7 show that the leaching behavior of CBs is considerably lower after being encapsulated with wax 
and bitumen. This is the result of sealing the CBs with viscous and insoluble materials, which prevents 
the release of metals or the intrusion of any liquid. For bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs, the leaching 
of metals Be, Cd, Se, As, Ti, Mo, Pb, Th, U, and Ag were immobilized by 100% while metals Mg, K, 
Fe, Cr, Co, Al were hindered between 70% to 98% for pH 2.9. Cu demonstrated a leaching drop of 2% 
for bitumen encapsulated CBs, and, although the leaching drop is trivial, it must be noted that the 
leachate concentrations are less than 0.05 mg/L. The samples for pH 5.0 demonstrated similar leaching 
drops. Metals Be, Se, Cd, As, Ti, Mo, Th, U, and Ag were immobilized by 100% for both wax and 
bitumen encapsulated CBs. While pH 9.2 resulted in the most effective immobilization of metals, 
whereby all metals were hindered by more than 80% for both sample types. Encapsulating CBs resulted 
in a significant decrease of 100% immobilization of toxic metal contamination, which can be an 
environmental advantage compared to the littering of CBs. To investigate whether the condition of a

construction of asphalt concrete, the possible damaged state (broken) will not affect the leaching 
behavior of the toxic waste.

5.2.4 Conclusions and recommendations 
5.2.4.1 Conclusion 
The objective of this paper was to carry out a comprehensive leachate analysis to examine the 
effectiveness and efficiency of the encapsulation method for the prevention of leachates from CBs and 
investigate the physical properties of wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs. The leachate analysis was 
conducted using the Australian Bottle Leaching Procedure (ABLP) [212] for pH values 2.9, 5.0, and 
9.2. The metals studied included Be, Mg, Al, Se, As, Pb, V, Mn, Fe, Co, Ni, Cu, Cr, Ag, Zn, Mo, Cd, 
Ti, Th, and U. Numerous experiments were conducted in accordance with the appropriate Australian 
standards to determine the physical properties of wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs. Based on the 
experimental studies, the following conclusions can be drawn: 
• The wax content of wax encapsulated CBs was found to be 77.1%, and the bitumen content of 
bitumen encapsulated CBs was found to be 69.15%, thereby demonstrating the effectiveness of 
the encapsulation process.  
• For 24 h testing, water absorption decreased from 382.98% for used CBs to 3.59% and 5.02% 
for bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs. That is a water absorption drop of 99% for bitumen 
and wax encapsulated CBs. Therefore, it can be presumed that the water absorption value  is 
relative to the surface water content (moisture content) and, essentially, not the absorption of the filter. 

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
 
123 
 
 
• Water penetration plays a vital role in spreading leachate containing toxic materials into the 
environment. Water absorption of only 3.59% for bitumen encapsulated CBs is exceptionally 
low, and when placed in an AC mixture, bitumen encapsulated CBs will be encapsulated with 
bitumen conse cutively. Therefore, signifying an almost 0% leachability possibility of toxic 
chemicals and metals during the use and disposal of the waste.  
• Micro-X-ray CT imaging was used to study and analyze the porosity and pore structure of CBs, 
and a substantial decrease in porosity from 66% to 0.5% was determined when CBs were 
encapsulated with bitumen. 
• The leachate concentrations of metals Be, V, Co, As, Se, Mo Ag, Cd, Ti, Pb, Th, and U for 
bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs were found to be below 0.001 mg/L, and, therefore, are of 
insignificant concern. 
• The findings suggest that the variance in pH influences the behavior of the metal concentration 
leached from bitumen and wax encapsulated CBs for both broken and unbroken samples. 
Generally, pH 2.9 resulted in the highest metal contamination for all sample types, followed by 
pH 5.0 and pH 9.2. 
• In comparing the leachate concentrations of heavy metals from wax and bitumen encapsulated 
CBs, generally, wax encapsulated CBs depicted higher leachate concentrations. This is due to 
bitumen having a higher cohesiveness compared to paraffin wax, which assists in trapping 
heavy metals more effectively 
• The results showed that damaged samples (broken) generally result in a higher leachate 
concentration than undamaged samples (unbroken). For pH 5.0, bitumen encapsulated CBs 
(broken) leached 0.098 mg L -1 for Al and 0.051 mg L -1 for Cu, whereas, for bitumen 
encapsulated CBs (unbroken), Al leached 0.150 mg L -1 and Cu leached 0.032 mg L -1. This 
shows that the damage reduces the effectiveness of immobilizing the leachate of metals. 
• The leachate results for wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs were found to be insignificant 
compared to the limits set by the EPA Victoria solid industrial waste guidelines and, therefore, 
can be categorized as non-hazardous. 
• The findings show that the leaching behavior of CBs is considerably lower after being 
encapsulated with wax and bitumen. This is the result of sealing the CBs with viscous and 
insoluble materials, which prevents the release of metals or the intrusion of any liquid. 
• Many health and safety issues arise from the storage of CBs, the process of encapsulating CBs, 
and their inclusion into manufactured materials. Due to their toxic nature, correct OH&S 
processes need to be pursued with all aspects of the encapsulation process.  
• This research has revealed unknown physical properties like mass, density, encapsulation 
materials content, and water absorption of wax encapsulated CBs and bitumen encapsulated 

=== PDF PAGE 142 ===
 
124 
 
 
CBs. Microscopic analysis has confirmed the unif ormity of the encapsulation process. 
Comprehensive leachate analysis has proved the efficacy of the encapsulation method in 
reducing the leaching of heavy metals from encapsulated CBs. As heavy metal leaching is one 
of the key environmental issues worldwide, this research has provided a sustainable solution 
that will contribute to solving global cigarette butt pollution. This research has also reviewed a 
potential application of encapsulated CBs in asphalt concrete, and promising results from 
laboratory investigation have supported the recycling method of encapsulated CBs in asphalt 
concrete. 
5.2.4.2 Recommendations 
The following recommendations are made based on the current study: 
• The leachate testing performed should be repeated to confirm the results and trends obtained. 
In addition, different damage scenarios could be tested to more accurately show the effect on 
leachate properties. Most importantly, when they become available, testing methods that are 
applicable to solid wastes should be used to repeat t he testing in this paper to achieve more 
accurate leachate properties.  
• Only one type of bitumen was used in this research, bituminous macadam base course A10E. 
This could be an important factor in the properties of the final encapsulated CB, so 
investigations need to be made into whether the grade of bitumen affects the penetration, water 
absorption, and, in turn, the leachate properties.  
• The interaction between encapsulated CBs added to manufactured materials and how this 
affects the physical and chemical properties was found in this research. Changes to the 
encapsulation process, such as the removal of paper around the filter, are also important to 
investigate as such factors affect the water absorption and material content properties and could 
also affect the leachate properties. Testing whether the compression of the CB filters before 
encapsulation could affect different properties is another interesting factor.  
• Finally, although a method of encapsulation that results in negligible water absorption for  
bitumen has been developed in laboratory conditions, further research is required into the 
feasibility and method required for a large -scale industrial approach to the recycling of CBs 
through the encapsulation process. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 143 ===
 
125 
 
 
Chapter 6: Polycyclic Aromatic 
Hydrocarbons (PAHs) in Fired Clay Bricks 
Incorporating Cigarette Butts 
Abstract  
Cigarette butts (CBs) are the most common littered waste in the world and may contain polycyclic 
aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) from the incomplete combustion of tobacco during burning. Therefore, 
to investigate the potential PAH residual remaining in fired clay bricks (FCBs) incorporating CBs and 
examine the environmental impact of utilizing toxic waste in the production of FCBs, a comprehensive 
PAH extraction analysis was condu cted. The Soxhlet extraction method was utilized to conduct a 
qualitative and quantitative analysis of sixteen toxic Environmental Protection Authority (EPA) 
Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons (PAHs) in FCB samples incorporating CBs using gas 
chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC–MS). The concentrations of the mean total (Σ)PAHs for FCBs 
incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by weight (wt) were found to be 0.183, 0.180, 0.242, 
0.234, and 0.463 µg mL-1. As expected, PAHs with higher water solubility and volatility, naphthalene, 
fluorene, anthracene, pyrene, fluoranthene, and chrysene were found at higher concentrations compared 
to lipophilic PAHs. The ΣPAH concentrations for all five FCB –CB mixes were well below the EPA 
Victoria solid waste hazard categorization threshold for industrial waste. Moreover, the samples were 
studied for their carbon content using the carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and sulfur (CHNS) analyzer and 
thermogravimetric analysis (TGA). The results confirm an almost 100% combustion process of CBs 
during the firing process. A content less than 0.3% suggests that all carbon within the FCB–CB mixture 
relatively disappeared during the firing process up to 1050 °C. However, further research regarding the 
emission of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) during the production of FCBs incorporating CBs 
should be conducted. 
The research presented in this chapter has been published in the following: 
Kurmus, H, Mohajerani, A and Grist, S. 2021. Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons in fired clay bricks 
incorporating cigarette butts. Materials, 14(8), 2032. https://doi.org/10.3390/ma14082032. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===
 
126 
 
 
6.1 Introduction 
Globally, over 5.4 trillion cigarettes are produced, and approximately one-third of cigarette butts (CBs) 
are littered into the nearby environment [125, 219, 220]. In Australia, CBs are the most common littered 
waste under the miscellaneous category, representing 91.5% [8, 125]. When littered, CBs persist in the 
environment for up to 10 years due to the composition of the filter. The fi lter of a CB is made from 
cellulose acetate, and, although cellulose is biodegradable, the acetate content prevents the cellulose 
from biological decomposition in an open environment [40, 125]. A study conducted in a controlled 
laboratory condition found that it took up to 720 days for a CB to lose 30% to 35% of its total weight 
while decomposing [221].  
In cigarette smoke, it is possible to classify more than 4000 chemical components generated during 
burning or distilled from the tobacco [125]. Essentially, the role of the filter of the CB is to trap and 
absorb the particulate smoke components, consisting of more than 3500 non-volatile and semi-volatile 
chemical compounds. The main toxic compounds include polycyclic aromati c hydrocarbons (PAHs), 
nicotine, metals, catechols, carbonyls, alcohols, nicotine alkaloids, and compounds specific to 
Solanaceae [11, 30]. Therefore, when CBs are improperly disposed of, they pose a toxicant risk to the 
global environment and urban and aquatic life, whereby harmful chemicals are leached [50, 149] . The 
leachate of the toxic compounds may eventually contaminate surface water and groundwater [151, 222], 
and ultimately bioaccumulate in the human food chain and wildlife [17, 18].  
Based on several years of research, Mohajerani et al. [1]  proposed that the CB conundrum can 
potentially be eliminated by incorporating 1% CBs into 2.5% of the world’s total FCB production. 
Various mechanical and physical tests were conducted to determine the practicality and competency of 
incorporating toxic waste in the production of FCBs, and the results were promising [22, 23, 115, 157, 
176]. The tests included compressive strength, density, water absorption, initial rate of absorption, 
efflorescence, shrinkage, energy savings, thermal conductivity, and gas emissions. The research 
continued with the implementation phase of recycling CBs in FC Bs, whereby several sterilization 
methods and odor elimination techniques were reviewed, CB collection systems were explored, and an 
implementation procedure was proposed on an industrial scale [2] . However, an environmental issue 
that may evolve through the addition of CBs in the construction of FCBs is the potential leachate of 
heavy metals and toxic chemicals during the use and disposal of the material. In 2020, Kurmus and 
Mohajerani Kurmus and Mohajerani [126] conducted a comprehensive leachate study of heavy metals 
from FCBs incorporating various percentages of CBs. The modified FCBs were found to be below the 
regulatory threshold limits and categorized as non-hazardous waste when compared to the EPA Victoria 
industrial waste guidelines. Regarding PAHs in FCBs modified with CBs, no s tudies have been 
conducted.  

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===
 
127 
 
 
PAHs in CBs are dangerous compounds produced through the incomplete combustion and pyrolysis of 
tobacco. They consist of two or more aromatic rings and are hydrophobic in nature with very low water 
solubility. PAHs are a concern, considering that they bioaccumulate and linger in the environment, with 
carcinogenic, teratogenic, and mutagenic properties [223, 224] . In 2008, 28 PAHs were identified as 
priority pollutants by the National Waste Minimization Program, a project which was funded by the US 
Environmental Protection Agency [225]. In a study conducted by Dobaradaran et al. Dobaradaran et al. 
[222], it was found that PAHs, particularly fluorine, acenaphthylene, naphthalene, and acenaphthene, 
are released into the environment from freshly littered CBs. In addition to CBs, PAH contamination in 
soil is a common issue, because soil is less receptive to chemical and biological degradation and the 
PAHs tend to absorb tightly into the organic matter present in t he soil. Moreover, the recalcitrance of 
PAHs towards treatment increases with prolonged aging time, promoting the sequestration of PAH 
molecules into micropores [226].  
Thus, the aim of this study was to conduct a comprehensive evaluation of PAHs in FCBs incorporated 
with CBs to examine the potential environmental impact of utilizing this toxic waste in the production 
of FCBs. In this study, a direct Soxhlet extraction method was employed using dichloromethane as the 
solvent. The aim of the extraction process is to effectively remove the anal yte (PAHs) from its matrix, 
with minimal solvent usage [227] . The extract was analyzed for sixteen US EPA priority PAH 
compounds using gas chromatography– mass spectrometry (GC–MS) in accordance with the US EPA 
Method 3540C [228]. The PAH concentrations were then compared to the Environmental Protection 
Authority (EPA) Victorian solid industrial waste hazard categorization and management thresholds 
[173] to assess the suitability of FCBs incorporating CBs. Furthermore, the FCB samples incorporating 
CBs were studied for their carbon content using the carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and sulfur (CHNS) 
analyzer and thermogravimetric analysis (TGA). 
6.2 Materials and Methods 
6.2.1 Materials and Brick Manufacturing Methodology 
Soil and CBs are the raw materials that were used in this study. A sieve analysis was conducted to 
classify the soil, and, according to unified soil classification, the soil was categorized as sandy silty clay 
(MC) [127]. The soil material was obtained from PGH Bricks and Pavers (Melbourne, Australia) and 
the CBs, of distinct brands and sizes, were provided by Butt Out Australia Pty Ltd (Melbourne, 
Australia). The raw materials, CBs and soil, were initially oven -dried at 105 °C for 24 h. Various 
percentages (0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by weight (wt)) of CBs were then added and uniformly 
mixed with the soil for 25 min in a mechanical mixer at a moisture content of 15.5%. The fast -mixing 
process caused the CBs to shred and homogenously integrate into the soil mixture. Prior to being placed 
in the oven at 105 °C for 24 h, the samples were compacted in cylindrical molds at a compaction 

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===
 
128 
 
 
pressure of 240 kPa. The final step involved firing the brick samples in the furnace at 1050 °C for 3 h. 
For each batch, three replicate samples were prepared, tested, and the mean value was reported. The 
compressive strength, water absorption, initial rate of absorption, shrinkage, and density property 
results for the FCBs incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs by wt. can be found in Kurmus 
and Mohajerani Kurmus and Mohajerani [115]. 
The chemical and mineralogical composition of the CB brick samples were determined through AXS 
D8 Endeavor wide -angle X-ray diffractometer (XRD) (Bruker, MA, USA) and AXS S4 Pioneer 
spectrometer X-ray fluorescence (XRF) (Bruker, MA, USA). Powder samples were prepared by 
crushing and sieving the FCB samples incorporating CBs to 10 µm in size. The XRD was operated at 
45 kV and 35 mA in reflection scanning mode from 6° to 90° for a total scan time of 29 min. 
6.2.2 Carbon Analysis 
The CHNS-2400 Elemental Analyser (PerkinElmer, MA, USA) was used to analyze the total carbon, 
hydrogen, nitrogen, and sulfur content in FCBs incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, 2%, and 5% CBs 
by wt. The purpose of this investigation was to determine whether carbon was trapped within the core 
of the brick structure from the potential incomplete combustion of CBs during the firing process. 
Therefore, for this experiment, only the extracted core samples were analyzed. The core of the 100 -
mm-diameter bricks was extracted using a 50 -mm drill-saw, as shown in Fig . 6.1. The extracted core 
bricks were crushed and sieved through a 1.14-mm sieve, and the quadrat sampling method was 
employed to select a homogeneously distributed sample for testing. 
CHNS analysis was conducted by combusting sa mples of 5 mg at a high temperature of 850 °C in a 
chamber in the presence of oxygen. The occurrence of oxygen allows individual elements to combine 
with oxygen and form gaseous byproducts and water vapor such as nitrogen (N2), sulfur dioxide (SO2), 
water (H2O), and carbon dioxide (CO 2). The gases are then captured in the gas control zone and 
homogenized. The homogenized gases are depressurized through a column, and the separation approach 
is used to elute the gases, which are measured and detected by a thermal conductivity detector. In this 
study, three replicate samples were prepared, and the mean average was reported. 

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===
 
129 
 
 
 
Fig. 6.1. The core of the brick extracted for CHNS analysis. 
Thermogravimetric analysis (TGA) and differential thermal analysis (DTA) wer e performed using the 
TGA 8000 Autosampler (PerkinElmer, MA, USA) to monitor the mass loss of the brick samples 
incorporating CBs. For the thermal analysis, the samples were heated from 30 °C to 850 °C at a constant 
heating rate of 20 °C min-1 in a 15 mL-per-min flow of nitrogen. For the purpose of this investigation, 
the brick samples were crushed to a particle size of <75 µm. All samples were tested under the same 
conditions. 
6.2.3 Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbon (PAH) Analysis 
6.2.3.1 Extraction Method 
The Soxhlet extraction technique is a widely employed method to extract PAHs from sediments and 
soils [229]. Therefore, the brick samples were extracted in accordance with the USEPA Soxhlet 
Extraction Method 3540C [228]. In the basic Soxhlet extraction test, 15 g of crushed brick samples of 
particle size <1 mm is placed into a cellulose thimble, which is then extracted using 300 mL of solvent 
via the reflux cycle. In this c ase, dichloromethane was used as the solvent. The whole assembly was 
heated for 8 h using an isomantle. The extracts from the Soxhlet extractor were concentrated to 10 mL 
using the rotary evaporator and then diluted. The concentrated samples were mixed wit h five 
isotopically labeled internal standard solutions to make a final concentration of 10 µg mL-1. The samples 
were stored at 4 °C for GC–MS measurement. 
6.2.3.2 Calibration Standards 
PAH calibration standards, including five types of isotopically labele d internal standards and 16 types 
of PAHs, were used for quantification. The PAH mix (2000 µg/mL) was purchased from Supelco and 
included acenaphthene, dibenz[a,h]anthracene, anthracene, chrysene, benz[ a]anthracene, 
benzo[b]fluoranthene, fluorene, benzo[ k]fluoranthene, naphthalene, benzo[ a]pyrene, phenanthrene, 
benzo[ghi]perylene, pyrene, fluoranthene, acenaphthylene, and indeno [1,2,3-cd ]pyrene. The internal 


=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
 
130 
 
 
standards (2000 µg/mL)  were also purchased from Supelco and included benz[ a]anthracene-d12 
solution, chrysene-d12 solution, naphthalene-d8 solution, perylene-d12 solution, and phenanthrene-d10 
solution. 
Five-point standard calibration solutions were prepared to ensure the accuracy and precision of the 
PAHs for quantitative and qualitative analysis. The  standard calibration solutions were measured at 
levels ranging from 1.25 to 25 µg mL -1 and internal standards of 10 µg/mL. Each analytical series 
included one blank test. The 16 PAHs and five internal standards in the samples were defined by the 
precursor ions and retention time, as shown in Table 6.1.

6.2.3.3 GC–MS Analysis 

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===
 
131 
 
 
A GC–MS system connecting a gas chromatograph with a triple-quadrupole mass spectrometer and 
equipped with an auto-sampler and sample preparation robot (Agilent 7000 Series GC–MS triple quad 
system) was used to analyze the PAH compounds. Two Agilent DB -5MS columns 5 m long and 0.25 
mm in diameter were used with mid-column backflush. The multi-mode injector was operated in solvent 
elimination mode with injection at 5 °C, ramping at 600 °C  min-1 to 325 °C. The oven temperatures 
were between 5 and 325 °C after an initial holding time of 1 min at 25 °C  min-1. To attain an initial 
oven temperature of 5 °C, CO2 was used as the carrier gas for cooling at a constant fl ow of 3 mL min-
1. The cryogenics mode offered the ultimate conditions for the solvent vent mode. The injection volume 
was set to 0.2 μL and the electronic energy to 70 eV. Each run took approximately 17 min. 
6.3 Results and Discussion 
6.3.1 Characterization of Brick Samples 
The chemical composition of the raw clay material and bricks incorporating CBs is presented in Table 
6.2. The data provided in Table 6.2 show that the major compounds in the raw clay are silicon dioxide 
(SiO2), saluminum oxide (Al 2O3), iron(III) oxide (Fe 2O3), and potassium oxide (K 2O). Similarly, the 
chemical compounds found in the FCB samples incorporating various percentages of CBs were SiO 2, 
Al2O3, Fe 2O3, and K 2O. The XRD analysis results for the raw clay soil and FCBs specimens 
incorporating CBs are presented in Fig . 6.2. The XRD peak at 26.7° indicates that the soil and brick 
samples incorporating CBs are mainly made of quartz, which is consistent with the XRF data presented 
in Table 6. 2, demonstrating the presence of a large amount of silica. The second -highest peak 
corresponding to the raw clay soil is muscovite, a hydrated phyllosilicate mineral of aluminum and 
potassium, which are present in quantities of 19.95% and 2.87%, respectively, in the XRF results.
