The results in Table 7. 4 show that Salmonella spp, E.coli, Coagulase +ve Staphylococcus spp, 
Clostridia spp, and Legionella spp were not found in any of the samples. The investigation of Coagulase 
+ve Staphylococcus spp for sample 1 (the freshest CBs of this investigation) was not present in 
comparison to pilot investigation 1 , where it was present in sample 2 (the fresh CBs of that 
investigation). This could be because the sample was slightly fresher or that the bacteria were only 
present within the sample collected. In this investigation, a small count of Pseudomonas aeruginos a 
was found in sample 1. As this species of bacteria is primarily spread by hand, its presence may be due 
to unclean hands that transfer the bacteria to CBs. A small count of Enterococcus faecalis was found in 
the control sample, which was likely contaminated by the preparation environment, as this bacterium 
was not present in the control sample of pilot investigation 1. 
In sample 2, Listeria spp was detected from the older CBs. Listeria spp is known to survive in waste 
water and sewerage sludge for a long period of time [278]. This sample may have been contaminated 
by this species of bacteria, where its characteristics of surviving for a long period of time can be 
observed.  
Lastly, for Bacillus spp, counts of 70,000 cfu/g were found on control sample 1, 39,000 cfu/g were 
found on sample 2, and 440 cfu/g were found on sample 3 (Fig. 7. 11). These higher quantities are not 
unusual for soil. As Bacillus spp have been recorded to grow on fresh and cured tobacco leaves [279], 
this could be a plausible reason for its presence. When comparing the dried CBs from sample 3 with 
the other samples, the count of Bacillus spp was significantly lower. This indicates that the majority of 
Bacillus spp present did not survive the drying process. It was noted that sample 4’s preparation method 
was easier than sample 3’s and was almost as effective at eliminating the Bacillus spp, counts. 

=== PDF PAGE 184 ===
 
166 
 
 
 
Fig. 7.11. Comparison of Bacillus spp present in samples. 
The workers dealing with cigarette butts should wear the appropriate PPE and follow OH&S procedures 
to mitigate the potential risk that th e pathogens and chemicals in CBs pose. Some of the PPE 
recommended include wearing long sleeves and gloves and the usage of an appropriate mask.  
The results of both pilot investigations 1 and 2 should in no way be interpreted as a comprehensive 
study. Onl y six types of bacteria were investigated for pilot investigation 1 , and 10 types were 
investigated for pilot investigation 2. In addition, only a small number of cigarettes were tested. About 
75 CBs were collected for each sample for pilot investigation 1, and 150 CBs were collected for each 
sample for pilot investigation 2. This quantity was then divided to investigate each species of bacteria, 
thus limiting the scope of the findings.  
This paper suggests that further investigation be conducted in this area, particularly given the positive 
confirmation that Enterococcus faecalis, Coagulase +ve Staphylococcus spp, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, 
Listeria spp, and Bacillus spp were present in such a small population of CBs. 
Attempts at determining a way of investigating the presence of viruses failed, as it is impractical to 
develop a method to utilise for testing and almost impossible to verify if the produced results would be 
accurate or that the virus presented a viable infectious threat. 
7.6.2.3. Presence of Viruses 
The possible presence of viruses, such as hepatitis A, B, and C, as well as HIV on CBs was initially 
investigated. However, hepatitis A can only be spread through the ingestion of food or water 
contaminated by the faecal matter of an infected person and is rarely fatal [280]. As a result, it is 
incredibly unlikely that any CB would be contaminated by the virus. Similarly, hepatitis B has been 


=== PDF PAGE 185 ===
 
167 
 
 
found in trace amounts in saliva (and therefore could contaminate CBs), however, this concentration is 
considered far too insignificant to transmit the virus [281] . Hepatitis C can only be spread through an 
infected person's blood directly entering the body of a non-infected person [2 82]. As CBs will not be 
ingested or come into contact with any worker’s orifices (as they will be wearing PPE) Hepatitis C can 
be disregarded as a threat. HIV/AIDS is also a serious virus, which can only be spread through blood -
to-blood contact or through contact with an infected person’s various bodily fluids (not including saliva) 
[283]. As a result, HIV is highly unlikely to be present on CBs, and cannot be spread if handlers are 
protected with PPE. 
7.6.3. Potential Cleaning Methods for CBs 
7.6.3.1. Naphthalene  
Naphthalene is a white crystalline powder that produces a well -recognised smell (mothballs). The 
chemical is isolated from coal tar and has a relatively short half-life in the atmosphere of 3 to 8 h [284]. 
Naphthalene is the major active ingredient in ‘toilet cakes’ and is used as a fumigant [285]. Naphthalene 
is a suspected carcinogen, and exposure to very high concentrations can lead to damage to blood cells 
and haemolytic anaemia. However, exposure to low concentrations in the open air should be safe for 
adults and children older than three [286]. Naphthalene and its derivatives have proven antimicrobial 
capacities [287]. In addition, a common dye, β -naphthol, possesses effective antimicrobial properties 
[288]. Mkpenie et al. (2008) confirmed this, testing azo -2 naphthol and 2-naphthol on five pathogenic 
microorganisms: Escherichia coli , Staphylococcus aureus , streptococcus faecalis, pseudomonas 
aeruginosa, and bacillus subtilis [289]. The results demonstrated that both chemicals were highly 
effective at killing all the microorganisms tested. Considering Naphthalene’s short half-life in the 
atmosphere and antimicrobial abilities, in addition to the ease in which ‘mothballs’ and ‘toilet cakes’ 
can be located and purchased, these products are potentially a very quick, easy, and cheap method for 
cleaning CBs, as seen in Section 7.6.2.2 (Table 7.4). This paper suggests that toilet cakes or mothballs 
could be placed into the bags that dry CBs are collected in (i.e. by the collector) and tied. The fumigation 
effects would then likely kill any possible bacteria. Care should be taken not to inhale the fumes released 
when the bag is opened, as per the reasons stated above. The fumes produced should be minimal if only 
a few toilet cakes/mothballs are used. This method would be cheap, simple and effective. 
The following methods are more suitable for large scale-cleaning plants. 
7.6.3.2. Ozone 
Ozone (𝑂𝑂3) is an unstable gas with powerful oxidising properties. It is predominately produced 
naturally as a result of ultraviolet rays irradiating oxygen molecules in the stratosphere, where up to 

=== PDF PAGE 186 ===
 
168 
 
 
90% of all ozone is located [290]. Research has shown that ozone is an effective antibacterial agent and 
that it is useful against both gram positive and gram-negative bacteria, as well as spores and vegetative 
cells [291]. This antimicrobial power is predominately a result of the oxidising potential of ozone, where 
molecular ozone or its decomposition products, such as radical hydroxyl ions, react with bacteria/virus 
cells and disrupt normal functions, thereby resulting in inactivation or destruction [292] . Ozone 
generators are widely available, and therefore, could be considered easily accessible in most situations. 
Ozone has been used to sterilise equipment and containers in the food industry for many years, including 
in Japan, where the method has been utilised in food manufacturing facilities since 1982 [293] . Using 
ozone to sanitize surfaces, equipment, and even food is quite safe, as even though ozone is highly toxic, 
it quickly disassociates into oxygen [294]. 
Ozone is effective as an antimicrobial due to its potent oxidisation capabilities [295]. The third oxygen 
atom in ozone is only loosely bonded, and, as a result, is unstable and highly reactive. It will attach 
itself to organic matter, such as bacteria, and destroy them through the process of oxidisation [296] . 
Ozone has also been shown to have a capacity to destroy a wide variety of viruses, including hepatitis 
A and influenza A [297]. Treating fruits and vegetables with ozone has also been shown to increase the 
shelf life of the products [298, 299]. It is essential to remember that ozone is a toxic gas, and can be 
lethal in sufficiently high doses [300]. Therefore, it is essential that system designs ensure that humans 
have no exposure to ozone. 
Ozone sterilisation of surfaces can occur in two main forms: gaseous ozone and ozonated water. Objects 
can be submerged in ozonated water for treatment or can be exposed to continuously generated gas -
phase ozone. Rooms can be sealed whilst gaseous ozone is generated for sterilisation. The ozone will 
decompose into oxygen atoms at a rate that is influenced by both room temperature and the surface 
composition of any catalyst [301]. No person can be present inside a sealed room during the gaseous 
ozone sterilisation process for the reasons stated above. A gaseous ozone concentration of 25 parts-per-
million (ppm) in the air at a Relative Humidity (RH) of 90% was found to be bactericidal after an 
exposure time of 20 min [294]. A large volume of research shows that ozone has substantial oxidising, 
antibacterial, and antiviral capabilities, which suggests that it could be applied for the treatment of any 
potential bacteria, viruses, and fungal spores on CBs. Considering the need to control the moisture 
content of mixes, and the relatively complex process of producing large volumes of ozonated water, 
this paper suggests that gas phase ozone is a more appropriate method. 
7.6.3.3. Hydrogen Peroxide 
Hydrogen peroxide (𝐻𝐻2𝑂𝑂2) is a weak acid with strong oxidising capabilities, and, therefore, can be 
utilised as a powerful bleaching agent [302]. Hydrogen peroxide is commonly utilised as a sterilisation 
technique in hospital settings, where it is used to disinfect rooms, in addition to re -usable medical and 

=== PDF PAGE 187 ===
 
169 
 
 
biomedical equipment [303, 304] . It is  also commonly used in food manufacturing and industrial 
settings and, more recently, in water treatment [305]. Hydrogen peroxide quickly decomposes into 
water and oxygen (its constituent atoms) and is , therefore, a non-toxic and environmentally friendly 
replacement for room sterilisation by formaldehyde, which is an expensive and carcinogenic chemical 
[306]. Hydrogen peroxide can be employed in either a liquid or gaseous form (vaporized hydrogen 
peroxide). As a liquid, it is commonly used as a surface disinfectant and, in some concentrations, as an 
antiseptic medicine [305]. 
Vaporised hydrogen peroxide (VHP) is used for the disinfection of surfaces, re -usable medical 
equipment, and room fumigation [303, 307]. Hydrogen Peroxide, as an oxidising agent, acts by forming 
HO. Free radicals react with bacteria/virus cells and thus prevent the correct functioning of these cells. 
This results in the inhibition of  infection and replication processes [308]. An advantage of vaporised 
hydrogen peroxide when compared to gaseous ozone is that it has relatively low toxicity to humans and 
breaks down spontaneously into harmless components [305]. Vaporised hydrogen peroxide generation 
systems are widely available. As a result of its availability and sterilising properties, hydrogen peroxide 
may be an effective treatment method for any viruses, bacteria, or fungi that may be present on CBs. 
7.6.3.4. Non-Ionising Ultraviolet Light Radiation 
Short wave ultraviolet light (250  to 260 nm) has been used successfully to inactivat e microorganisms 
in water and food in addition to those located on surfaces [309]. Additionally, UVC light has been 
successfully utilised to disinfect high-touch surfaces in hospital settings [310]. Research also indicates 
that the effective reduction of bacteria on stainless steel and polypropylene surfaces can be increased 
by utilising dry heat during the UV sterilisation procedure [311]. 
UV light comprises wavelengths between 100 and 400 nm. These wavelengths are non-ionising. Short 
wave ultraviolet light (UVC) refers to UV wavelengths of between 200  and 280nm [312]. UVC is 
commonly termed the ‘germicidal spectrum’ because it is the band width of UV radiation that 
demonstrates the greatest biocidal effects on bacteria [313] . The lethal effect of UVC light is due to 
direct alteration of microbial DNA, resulting from the formation of dimers. Once the DNA is damaged, 
the cells can no longer reproduce and are inactivated [314]. 
Short wave UV lamps are widely available and are identical to fluorescent lamps; however, they lack a 
phosphor coating, which allows the glass to transfer UVC. It is important to note that short wave UV 
lamps that produce radiation of 260  nm or lower will also produce ozone and, therefore, must be 
monitored for safety [309] . UV light, predominately UVB, but in some circumstances UVA, can 
penetrate human skin and cause cyclobutene pyrimidine dimers (mainly thymine dimers), which result 
in mutations found in skin cancers [315]. In contrast, UVC light is absorbed by the outer layer of human 
skin (epidermis) and is not known to form CPD (cyclobutane pyrimidine dimers) in human skin  [316]. 

=== PDF PAGE 188 ===
 
170 
 
 
Despite this, exposure of the eye to UVC can cause incredibly painful corneal burns [316]  or result in 
the development of CPD in the first 10% of the cornea [317]. Therefore, whilst UVC lamps are 
relatively safe to use, it is essential to ensure that workers are not exposed to UV irradiation systems 
whilst they are in operation. 
7.6.3.5. Dry and Moist Heat Treatment 
Heat treatment for the sterilisation of food in the food industry has been utilised for over 90 years [318], 
as well as in healthcare settings [319]. There are two heat treatment methods, dry heat sterilisation, and 
moist heat sterilisation. Dry heat sterilisation involves purely heated air with no or little moisture, which 
kills cells via oxidisation. Temperatures usually exceed 180 °C and exposure times generally last up to 
60 min [320]. Temperature increases result in a reduced time of exposure required to achieve 
sterilisation.  Alternatively, moist treatment sterilisation involves lower temperatures than dry heat 
sterilisation and is applied as steam under pressure, resulting in what is considered as being a generally 
more efficient sterilisation method in that highly resistant spores can be destroyed with temperatures of 
around 121 °C after approximately 30 min [320]. Dry heat sterilisation requires higher temperatures 
and longer exposure times because bacteria are far more resistant to dry heat sterilisation than moist 
heat sterilisation [318]. This is predominately a result of the thermal conductivity of water vs. air, where 
water allows more heat to be transferred into the bacteria cell. 
However, steam will only destroy cells on contact with the surface of an object, whereas radiant heat 
will still result in cell death [319] . The implications of this are that large volumes of CBs may not be 
successfully treated with the moist heat sterilisation method, as it is unlikely steam would be able to 
penetrate/access all surfaces. However, moist heat sterilisation could be utilised if relatively small 
volumes of CBs are placed in an autoclave at any given time. This would introduce additional handling 
times (i.e. repetitive loading/unloading of small samples, etc.). 
The ignition temperature of paper has been reported to be in the range of 218 to 246 °C [321] , and, 
therefore, under dry heat temperatures of 180 °C CBs would not be expected to combust. However, 
great care must be ensured if dry heat sterilisation is utilised for the treatment of CBs, as the presence 
of various chemicals may alter the ignition temperature. 
7.6.3.6. Notes on Nicotine 
Nicotine is the most common chemical found in CBs. It is highly addictive and a known carcinogen. 
Furthermore, the toxicity of Nicotine does lend it some antimicrobial power, and high concentrations 
of nicotine, diluted in phosphate-buffered saline have been found to be effective at killing a number of 
common bacteria [322] including Escherechia coli. However, the same study found that nicotine had 
little or no effect on Staphyl ococcus aureus. Similarly, it has been reported that both aqueous and 

=== PDF PAGE 189 ===
 
171 
 
 
ethanolic extracts of Nicotiana tabacum were effective at inhibiting the growth of M.tubercolosis [323]. 
Further study found that six extracts of Nicotiana tabacum (Ethanol, Acetone, Butanol, Water, n-
Hexane, and Ethyl acetate) were effective in inhibiting the growth of a number of gram positive and 
gram-negative bacteria, many of which are known pathogens [324]. 
Historically, tobacco was used to treat several ailments. A review of 128 cases of tobacco treatment, 
published between 1785 and 1860, identified several allegedly successful applications of the plant to 
treat a number of ailments. These included the treatment of tetanus, rodent ulcer, general ulcers, and 
wounds [325] . Additionally, in 1924, a salve composed of burnt tobacco leaves and lanolin was 
pronounced to have antiseptic effects on superficial ulcers and wounds [326]. Before its introduction to 
Europe, tobacco was also utilized by the Native American populations. Fernando Ocaranza summarised 
the medicinal uses of tobacco by Native Americans in Mexico as being for the relief of pain and the 
treatment of wounds and burns [326].  
Nicotine is a toxic chemical and lethal to humans. Although the literature generally accepts that a dose 
of 30-60mg is lethal, growing evidence supports 0.5 g as being a lethal dose[327].  
Considering the findings discussed here, it would be expected that due to the toxicity of nicotine and 
other chemicals in CBs and CB smoke, that they would present an incredibly hostile environment for 
bacteria to survive in. However, our initial microbiological pilot study into the presence of bacteria on 
CBs has shown that bacteria can possibly survive on CBs, albeit for an unknown period. As a result, 
further investigations into this area should be undertaken. 
7.6.4. Safe Handling of CBs  
Prior to discussing the safe handling of CBs, it is known that high levels of dust exposure can lead to 
respiratory problems, most notably in a brick factory environment where workers are likely to encounter 
dust particles, which is seen in a large variety of studies conducted across the world. One such study 
found that exposure to high levels of dust in two brick manufacturing plants in Croatia had led to an 
increased prevalence of chronic respiratory symptoms in exposed workers when compared to the 
control group [328]. Similar research was conducted in Cape Town, South Africa, where 268 brick 
workers from five brickworks were studied. It was found that both smoking and exposure to dust had 
an impact on the prevalence of chronic respiratory disease. However, smoking generally had a weaker 
effect when compared to dust exposure, and only affected early onset symptoms [329]. Similarly, brick 
workers in Nepal and Pakistan were found to suffer significant volumes of respiratory problems that 
were predominately linked to particulate matter exposure [330, 331]. Additionally, studies have linked 
silica exposure in the brick manufacturing industry to silicosis, tuberculosis, and lung cancer. Fishwick 
et al. (2015) states that current workplace exposure contributes to the risk of developing silicosis [332]. 
As it is difficult to control the levels of dust generated within brick manufacturing plants and their 

=== PDF PAGE 190 ===
 
172 
 
 
environments, it is suggested that employees should always wear protective masks when exposed to 
dust, particularly where no effort has been made to reduce the volume of dust generated. 
When handling dry CBs, it is essential to wear appropriate masks. This is to protect against any possible 
inhalation of VOC or exposure to any pathogens tha t may be present in the sample, and is especially 
important in a brick factory due to the dust exposure alone. Hand protection is required to ensure that 
pathogens do not enter cuts by chemicals getting absorbed through the skin. In addition to this, it is very 
important to wear gloves when handling CBs that are moist, as heavy metals, such as lead, cadmium, 
and arsenic, can leach off the butts. 
It is recommended that CBs be sterilised immediately upon or prior to delivery to the manufacturing 
site. Several  appropriate methods have been discussed which have been deemed to be suitable for 
industrial scale sterilisation of used CBs. For small volumes of CBs, the use of mothballs or toilet cakes 
that contain Naphthalene can be cost efficient, as, when placed in the bag containing CBs, the 
Naphthalene will fumigate all or some of the bacteria that may be present (further investigation is 
recommended in this area). This solution however is considered viable for small volumes and should 
be used in the interim until a larger scale sterilisation method can be implemented 
Previous investigations into the leachates from manufactured CB bricks show that, after firing, only 
trace amounts of metal leachates were found in bricks with 2.5, 5.0, 7.5, and 10.0% content of CBs [1]. 
These concentrations were lower than the regulatory limits set by USEPA (1996) and EPAV (2005) 
[243, 244].  
Odor from the incorporation of CBs into brick production should also be addressed. The issue stems 
from VOCs being emitted from CBs and can either be addressed prior to entering the manufacturing 
site or during manufacturing of CB bricks. It should be noted that odor  disappears after firing of the 
bricks occurs. 
As always, it is essential that local regulations and standards regarding the handling of municipal waste 
be adhered to. 
7.7. Implementation Guide 
The following steps may be followed for the implementation of recycling CBs in fired-clay bricks. 
i. For CB collection, aim to  develop a close relationship with CB collection companies to 
facilitate the delivery of CBs to manufacturing sites. These CBs are normally collected from 
modern bins or receptacles.  
ii. Once the CBs are collected, a sterilisation method (Section 7. 6) should be used to clean the 
CBs from bacteria. The odor may be purged from CBs in this stage (Section 7.5). If mothballs 
(containing naphthalene) are used, they should be put into the bags containing CBs to inactivate 

=== PDF PAGE 191 ===
 
173 
 
 
any bacteria that may be present. This can be done by the collector or by workers on-site. Care 
should be taken to not breathe in fumes when the bags are opened. The CBs will then be stored 
on-site. 
iii. When ready, CBs can be incorporated into the brick clay mix through a method suggested in 
Section 7.5 for incorporation into Automatic Processes, and 7.5.2 for incorporation into Manual 
Processes. Once the CBs have been incorporated, the remaining steps that are common within 
the brick manufacturing process can be followed.  
iv. Always ensure that relevant OH&S standards are followed, the correct PPE is worn, and that 
the fumes are not breathed. Refer to Sections 7.5.1.4 and 7.5.2.3 for more detail on the safe 
handling of CBs. 
7.8. Conclusion 
This study has presented and discussed some of the results of an ongo ing study on recycling CBs in 
fired-clay bricks. The results of a CB energy contribution study, bacteriological investigation of CBs, 
and the properties of bricks incorporating CBs have been discussed. Also, the implementation procedure 
for recycling CBs in bricks and the pre-processing issues of CBs are analysed and discussed. The main 
findings are summarised and discussed below. 
Energy savings: The calorific value of CBs was investigated. It was found that the average gross 
calorific value of CBs with rem nant tobacco was 16.5 MJ  kg-1. This is a high value compared to the 
calorific value of black coal, which is 20 MJ kg -1. If 2.5% of all the bricks produced annually around 
the world include 1% CB content, the energy consumption of the process can be reduced by over 20 
billion MJ. This approximately equates to the power used by one million homes every year in the State 
of Victoria, Australia. Therefore adding 1% CBs (about 20 kg/m3) to the brick clay results in significant 
energy savings, solves the CB pollution problem globally, and tangibly reduces the environmental 
impact of the brick manufacturing industry, while, concurrently providing financial savings to 
manufacturers through the reduction in energy for firing bricks. Fired-clay bricks are excellent for  
recycling waste materials. There is a need for a specially dedicated brick factory in every state/country 
for recycling cigarette butts and other similar waste materials. Due to the high calorific value of CBs, 
it is discouraged large amounts of CBs to be  incorporated in clay bricks. During the firing phase, lime 
lumps may occur at elevated temperatures within the brick structure due to the increased organic matter 
found in CBs. 
Bacteriological investigations: A serious concern in the recycling of used CBs is that they may be 
contaminated with bacteria or viruses from contact with people or the environment. As a result, this 
study undertook two pilot investigations. The presence of ten common bacteria was investigated on 
unused, fresh, and old CBs. In the first investigation, used CBs that were one week old, and used CBs 

=== PDF PAGE 192 ===
 
174 
 
 
that were collected on the day of the testing day were compared with an unused control sample. A small 
colony of Coagulase +ve Staphylococcus spp was detected on the same-day sample.  For the second 
investigation, used CBs that were collected a day before the testing, used old CBs, and used CBs dried 
at 105 °C for 6 hr were tested and compared with the control sample. The most significant finding from 
this investigation was that Listeria spp was detected in the used old CB sample and that all used and 
unused CB samples except the dried sample had significant counts of  Bacillus spp. It was also found 
that the effect of the contact of naphthalene balls with fresh used CBs on the reduction of Bacillus spp, 
bacteria, was very significant. This study suggests that naphthalene-containing mothballs be used to 
fumigate bags used to collect and store CBs. Further study is recommended in this area. To address the 
potential for the presence of pathogens i n CBs, several suitable sterilisation methods have been 
reviewed and discussed. 
Sterilisation methods: Following the two bacteriological investigations, several industrial sterilisation 
measures have been studied, including ozone, vaporized hydrogen peroxi de, moist heat sterilisation, 
and dry heat sterilisation, Ultraviolet Light Radiation (UV), and Naphthalene. These methods could be 
employed to sterilise large volumes of waste materials. Hydrogen peroxide may be an effective and 
practical sterilising meth od for any viruses, bacteria, or fungi that may be present on CBs for large 
scale-cleaning plants. 
Properties of bricks incorporating CBs: The compressive strength of 1% CB content bricks with 15.5% 
and 17.5% moisture content averaged 27.5 MPa and 25.8 MPa, respectively, down from the 43.2 MPa 
exhibited by the 15% moisture content control bricks. The standard compressive strength for bricks for 
low-medium rise residential structures in Australia is between 10 and 20 MPa. The decrease in 
compressive strength as a result of the increase in CB content, establishes a limitation regarding the 
content of CB that can be incorporated in fired clay bricks. The dry density of the bricks decreased from 
2134 kg m -3 to 1964 kg m -3 (8% reduction). This results in bricks w ith lower thermal conductivity, 
which are lighter and therefore reduce the ‘dead- weight’ of potential structures. Lower thermal 
conductivity can have a significant influence on the energy consumption of a home, as it reduces the 
need to use air-conditioning systems to adjust the temperature inside the house. The water absorption 
and initial rate of absorption of the brick increased as expected due to the reduction in density of the 
samples. However, the changes were minor and well within acceptable limits. These results confirm the 
previous results published by Mohajerani et al. (2016). 
Implementation procedures: To incorporate the CBs into the automatic brick manufacturing process, 
three processing methods have been suggested. Shredding CBs is expected to improve uniformity in 
the particle size and distribution of CBs within the mix, thereby leading to clay bricks with more 
uniform properties. However, shredding of the CBs must be performed in a fully enclosed environment, 

=== PDF PAGE 193 ===
 
175 
 
 
such as an enclosed industrial shredder. This is necessary as it keeps the dust and chemicals from 
spilling out into the environment. 
CB collection systems: In Australia, several companies have developed CB disposal systems. These 
companies provide receptacles for the disposal of CBs. Currently, the collected CBs are placed in 
landfill or incinerated by the collection company. There are different types of receptacles, including 
wall-mounted, post-mounted, and free standing. 
CB odor: The odor from VOC emissions in CBs can be an issue for recycling CBs in bricks or any other 
application. This has been discussed and analysed. UV light has the capacity to oxidise and shatter the 
molecules in VOC elements from CBs to form new molecules that are non -odorous or less odorous. It 
is possible to deodor ize and sterilize CBs at the same time using a UV light with an appropriate 
wavelength. This was studied, and UVC or UVV is suggested for this dual treatment. A comprehensive 
experimental study is recommended in this area. 
7.8.1. Proposal  
About 6 trillion cigarette butts are littered worldwide every year, resulting in over 1.2 million tonnes of 
toxic to highly toxic waste contaminating our cities and the environment. Considering the combined 
risks from hundreds of highly toxic chemicals and possible pathogens in cigarette butts, it is proposed 
that the littering of this waste anywhere in cities and the environment be strictly prohibited, and that 
offenders be heavily fined. This strategy should be supported by appropriate education, guidelines, and 
advertising. Our cities, parks, waterways, beaches, and oceans have been contaminated for many years, 
with millions of tonnes of unsightly and toxic cigarette butts. Effective and strong laws and guidelines 
by governments for solving this global pollution problem are overdue and need to be established 
urgently. This action should be supported by an adequate number of receptacles installed at critical 
locations in cities and public places by local governments for the collection of CBs for recycling. The 
sincere, effective, and urgent support and cooperation of smokers, governments, educators, and waste 
management industries are essential for ending the littering of cigarette butts in the environment. 
 

=== PDF PAGE 194 ===
 
176 
 
 
Chapter 8: Application of UVC light to 
minimize the odor generated from volatile 
organic compounds during the production 
of fired clay bricks incorporating cigarette 
butts 
Abstract  
The increasing trend in global cigarette consumption in many parts of the world has led to a tremendous 
volume of waste being generated in the form of used cigarette butts (CBs). Mohajerani et al. (2016) 
proposed a novel method involving the incorporation of CBs in fired clay bricks (FCBs). However, an 
implementation issue has surfaced, where strong odors are being released through the emission of 
volatile organic compounds (VOCs) during the storage of CBs. To address this problem, the objective 
of this study was to determine techniques to detect and analyze the VOCs generated from CBs, along 
with investigating methods to minimize or eradicate the odors generated during the production 
processes of FCBs incorporating CBs. Charcoal sorbent tubes were used to collect the gas components, 
and gas chromatography -mass spectrometry (GC -MS) was used to identify and quantify the VOCs 
generated. UVC light was implemented to determine its effect on odor reduction and mitigation. The 
main hazardous constituents in freshly discarded CBs were found to be benzaldehyde, benzyl alcohol, 
p-xylene, styrene, and 2,5-dimethylanisole. The results revealed that UVC radiation could diminish the 
release of the toxic components and minimize the odor generated from CBs during the storage and 
manufacturing process of FCBs incorporating CBs. Furthermore, it was found the emission of VOCs 
during the mixing, compaction, and firing processes were much higher for untreated CBs as opposed to 
the UVC light treated CBs. 
The research presented in this chapter has been submitted to the following: 
Kurmus, H., Mohajerani, A., and Suntovski, J., 2021. Application of UVC light to minimize the odor 
generated from volatile organic compounds  during the production of fired clay bricks incorporating 
cigarette butts. Clean Technologies and Environmental Policy. (under review) 
 

=== PDF PAGE 195 ===
 
177 
 
 
8.1 Introduction  
One of the most common types of littered waste globally is cigarette butts (CBs). It was estimated by 
The Tobacco Atlas, that in 2016 alone, about 5.7 trillion cigarettes were smoked worldwide [333]. This 
amounts to over 1.2 million tonnes of CB waste produced annually. This is indeed a significant amount 
of waste, and it is expected to rise as the global population increases [334].  
CBs are manufactured with filters to reduce the tar intake by smokers. The filter is made from cellulose 
acetate, which is a synthetic compound derived from acetylation of the plant substance cellulose [335], 
which can take 18 months or longer to degrade naturally [336] . CBs are a nuisance, as they are 
predominantly littered and tend to create a plethora of waste with th e potential of leaching harmful 
chemicals and heavy metals into the environment, which can be detrimental. A number of heavy metals 
found in CBs which are potentially leached into the environment include chromium, cadmium, nickel, 
arsenic, and lead [336, 337] . Therefore, it is paramount that waste materials be managed efficiently 
[338]. One such way of managing and reducing CBs in the environment is through recycling the waste 
in the production of construction materials.   
In recent years, a novel method has been proposed to incorporate CBs into the production of fired clay 
bricks (FCBs) [1, 2, 22, 23, 115, 126, 176, 339] . Based on several years of research, Mohajerani et al. 
[1] proposed the CB problem can potentially be abolished by incorporating 1% CBs into 2.5% of the 
world's total FCB production. Numerous mechanical and physical tests were conducted on the FCB 
samples incorporating various percentages of CBs, including compressive strength, density, water 
absorption, initial rate of  absorption, efflorescence, shrinkage, energy savings, thermal conductivity, 
and gas emissions and the results were promising. Moreover, a comprehensive leachate analysis of 
heavy metals and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) in FCBs incorporating CBs was conducted 
to investigate the potential leachate of heavy metals and toxic chemicals during the use and disposal of 
the material. The modified FCBs were found to be below the regulatory threshold limits and categorized 
as non-hazardous waste when compared to the EPA Victoria industrial waste guidelines.  
An implementation issue with the addition of CBs in FCBs is the high volume of odors generated from 
the mass storage of CBs in industrial settings and during the manufacturing process of FCBs 
incorporating CBs. The odor released from waste materials has received increasing attention, as the 
consensus has shifted towards an environmental-centric approach. Odors are perceived as atmospheric 
pollutants, hence why it is a source of complaint in many industrial processes. 
Concerns associated with the strong odor of CBs and the potential release of volatile organic compounds 
(VOCs) during the storage and manufacturing process of CBs in FCBs need to be addressed and 
investigated. Practical techniques need to be adopted to measure the VOCs released from CBs. 
Therefore, the aim of this research was to determine techniques to detect and analyze the emission of 

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===
 
178 
 
 
VOCs generated from CBs, along with investigating methods to minimize or eradicate the odors 
generated from  CBs. Charcoal sorbent tubes were used to collect the gas components, and gas 
chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC-MS) was used to identify and quantify the VOCs generated. 
Industrial methods and techniques adopted to eliminate CB odor on an industrial sca le were reviewed 
and analysed, including ultraviolet -C (UVC) light, ozone, and vaporized hydrogen peroxide. Several 
factors were considered when selecting the most effective and efficient equipment. High safety and 
affordability of the proposed equipment w ere other significant characteristics taken into consideration 
when selecting the preferred device. Based on an extensive review, the UVC was implemented to 
determine its effect on odor reduction and mitigation. The purpose of this study includes: 
1. Determination of VOCs in freshly smoked CBs and UVC-treated smoked CBs. 
2. Determination of VOCs during the mixing process of raw clay soil, water, oven-dried CBs, and 
UVC-treated oven-dried CBs. 
3. Determination of VOCs during the compaction process of clay bricks incorporating oven-dried 
CBs and UVC-treated oven-dried CBs. 
4. Determination of VOCs during the firing process of clay bricks incorporating oven-dried CBs 
and UVC-treated oven-dried CBs. 
8.2 Odor  
8.2.1 Quantifying Odor  
Smell is a biochemical reaction where odorous molecules attach themselves to olfactory receptors 
located in the nose. The chemicals are required to be in gaseous form in order to both enter and attach 
to the olfactory receptors. The nose also filters out the large particulate matter with nostril cavities lined 
with mucus. Once the molecules attach to the olfactory receptors, the receptors then send an electrical 
signal to the brain, which we perceive as odor [340]. Not all molecules can attach to olfactory receptors, 
which leads to some molecules being odorless; for example, methane is considered an odorless chemical 
[341]. However, it is difficult to chemically or physically measure and quantify odor, as smell is a 
human perception that varies based on the individual [342].  
8.2.2 Composition of Cigarette Odor 
The composition of cigarette odor is complex as cigarettes can emit over 4000 different chemicals [336]. 
A combination of different chemicals can enter the nose and create smells, with some chemicals having 
more pungent odor whilst others are present in higher concentrations [342] . In order to simplify the 
research, it is best to describe odor using functional groups rather than listing each individual molecule. 
Functional groups are particular combinations of atoms within molecules that have their own distinctive 
properties, irrespective of the other atoms present in a molecule [343].  

=== PDF PAGE 197 ===
 
179 
 
 
Cigarette smoke contains the following odor causing functional groups, aromatic hydrocarbons, 
saturated hydrocarbons, terpenes, aromatic hydrocarbons, cyclic hydrocarbons, alcohols, organic acids, 
esters, unsaturated hydrocarbons, N-containing compounds, ketones, and O -containing compounds 
[344]. The majority of the tobacco odor is made up of aldehydes which contributed to 50-90% of the 
odor, depending on environmental conditions [344] . The individ ual aldehyde compounds such as 
acetaldehyde (1.2~26%), butylaldehyde (2.3~18%), furfural (7.6~58), valeraldehyde (1.1~6.7%), and 
hexaldehyde (1.4~41%) contributed most to the odor. The next highest group, which is organic acids, 
only contributed 0.92-6% of the odor [344].  
Therefore, if aldehydes were to be reduced, the cigarette odor intensity would also reduce. It is to be 
noted that even if aldehydes were to reduce, the other pungent odor groups such as alcohols, organic 
acids, and ketones would also have to be monitored so that the chemical reactions don’t result in the 
increased pungent odor groups. 
However, the above aforementioned functional groups may not be present in CBs as they are too volatile 
or can be retained and are unable to be remitted. Aldehydes may become trapped but then remitted 
quickly out of CBs due to the volatile nature of aldehydes. Lower molecular weight chemicals tend to 
break through the filters faster. Some of the medium volatile chemicals that are present and can be 
emitted from CBs over a long period of time include nicotine, polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons 
(PAHs), hydrogen cyanide, and some organic gasses [345]. 
8.2.3 Diffusion and Flow  
Diffusion is the main cause of odor emission from  the CBs, where odor molecules travel from an area 
of high concertation to low concentration [346]. CBs, although not an effective adsorptive material, can 
still capture some particulate matter, and the filter may reemit some of the particles back into the 
environment as third-hand smoke, similar to how some smoke room walls reemit smoke after a given 
period of time [347, 348]. Nicotine and tar particulate matter are considered heavy and sticky, with the 
median diameter size of the particulate matter being 300 nm [346] . This could explain why nicotine 
smoke odor lingers on CBs well after combustion has ended and stays static within the environment. 
When compared to e-cigarette vapor which can vaporize in seconds, juxtaposed to that regular cigarette 
odor stays in the environment for a long time depending on the ventilation of the room. The reemitting 
rate is dependent on environmental conditions such as temperature, relative humidity, and volatility of 
the chemicals being emitted [345] . Other possible movement methods of the particles include 
gravitational settlement, electrostatic effects, thermophoresis, and ventilation [346].  
 

=== PDF PAGE 198 ===
 
180 
 
 
8.2.4 Measurement of Odor 
Measurement of odor can be achieved in two unique ways. Method one involves counting the individual 
molecules by weight using a specialised detection method such as the gas chromatography -mass-
spectrometry (GC-MS) to give specific results. The second method is evaluating the odor as a whole 
and giving it a rating based on the odor threshold and intensity of the odor. A device such as an 
olfactometer does this by diluting fresh air with odorous air until it detects the odor [349]. Method one 
considers all the various VOCs and counts each VOC compound, whilst method two is an overall 
evaluation of odor based on the odor threshold and intensity. In gene ral, in the last decade, the use of 
olfactometers has gained attention in the field of research due to lower operational costs, ease of 
operation, and accuracy in results, however, the initial setup cost is substantially higher compared to 
other methods [350].  
8.2.5 Techniques for Odor Reduction 
CBs are considered lethal wastes as they consist of heavy metals, toxic chemicals, and bacteria and 
therefore require treatment prior to being handled by humans when being used for recycling purposes 
[351-353]. Moreover, the odor emitted from CBs in the form of VOCs is a health issue when potentially 
stored in mass amounts in an industrial setting. Therefore, it is vital to find a method capable of treating 
CBs on a large scale which can potential ly be adopted in the brick- making industry. A few of the 
popular and widely employed techniques utilised to eliminate odor on an industrial scale include UVC 
light, ozone, and vaporized hydrogen peroxide. 
8.2.5.1 Ultraviolet Light-C (UVC)  
The application of UVC light is a very popular technique utilised worldwide in the food industry and 
for disinfection and sanitisation purposes due to its low environmental impacts and high safety 
standards as it emits radiation in the range of 200 to 280 nm (Fig. 8.1) [354] . Initially, mercury was 
employed in the production of UV lamps as the principal source of light emission, however, the import 
or export of products containing mercury was globally prohibited due to the environmental health risks 
the heavy metal posed [354] . An alternative to the mercury -based UV lamps was the use of light 
emitting diode (LED) UV lamps which became adopted extensively due to it being affordable and eco-
friendly [354]. 

=== PDF PAGE 199 ===
 
181 
 
 
 
 
Fig. 8.1. Spectrum of light and UVC light radiation range. 
The technique of sanitising or disinfecting a surface or product through the use of UVC light requires 
an exposure period ranging from 10 – 30 min, however, the exposure time depends on the characteristics 
of the material [355]. It is recommended plastic and fiber -based materials, similarly to CBs require an 
exposure period of roughly 5 min to UVC light to treat the material [356].  
8.2.5.2 Ozone 
Ozone is well-known for its oxidation characteristics and is produced through ozone generator devices 
which break down oxygen molecules into two single oxygen atoms [357] . The two single atoms then 
bind with molecules existing in the air to produce ozone molecules [358] . Car dealer services and the 
hospitality industry prominently utilise ozone gas to eradicate the unpleasant smells of smoke and paint. 
Moreover, ozone is applied for disinfection purposes and for taste and odor removal in wastewater 
[359]. Therefore employing ozone on waste materials to remove odor  is potentially a valid method, 
especially to eradicate or mitigate the harmful smells emitted from CBs. 
8.2.5.3 Vaporised Hydrogen Peroxide 
Hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) has been implemented in sanitation since its discovery by the French chemist 
Louis-Jacques Thénard in the early 1800s [360]. There has been a plethora of reiteration techniques and 
methodologies over the years to refine the use of hydrogen peroxide as a disinfectant agent. H 2O2 has 
become an integral component in surface disinfectant s in the food and pharmacology industries. A 
technique to improve the delivery of H 2O2 as a sterilising agent was first developed by Francis Moore 
and Leon Perkinson in 1977, which was based on a cold sterilization process using the vaporized 
hydrogen peroxide sterilisation method [361].   


=== PDF PAGE 200 ===
 
182 
 
 
In its vapor form, the chemical is transparent, water -soluble, and transparent [360]. It is an excellent 
disinfectant due to its acidic nature, and when found in high concentrations, the substance acts as an 
oxidizing agent and is therefore effective at nullifying bacteria, viruses, VOCs, and odor. Based on 
research, it was found the vapor form of hydrogen peroxide is much more effective and adequate as 
opposed to the aqueous form. This is due to the gaseous form consisting of greater kinetic energy and 
therefore proceeding to a faster penetration rate [360].  
Implementation of vaporized hydrogen peroxide can be further divided into atmospheric pressure or 
vacuum conditions. An atmospheric pressure condition is generally implemented for the disinfection of 
large-area surfaces such as rooms and isolators [362]. The equipment to conduct such work ranges from 
handheld devices to industrial instruments, which can be integrated into ventilation systems. These 
instruments tend to create a vapor or a fog of a diluted solution of hydrogen peroxide  with varying 
concentrations (eg. 25% and 35%), which can be used to treat and disinfect objects or an open area. 
In this section, three industrial methods and techniques were reviewed and analysed to determine the 
most effective and efficient process to apply to eliminate CB odor/smell on an industrial scale, including 
UVC light, ozone, and vaporized hydrogen peroxide. Several factors were considered when selecting 
the most practical equipment. High safety and affordability of the proposed equipment were other 
significant characteristics taken into consideration when selecting the preferred device. Based on an 
extensive review, UVC light was selected to be implemented in this study as UVC apparatuses are 
simple, efficient, and effective equipment, with minimal training required to operate. The use of UVC 
light to treat CBs and the smell emitted from littered CBs will require a sanitization device/chamber 
that is commercially available. Therefore, a Lumin Multipurpose Sanitizer was purchased for the 
purpose of this study.  
8.3 Materials and methods 
8.3.1 Materials and reagents 
This study utilised CBs of various brand sizes supplied by Buttout Australia Pty Ltd. The raw clay soil 
was obtained by PGH Bricks and Pavers (VIC, Australia). For the preparation of standard solutions, 
benzene-d6, toluene-d8, and the 48-component indoor air standard were purchased from Sigma-Aldrich 
Pty. Ltd. (NSW, Australia). For the sampling of VOCs, an AirCheck50 00 pump and charcoal sorbent 
tubes were purchased (Active Environmental Solutions, VIC, Australia). A Lumin Multipurpose 
Sanitizer (VIC, Australia) was used to treat the samples with UVC light. The fundamental properties 
and functions of the materials and equipment utilised for this study can be seen in Table 8.1.

8.3.2 Production process 
The production process can be divided into the exposure of CBs to UVC rays, oven drying of raw 
materials at 105 °C for 24 h, mixing of raw materials with water for 25 min, compaction of samples at 
240 kPa, and firing of brick samples for 3 h at 1050 °C. Detailed information regarding the production 
process of FCBs incorporating CBs can be found in Kurmus and Mohajerani [176]. The exposure of 
CB samples to UVC-rays in the initial step of the process was carried out using a 5 min exposition time. 
The UVC lamp emitted rays at lengths of 254 nm. Changes in the concentration of VOCs were 
determined in the CB samples before and after the exposition to UVC light rays. The processing 
technology of unexposed CB FCB samples is the same as that of exposed CB FCB samples, except for 
the use of the Lumin sanitiser in the initial step. The necessary details of the preparation process, such 
as the control parameters, are shown in Fig. 8.2. The samples collected at each stage comprised of both 

=== PDF PAGE 202 ===
 
184 
 
 
UVC treated/untreated CB samples to compare and analyse the effect of UVC light radiation on 
reducing the emission of VOCs. 
 
 
Fig. 8.2. Laboratory process flow chart of FCBs incorporating treated/untreated CBs and sample collection 
methods. 
8.3.3 VOC collection process  
8.3.3.1 Headspace Vials 
Freshly smoked CB and UVC-treated smoked CB samples were placed in 20 mL vials and analysed 
with headspace analysis to determine the mass emitted from the butts into the headspace air of the vial. 
The method followed is a modified version of Method 5021A [363]. 
8.3.3.2 Charcoal Sorbent Tubes 
Charcoal sorbent tubes were used for the solvent extraction method [364] . The sorbent tube cartridges 
are glass tubes with both ends glass sealed and contain two sections of 20/40 mesh of foam wool to 
separate the adsorbing section from the back -up section containing 400 mg and 200 mg of charcoal. 
Samples were collected through active sampling at the large end of the tube. A personal sample pump 
was used to pump air samples at a flow rate of 500 mL min-1. During the mixing process of the raw 
materials and water, the sampler was positioned above the Hobart mechanical mixer for effective 


=== PDF PAGE 203 ===
 
185 
 
 
sampling. Throughout the compaction process, the sampler was located at the breathing zone of the 
person (1.5 m above the ground), and during the firing process, the sampler was located right above the 
opening valve of the electric furnace. 
After sampling, the charcoal tubes were detached from the pump, and the two open sides were firmly 
sealed with PTFE caps to prevent unwanted desorption. The samples were stored in a freezer at -5 °C 
until the analysis, to avoid a possibility of deterioration and contamination. Weekly field blanks were 
tested by placing a charcoal tube with broken ends to the sampler but without pa ssing any air sample 
through, to identify any possible contamination. The blank tube was then collected and analysed.  
In preparation for the analysis, each charcoal tube was scored with a quartz blade in the adsorbing 
section of charcoal and broken open. The glass wool was removed and discarded. The charcoal in the 
adsorbing section was transferred to a headspace screw top 20 mL clear vial. The separating foam was 
removed and discarded, and the backup section was transferred to another capped vial. These t wo 
sections were analysed separately.  
To desorb the samples, 1 mL of desorbing solution was pipetted into each sample vial. The desorbing 
solution consisted of 25 µL of internal standard solution in carbon disulphide. The sample vials were 
capped with PTFE/Sil magnetic headspace caps as soon as the solution was added. Immediately after 
the solvent process, 250  µL of sample extract was injected into a split/splitless inlet port. 
Chromatographic conditions are detailed in the following section. 
8.3.3.3 Chromatographic conditions    
In both methods separation and detection were performed in an Agilent 7000 Series GC-MS triple quad 
system using two Agilent DB -5MS columns 5 m long and 0.25 mm in diameter. Analyses was 
performed by injecting 250 µL of extract in a split/splitless port at 250 °C with a split of 7.5 mL min-1. 
The oven temperature of the GC was initially held at 40 °C for 5 min then raised to 260 °C at a rate of 
20 °C min-1 and held at that temperature for 8 min. 
8.3.3.4 Calibration Standards  
Quantitative analysis was only conducted on the samples collected from the sorbent charcoal tubes. 
Calibration for the charcoal sorbent tubes was done by enriching the sorbent tubes with the internal and 
calibration standards. Concentrations  between 3.125 to 50 µg ml -1 of standard solutions of the forty -
eight target chemicals and internal standards benzene-d 6 and toluene-d8 were injected into the sorbent 
tubes. The syringe was placed in the sorbent for 20 s and immediately placed in the headspace vials and 
analysed as described above. 

=== PDF PAGE 204 ===
 
186 
 
 
8.4 Results and discussion  
8.4.1 Components in headspace vials 
The process of removing VOCs through UVC radiation is called photodegradation which involves two 
pathways [365]. The first pathway being photolysis whereby the VOC molecules are broken down by 
UVC light photons and one or more covalent bond is cleaved resulting from the absorption of light. The 
second pathway is photooxidation, whereby the UVC irradiation excites the VOC molecules resulting 
in an oxidation reaction. Fig. 8.3 shows the composition of the VOCs generated from UVC light treated 
and untreated CBs. The peak heights of different substances are different at various retention times. The 
higher peak value represents the high content of the component. Fig. 8.3 shows that the peak heights of 
freshly smoked CBs are generally higher than those of UVC light treated CB samples. Especially in 
benzaldehyde, benzyl alcohol, p-xylene, styrene and 2,5 -Dimethylanisole, the peak value of untreated 
CBs was significantly higher than that of treated CBs. T his finding indicates that UVC light can 
effectively reduce the release of volatile gases in CBs. Similarly, Włodarczyk -Makuła [366] found the 
exposure of UVC-rays on the removal of polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) in wastewater to 
be effective. A decrease of 65% in PAHs was achieved when the wastewater was exposed to radiation, 
the exposure furthermore resulted in the disinfection of the wastewater samples.  
 
Fig. 8.3. Mass spectrogram of VOCs in UVC light treated and untreated CB samples.

In the following part only thirty of the forty-eight compounds were examined and quantified. Table 8.3 
shows the total emissions of VOCs during the mixing, compaction and firing process of FCBs 
incorporating 2% CBs by weight. The emissions of trichloroethylene, 2,2,4-trimethylpentane and nonanal 
were not detectable. The various volatile compounds are grouped into aromatics, alkanes, halides, 
aldehydes, ketones, terpenes, and chlorinated hydrocarbons. The alkane compounds including hexane, 
nonane, heptane, octane, undecane, dodecane, tridecane, tetradecane and pentadecane were found in 
concentrations ranging from 2.78 to 61.12 µg ml -1 for all process’s during the production of FCBs 
incorporating CBs. While the aromatics were found in concentrations ranging from 2.21 to 14.65 µg 
ml-1. Compounds including benzene, toluene, xylenes, ethylbenzene and styrene were similarly found 
in a study involving the analysis of VOCs generated from the smoke of cigarettes [367]. The halide 
chloroform remained constant in concentration throughout each process for both UVC light treated and 
untreated CBs, although it is a known constituent of tobacco smoke. 
The emission of VOCs during the various processes were much higher for untreated CBs as opposed to 
the UVC treated CBs. For ethylbenzene a 50% reduction was observed during the firing process when 
UVC treated CBs were used, similarly a 57% reduction in the emission of styrene was observed with 
the use of UVC treated CBs. This displays the effectiveness of th e UVC light radiation on breaking 
down the covalent bonds of the organic compounds in the CB samples and therefore diminishing the 
odor generated during the storage and production process of FCBs incorporating CBs. 
In general, the emission of VOCs during the firing process was greater than the mixing and compaction 
processes. This is because of the high temperatures (1050 °C) the FCB samples incorporating CBs are 
subjected during the firing process to which results in the evaporation of the organic compounds and , 
therefore its release through the valve located on the top of the furnace to the external environment. The 
mixing process of the raw materials (clay, CBs, and water) also resulted in a high quantity VOCs being 
generated due to the release of particles from the fast-mixing process in the mechanical mixer. For the 
aromatics like 1,2,4 -trimethylbenzene, a concentration of 3.64 µg ml -1 was released during the firing 
process for UVC light treated CBs as opposed to 9.76 µg ml-1 during the mixing process.

incorporating CBs.

8.5 Conclusion  
This research mainly aimed to determine the chemical composition of cigarette butt (CB) emissions 
and incorporate Ultraviolet-C (UVC) light radiation to retard the release of hazardous gases and odor 
to solve a reoccurring issue involving the release of toxic compounds during the storage and production 
of fired clay bricks (FCBs) incorporating CBs for the successful implementation on an industrial scale. 
It was found by using charcoal sorbent tubes, all kinds of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) can be 

=== PDF PAGE 208 ===
 
190 
 
 
effectively collected and easily evaluated with chemical test analysis. The gas chromatography -mass 
spectrometry (GC-MS) test reveals that the hazardous organic constituents in CB emissions mainly 
include benzaldehyde, benzyl alcohol, p-xylene, styrene, and 2,5-Dimethylanisole. The VOC emissions 
in UVC treated CB samples are significantly less than that of untreated CB samples, therefore showing 
UVC radiation can effectively reduce the generation of hazardous gases. The emission of VOCs during 
the various processes was much higher for untreated CBs as opposed to the UVC treated CBs, 
displaying the effectiveness of the UVC light radiation in breaking down the covalent bonds within the 
complex VOCs. For ethylbenzene a 50% reduction in emissions was observed during the firing process 
when UVC treated CBs were used, similarly, a 57% reduction in the emission of styrene was observed 
with the use of UVC treated CBs. In general, the emission of VOCs during the firing process was greater 
than the mixing and compaction processes. This is due to the high temperatures the samples are 
subjected to, resulting in the evaporation and release of VOCs. However, it is recommended further 
research is conducted utilizing distinct methods to capture the VOC emissions during the various 
processes of manufacturing FCBs incorporating various percentages of CBs. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

=== PDF PAGE 209 ===
 
191 
 
 
Chapter 9: Conclusion and 
Recommendations  
9.1 Conclusions 
This study investigated recycling 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% cigarette butts (CBs) by weight (wt) in fired 
clay bricks (FCBs) and compared them with those of controlled FCBs. In alignment with the research 
objectives, a series of systematic investigations and detailed studies were conducted to develop 
environmentally and economically  sustainable and practical techniques to recycle CB waste in the 
production of FCBs on an industrial scale. All objectives relative to the study have been achieved, and 
a summary of each chapter has been concluded as follows. 
In Chapter 2, a comprehensive review was conducted on  various studies involving the  toxicity and 
valorization options of CB waste and the feasibility and sustainability of the recycling methods adopted. 
Eleven CB valorization proposals and practices were studied, including the incorporation of CBs in 
FCBs and asphalt concrete. Based on the extensive research, it was determined a potential solution to 
the CB waste conundrum could be through the recycling of CBs in the production of FCBs. 
In Chapter 3 , numerous physical and mechanical tests were conducted , including water absorption, 
compressive strength, efflorescence, initial rate of absorption (IRA), density , and  shrinkage. It was 
found compressive strength decreased from 48.6 MPa to 30.8 MPa with the addition of 1% CBs to 
FCBs, similarly, dry density decreased from 2114 kg m-3 to 1983 kg m-3 with the addition of 1% CBs.  
Furthermore, the increase in CB content resulted in an increase in porosity, consequently resulting in 
an increase in water absorption. The advantages of lightweight construction material s include cheaper 
production, lower overall life cycle energy usage, and design flexibility. The results found in this study 
complete and confirm the previous findings on recycling CBs in fired clay bricks at RMIT University 
and the published proposal that all CBs can be sustainably recycled in FCBs by incorporating 1% CBs 
in 2.5% of the world’s brick production. 
Chapter 4 presented the energy savings, thermal conductivity, porosity, micro - and macro-structural 
analysis results of FCBs incorporating CBs. Based on the novel method developed to monitor energy 
consumption, it was found for bricks incorporating 1% CBs by wt., a minimum energy saving of 8% is 
achievable. This means that an estimated $53.15 million in terms of electricity usage could be saved in 
Australia every year, thereby leading to economic construction. The porosity values ranged from 3.96% 
to 20.57% for bricks incorporating 0% to 2% CBs by wt. The loss of organic content in the CBs during 

=== PDF PAGE 210 ===
 
192 
 
 
the firing process resulted in the formation of macropores within the brick structures. The 3D models 
of the bricks incorporating CBs demonstrated a homogenous distribution of pores within the brick 
structures. A reduction of up to 15.12% in thermal conductivity was achieved with the addition of 1% 
CBs by wt. in clay bricks compared to traditional bricks. That is a decrease from 0.463 W m -1 K-1  to 
0.393 W m -1 K-1 in thermal conductivity due to the formation of pores from the burnt CBs, thereby 
improving the thermal performance. Consequently, this will decrease the energy required for cooling 
and heating in households.  
Chapter 5 clarified issues regarding the possible leachate of heavy metals from (1) the use and disposal 
of bricks incorporating CBs (2) used, unused, and shredded used CBs and (3) paraffin wax and bitumen 
encapsulated CBs. It was found CBs are more likely to leach heavy metals in areas with highly acidic 
rain compared to the natural range of precipitation.  In addition, more than 50% of all the tested heavy 
metals were immobilized after the firing process of FCBs incorporating CBs . Hence the addition of 
CBs in FCBs may be a feasible option to hinder the heavy metals present in CBs. The findings suggest 
that the variance in pH influences the behavior of the metal concentration leached from bitumen and 
wax encapsulated CBs for both broken and unbroken samples. The findings show that the leaching 
behavior of CBs is considerably lower after being encapsulated with wax and bitumen. This is the result 
of sealing the CBs with viscous and insoluble materials, which prevents the release of metals o r the 
intrusion of any liquid. The leachate results for FCBs incorporating 0%, 0.5%, 1%, 1.5%, and 2% CBs 
and wax and bitumen encapsulated CBs were found to be insignificant compared to the limits set by 
the EPA Victoria solid industrial waste guidelines and, therefore, can be categorized as non-hazardous 
waste.  
Chapter 6 presented the concentration levels of 16 EPA polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) 
extracted from FCB samples, incorporating various percentages of CBs. The results showed that lower-
molecular-weight PAHs (light PAHs), including naphthalene, fluorene, anthracene, pyrene, 
fluoranthene, and chrysene, with higher volatility and lower lipophilicity, were released at very low 
concentrations compared to the higher-molecular-weight PAHs (heavy PAHs) from FCB–CB samples. 
The concentrations of the mean ΣPAH for all samples were well below the EPA solid waste hazard 
categorization threshold limit for industrial waste. The results from the carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, and 
sulfur (CHNS) and thermogravimetric (TG) analysis confirm an almost 100% combustion process 
during the firing process of FCBs. A content of less than 0.3% suggests that all carbon within the FCB–
CB mixture relatively disappeared during the firing process up to 1050 °C. It can be concluded that the 
results presented in this study clarify the issues raised regarding the possible release of toxic PAHs into 
the environment during the use and disposal of FCBs incorporating CBs or the potential storage of 
carbon in the FCB–CB mixture.  

=== PDF PAGE 211 ===
 
193 
 
 
Chapter 7  presented the results of a bacteriological investigation of CBs and the implementation 
procedure for recycling CBs in FCBs, and the pre -processing issues of CBs. The presence of ten 
common bacteria was investigated on unused, fresh, and old CBs. The most significant finding from 
the investigation was that  Listeria spp was detected in the used old CB sample and that all used and 
unused CB samples except the dried sample had significant counts of  Bacillus spp. It was also found 
that the effect of the contact of naphthalene balls with fresh used CBs on the reduction of Bacillus spp, 
bacteria, was very significant. It was suggested  shredding of the CBs must be performed in a fully 
enclosed environment, such a s an enclosed industrial shredder. This is necessary as it keeps the dust 
and chemicals from spilling out into the environment. The odor from volatile organic compound (VOC) 
emissions in CBs can be an issue for recycling CBs in bricks or any other applicat ion. Therefore it is 
suggested UV light with an appropriate wavelength is implemented to deodorize and sterilize the CBs. 
Chapter 8  aimed to determine the chemical composition of CB emissions and incorporate UVC 
radiation to retard the release of hazardous gases and odor to solve a reoccurring issue involving the 
release of toxic compounds during the storage and production of FCBs incorporating CBs for the 
successful implementation on an industrial scale. It was found UVC radiation can effectively reduce 
the generation of hazardous gases. The pollutant gas production in UV C light treated CB samples is 
significantly less than that of untreated CB samples. The emission of VOCs during the various processes 
were much higher for untreated CBs as opposed to the UV C treated CBs. For ethylbenzene a 50% 
reduction in emissions was observed during the mixing process when UVC treated CBs were used, 
similarly a 63% reduction in the emission of styrene was observed with the use of UVC treated CBs. In 
general, the emission of VOCs during the firing process was greater than the mixing and compaction 
processes. 
9.2 Recommendations  
The future works recommended are listed as below: 
i. A life -cycle assessment (LCA) is recommended to analyse the environmental impacts of  
incorporating CBs in the production of FCBs.  
ii. It is recommended to investigate the leaching performance of toxic metals at the various liquid 
to solid ratios and for compacted granular material, as described in EPA Methods 1314 and 
1315 to consider the leaching of bricks subjected to various disposal or use scenarios.  
iii. Further research regarding the emission of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) during the 
storage of CBs and during the mixing, oven-drying, and firing process of producing FCBs 
incorporating various percentages of CBs should be conducted.  
iv. Other deodorization and sterilization methods for CBs should be  examined and investigated 
and compared with the current UVC light results to determine the ultimate treatment technique.
